NBCEPHT Answer Keys and Explanations

Numbered to match the practice tests in your printed study guide.

Practice Test 1 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is C (Shortened rectus femoris crossing both the hip and the knee on the tested side): In the Ely test, knee flexion pulls on the rectus femoris, the only quadriceps head that crosses the hip, so shortening lifts the hip into flexion and tilts the pelvis. The vasti cross only the knee and cannot affect the hip. The iliopsoas is assessed with the Thomas test. The hamstrings lie on the posterior thigh and would limit knee extension, not flexion.
  2. The correct answer is B (The quadratus lumborum, with assistance from the lateral abdominal obliques): The side plank resists frontal plane sag of the pelvis, which mainly challenges the quadratus lumborum and the lateral abdominal obliques on the supported side. The rectus abdominis acts in the sagittal plane and contributes little. The erector spinae are mainly extensors. The hip flexors are not the main limiters of lateral sag.
  3. The correct answer is C (Remove the tape immediately and recheck the toes): Tingling with dusky, cool toes signals circulatory compromise or nerve compression from the tape, and the tape should come off at once and circulation and sensation be reassessed. Waiting under elevation, loosening one strip or icing over the tape all leave the constriction in place. Capillary refill and sensation should be rechecked after any ankle taping job.
  4. The correct answer is C (Parkinsonian gait, with rigidity and reduced arm swing on the right): Resting tremor, short steps that speed up, loss of arm swing and turning in one piece are the classic parkinsonian picture. A hemiplegic pattern holds the arm flexed and swings the stiff leg outward, which this man does not show. Ataxic gait is wide and irregular rather than short and hurried, and frontal magnetic gait has a wide base and sticking feet without a resting tremor.
  5. The correct answer is C (A timed MED test performed on each individual patient before treatment): Skin type predicts a trend, but individual sensitivity varies with pigmentation, medications and prior sun exposure. A timed MED test measures the patient's own threshold, and every later dose is calculated from it. Chart adjustments, averages from other patients and body size estimates all substitute a guess for a measurement. The MED test is the safest way to start.
  6. The correct answer is D (Progressive balance training on unstable surfaces with peroneal strengthening and sport specific agility drills): Recurrent sprains with a positive drawer and poor eyes-closed balance reflect mechanical laxity plus lost proprioception and weak dynamic stabilizers. Balance work on unstable surfaces, peroneal strengthening and sport-specific agility drills retrain protective reflexes and cutting control. Strengthening on firm ground with two-leg balance omits the sensorimotor challenge this athlete lacks. Early surgery is reserved for failed conservative care, and taped cutting drills do not correct the deficit.
  7. The correct answer is C (Electrodes on intact skin bordering the dressing, within the same dermatomes as the incision.): Conventional TENS works best when pads sit on intact skin in the same dermatomes as the pain, so A-beta input reaches the segments carrying the incisional pain. Bordering the dressing meets that goal without disturbing the wound. Pads over gauze lose conduction and break the sterile field, and distant arm placement suits low rate acupuncture like TENS instead. Bordering pads set at the wrong segmental level miss the spinal segments that carry the pain.
  8. The correct answer is C (The hand muscle relies mainly on rate coding, while the quadriceps still uses recruitment): Small muscles such as the hand muscles finish recruiting motor units at roughly half of maximal force, so higher forces come mainly from faster firing, or rate coding. Large muscles like the quadriceps continue recruiting new units to much higher force levels. Rate coding is not limited to low loads, the muscles are not reversed, and force certainly rises above 60 percent.
  9. The correct answer is D (Abnormal, a 9-beat drop reflecting blunted parasympathetic reactivation and higher mortality risk): Heart rate recovery is the fall from peak to one minute into recovery, and a drop of 12 beats or less is an abnormal response linked to reduced vagal reactivation and higher mortality. His 9-beat drop falls in that range. Returning fully to resting within a minute is not expected in anyone. Recovery at one minute is the standard point of assessment.
  10. The correct answer is A (Heat raises capillary permeability and blood flow, increasing swelling in fresh injury): Heat dilates vessels and increases capillary permeability, so it feeds fluid into a fresh, already swollen injury. Faster nerve conduction is a real heat effect but it is not why heat is withheld here. Slowed conduction and arteriolar constriction are effects of cold, so they describe the wrong modality.
  11. The correct answer is D (Bridge with both heels resting on a Swiss ball, holding the pelvis level): Placing the heels on a Swiss ball keeps the same hip and trunk pattern but makes the base unstable, so the stabilizers work harder to hold the pelvis level. A single-leg bridge is a real progression but changes the pattern and increases load rather than instability. The banded bridge adds resisted abduction, and longer holds raise endurance demand without changing surface stability.
  12. The correct answer is B (Bracing, scoliosis-specific exercise and scheduled radiographic monitoring by orthopedics): A skeletally immature adolescent with a 25 to 40 degree curve is the classic indication for bracing, usually paired with scoliosis-specific exercise and close radiographic follow-up. Observation fits curves under about 25 degrees, and fusion is considered when curves approach 45 to 50 degrees. Waiting for pain is inappropriate because idiopathic curves are often painless.
  13. The correct answer is C (Lower the pulse rate to 35 to 50 pps and lengthen the off time.): Higher pulse rates and short rest periods fatigue stimulated muscle quickly. Lowering the rate to 35 to 50 pps still produces a tetanic contraction, and a longer off time allows recovery between contractions, so torque is better maintained. Raising the rate or shortening rest increases fatigue. Pulse duration and ramp do not address the cause, and continuous stimulation removes recovery entirely.
  14. The correct answer is D (Knee fully extended, heel flat on the floor, ankle dorsiflexed): The gastrocnemius crosses both the knee and the ankle, so it is stretched with the knee extended and the ankle dorsiflexed. Bending the knee slackens the gastrocnemius and shifts the stretch to the soleus, which crosses only the ankle. Plantarflexing the ankle shortens the calf. A neutral ankle with an outward foot does not dorsiflex the ankle, so it does not lengthen the muscle.
  15. The correct answer is B (Stop weight bearing activity and arrange urgent imaging for suspected femoral neck stress fracture): Groin pain with night pain, pain on hopping, and a positive fulcrum test in a runner suggests a femoral neck stress fracture, which can displace and cause avascular necrosis if loaded. Protection from weight bearing and urgent imaging are required. Strain treatment, gradual tapering, and modalities delay the diagnosis.
  16. The correct answer is D (0.4 W/cm2, found by multiplying the peak intensity by the duty cycle): Averaged across on and off periods, intensity is the peak value times the fraction of time the sound is on, so 2.0 times 0.20 equals 0.4 W/cm2. Dividing by 0.20 would inflate the figure to 10. The displayed peak intensity overstates the energy actually delivered per unit time.
  17. The correct answer is A (Slow, rhythmic effleurage and petrissage, using a calm tempo): Slow, rhythmic effleurage and petrissage lower muscle tone and calm an anxious, guarded patient. Deep friction suits localized adhesions and would increase guarding here. Tapotement is stimulating, and fast light stroking is too superficial and too quick to relax a spasmed muscle.
  18. The correct answer is A (60 mL/kg/min, from 4,500 mL per minute divided by 75 kg): Relative VO2 max is absolute uptake in milliliters per minute divided by body mass in kilograms: 4,500 divided by 75 gives 60. Dividing liters by kilograms leaves the wrong magnitude. Using pounds understates the result, and dividing by lean mass is a different metric from the per-kilogram body mass norms in the tables.
  19. The correct answer is A (Glenohumeral capsule mobilization and stretching to restore passive glenohumeral elevation): A firm limit of passive glenohumeral motion shows the shoulder joint itself restricts elevation, and the early shrug is a compensation for it. Restoring capsular motion is the priority. Strengthening scapular or cuff muscles cannot overcome a passive restriction.
  20. The correct answer is B (Irradiation, in which strong resisted contractions spread overflow to weaker muscles): Irradiation is the spread of facilitation from strong resisted muscles to weaker ones within the same pattern. Successive induction facilitates the antagonist after the agonist contracts, and reciprocal inhibition relaxes the antagonist during agonist contraction. Autogenic inhibition relaxes the tensed muscle itself. Only irradiation describes the weaker muscles improving.
  21. The correct answer is A (Heavy loads near 80 percent of one repetition maximum, lifted with controlled but forceful effort): By the size principle, Type II units are recruited when force demand is high, so heavy loads near 80 percent of one repetition maximum with forceful intent are needed. Light loads stopped short of fatigue stay mostly within slow units. Moderate loads stopped well short of fatigue also leave Type II units largely unrecruited. Walking is mainly Type I work.
  22. The correct answer is C (A pelvic support belt fitted low, below the anterior superior iliac spines): A belt placed low around the pelvis compresses the sacroiliac joints and improves force closure during loading. That reduces pain with stairs and single-leg tasks. Spinal braces and corsets act on the lumbar and thoracic spine, not the pelvic ring. A binder high over the ribs also gives no sacroiliac compression.
  23. The correct answer is B (Closed chain squats limited to about forty five degrees of knee flexion): In closed chain work patellofemoral stress rises as the knee flexes, so the range is limited to roughly zero to forty five degrees. In open chain work stress is greatest near extension, so heavy resistance in the last forty five degrees is the worst choice. Deep squats and full range extension both enter high stress zones.
  24. The correct answer is D (150 s, found by dividing 9 J by 0.06 W): A 1.5 cm2 spot at 6 J/cm2 needs 9 J of energy. Dividing 9 J by 0.06 W gives 150 s, which is three times the original because the area tripled. Keeping 50 s would drop the dose to a third, dividing by three goes the wrong direction, and tripling again counts the area twice.
  25. The correct answer is B (Anchor at the acromion and lay the strip toward the occiput with light tension): In the standard teaching, inhibition tape runs from insertion toward origin with light tension, which here means starting at the acromion and finishing near the occiput. The origin-to-insertion direction is the facilitation pattern and would raise tone. A fan strip is used for swelling, and a perpendicular tensioned strip is a space or correction technique. None of these match the goal of reducing tone.
  26. The correct answer is A (Refer for urgent medical evaluation and imaging to rule out spinal metastasis): Prior cancer, unexplained weight loss, age over 50 and constant night pain together make spinal metastasis a serious possibility. Prompt medical referral with imaging is the appropriate action. A trial of core work or directional exercise would delay diagnosis, and a routine imaging request three months away is too slow for these red flags. Painful motion in all directions also argues against a simple mechanical pattern.
  27. The correct answer is A (Cooling lowers conduction and proprioception, raising reinjury risk at maximal effort): Cold slows nerve conduction and dulls proprioception and protective reflexes for a time. Returning to full effort too soon therefore risks reinjury. Cold lowers rather than raises metabolism and blood flow. It also reduces collagen extensibility rather than increasing it.
  28. The correct answer is B (Its pulses are monophasic, so net charge accumulates under each electrode with distinct effects): High voltage pulsed current is monophasic, so current flows one direction and each electrode keeps a defined polarity with its own tissue effects. Interferential current alternates and its net charge averages to zero, so electrode labels carry no meaning. High voltage pulsed current has no carrier. The pulse is not biphasic, and voltage level does not reverse ion flow.
  29. The correct answer is A (The deep cervical flexors together with the lower trapezius and serratus anterior): In upper crossed syndrome the deep cervical flexors, lower trapezius, serratus anterior and rhomboids are inhibited and lengthened, while the upper trapezius, levator scapulae, sternocleidomastoid and pectoral muscles become tight. The upper trapezius group and the pectorals are the tight arm of the cross, so they would be stretched rather than strengthened. The suboccipitals, cervical extensors and scalenes also shorten in a forward head posture.
  30. The correct answer is A (A vestibular loss pattern, since only vestibular input remains on those conditions): A person with vestibular loss compensates with vision and somatosensation when they are accurate, but loses balance when both are unreliable and only the labyrinth can help. Healthy people stay upright in those conditions. A visual preference pattern fails when vision is misleading, and somatosensory loss would affect the surface conditions with the eyes closed.
  31. The correct answer is D (Defer loaded pushing and pulling with the arms until the surgeon clears the sternum): After a median sternotomy the divided sternum needs about six to eight weeks to heal, and pushing, pulling, or lifting with the arms creates tension across it. Walking and lower-body work can proceed. Light barbell pressing and push-ups both load the chest wall and arms substantially. Rapid overhead throws add dynamic stress, so loaded arm work waits for surgical clearance.
  32. The correct answer is B (PNF contract-relax with partner resistance followed by a passive stretch of about 30 seconds): PNF techniques that include a contraction before the passive stretch, such as contract-relax, tend to produce slightly larger acute range gains than passive static stretching alone. They also give the runner a partner-assisted method with no pain on contraction. A single static hold gives less range, and ballistic bouncing provokes the stretch reflex. Very brief static holds do not create enough creep to change range.
  33. The correct answer is B (Pain in the posterolateral neck, behind the ear and into the temple): Active upper trapezius trigger points refer to the posterolateral neck, behind the ear and to the temple, which is why they feed tension-type headache. Infraspinatus points refer to the shoulder and arm. Quadratus lumborum points refer to the iliac crest and trochanter. Gluteus minimus points refer along the lateral thigh and calf.
  34. The correct answer is A (Larger disturbances in temperature, catecholamines, phosphocreatine and ion balance that take longer to restore): Intense exercise produces larger and longer lasting disturbances, including higher core temperature, more circulating catecholamines, greater phosphocreatine depletion and ion shifts, so recovery needs more oxygen for longer. Lactate conversion to glucose is only a small part of recovery. Glycogen resynthesis contributes but does not explain the full elevation, and blood volume is restored within hours.
  35. The correct answer is A (Substitution by the global trunk flexors, so retrain gentle drawing in): A pressure rise from 40 to 58 mmHg with trunk flexion and pelvic tilt shows the global flexors substituting for the deep transversus abdominis. The correction is gentle, low-effort drawing in with the pressure kept near baseline. Progressing exercises would reinforce the compensation, and erector spinae or hip flexor explanations do not produce a pressure rise from trunk curl.
  36. The correct answer is D (A combined score of 18 or lower out of 28 points): The Tinetti tool totals 28 points, and a score of 18 or lower indicates high fall risk, 19 to 23 moderate risk, and 24 or higher lower risk. A total of 45 out of 56 is the Berg threshold, and 19 out of 24 belongs to the Dynamic Gait Index. Treating 24 as the high risk cutoff would label most moderate and even low risk patients as high risk.
  37. The correct answer is A (Serratus anterior, strengthened with progressive push-up plus and wall slide exercises): The serratus anterior holds the medial border of the scapula against the thorax, and winging during a pushing movement points to its weakness. Push-up plus and wall slides train it. Rhomboids and lower trapezius are retractors and depressors that do not prevent winging in a push, and pectoralis minor tilts the scapula forward.
  38. The correct answer is D (Systemic lupus erythematosus, an autoimmune disease with photosensitive skin involvement): Lupus is photosensitive, and ultraviolet exposure can flare systemic disease and the rash, so it is a contraindication. Levothyroxine is not a recognized photosensitizer. Type III skin tolerates dosing once the MED is measured. A family history of psoriasis has no bearing on safety and does not make irradiation worse.
  39. The correct answer is D (Avoid loaded flexion beyond ninety degrees and limit weight bearing per the surgeon's protocol): Deep loaded flexion and rotation compress and shear the meniscus, especially the posterior horn, so repairs are protected from loaded flexion beyond about ninety degrees and from twisting in the early weeks. Being pain free does not mean the repair has healed. Overpressure into flexion and pivot drills put the repair at risk.
  40. The correct answer is C (60 lb, calculated as seventy five percent of 80 lb, for ten repetitions): DeLorme uses 50 percent of 10RM in set one, 75 percent in set two, and 100 percent in set three. For a 10RM of 80 lb, the second set is 0.75 times 80, which equals 60 lb. The 40 lb value is the first set, since 0.50 times 80 equals 40 lb. The 80 lb value is the third set. Subtracting a flat 25 lb has no basis in the protocol.
  41. The correct answer is A (Symmetrical biphasic waveform in which the two phases carry equal and opposite charge.): A symmetrical biphasic waveform sends equal charge in opposite directions, so the net charge is zero and no acid or alkaline products accumulate. Monophasic pulsed current and direct current deposit charge of one polarity and produce polar effects under each electrode. A twin-peak monophasic waveform still has a single polarity, so it also leaves a net charge.
  42. The correct answer is C (Improved motor unit recruitment, firing rate and synchronization from neural adaptation): Strength gains in the first several weeks of resistance training occur mostly because the nervous system learns to recruit more motor units, fire them faster and coordinate them better. Measurable hypertrophy lags behind, which is why girth did not change. Fiber splitting is not an established main mechanism in humans, and resistance training shifts Type IIx toward Type IIa rather than creating Type I into Type IIx conversion.
  43. The correct answer is C (Vaulting, lengthening the stance limb to give swing clearance): Vaulting is a rise onto the toes of the stance limb that effectively lengthens it, giving the stiff opposite limb room to swing through. The stem describes plantarflexion of the stance ankle and rising of the body, not elevation of the pelvis or an outward arc. Steppage needs a knee that can flex.
  44. The correct answer is C (Apply the moist heat first, then stretch while the tissue is still warm): Heat increases collagen extensibility and reduces resistance for a short time, so stretching right after heating gives more length gain. Stretching first wastes the effect. Cold does not increase collagen extensibility, and stretching through a pack with thin toweling risks a burn and is not practical.
  45. The correct answer is A (Thumb spica splint holding the thumb and wrist still, with less repeated pinching): The first dorsal compartment tendons move with thumb and wrist motion, so a thumb spica splint rests them and reduced pinching limits irritation. A cock-up splint that frees the thumb leaves the tendons working. An elbow night splint treats nerve problems at the elbow, and a wrap with free thumb gives little rest.
  46. The correct answer is A (Loads of 60 to 70 percent of maximum for 10 to 12 repetitions with continuous breathing): Hypertensive clients should use moderate loads for higher repetitions with continuous breathing, because heavy lifting and breath holding produce large pressure surges. Sixty to 70 percent of maximum for 10 to 12 repetitions fits this. Near-maximal loads and sustained maximal isometrics both drive pressure up sharply. Holding the breath, even with lighter loads, adds a Valsalva effect that is the problem to avoid.
  47. The correct answer is D (Wear single-vision distance glasses when walking outdoors and using stairs): Multifocal lenses blur the floor and reduce contrast and depth cues at the lower field, which raises tripping risk on curbs and stairs. Single-vision distance lenses give a clear view of the walking surface during these tasks. Looking through the reading segment, removing glasses, or using bifocals keeps either blur or uncorrected vision in the critical area.
  48. The correct answer is A (A monophasic pulse made of two very brief peaks separated by a short interval): High voltage pulsed current delivers a monophasic pulse with two extremely short peaks, called the twin-peaked waveform. The symmetrical biphasic square wave is typical of some low-voltage stimulators. The amplitude-modulated sinusoid is interferential current. Long rectangular monophasic pulses are used to stimulate denervated muscle.
  49. The correct answer is C (A dose equal to five times the minimal erythemal dose): The standard grading uses about 2.5 times the MED for E1, 5 times for E2 and 10 times for E3. An E2 reaction is therefore five times the MED. Two and one half times is the E1 dose and ten times is the E3 dose. A simple doubling is not part of the system.
  50. The correct answer is D (Withhold massage and arrange urgent evaluation for deep vein thrombosis): Unilateral calf swelling with warmth, tenderness and a recent long flight is a classic risk picture for deep vein thrombosis, and massage could dislodge a clot. The patient needs prompt imaging and medical evaluation. Even light effleurage, heat or stretching is not safe until the diagnosis is excluded.
  51. The correct answer is B (Medial glide taping of the patella, to reduce pain and permit quadriceps exercise): The pain improvement with a manual medial glide acts as a test of the corrective direction, so medial glide taping is chosen to mimic it and allow pain free strengthening. Lateral glide would worsen the lateral position. Circular compression does not correct patellar position, and a superior glide does not match the finding.
  52. The correct answer is B (Expected cutaneous vasodilation after cooling, resolving within minutes without injury): Uniform redness and warmth that fade within minutes, with normal sensation and no welts, reflects the usual vasodilation after cooling. Frostbite appears white or waxy with sensory loss. Urticaria forms itchy raised welts, and a burn would show injury rather than uniform transient color.
  53. The correct answer is B (A shift of Type IIx fibers toward Type IIa fibers with greater mitochondrial content): Endurance training increases mitochondria, capillaries and oxidative enzymes, and the fast fiber population shifts from the most glycolytic Type IIx toward the more oxidative Type IIa. The reverse shift is seen with detraining. Slow fibers do not convert into fast fibers with training, and capillary density increases rather than falls.
  54. The correct answer is B (Reciprocal inhibition, where quadriceps activity reduces motor neuron drive to the hamstrings): When the agonist, here the quadriceps, contracts, spinal circuits reduce activation of the antagonist hamstrings, which is reciprocal inhibition. This lets the hamstrings relax and permits a greater passive stretch. Autogenic inhibition is the proposed mechanism of hold-relax and contract-relax, where the stretched muscle itself contracts. Spindle excitation of the quadriceps does not facilitate the hamstrings, and creep is a mechanical property, not a neural mechanism.
  55. The correct answer is C (Upper trapezius, lower trapezius, and serratus anterior working together): The upper and lower trapezius and the serratus anterior pull in different directions and together produce scapular upward rotation. The rhomboids, levator, and pectoralis minor rotate it downward. The latissimus, teres major, and posterior deltoid act at the humerus, and the cuff muscles centre the humeral head.
  56. The correct answer is B (Sensory ataxia from loss of position sense, worse with eyes closed): Stamping steps, a wide base, visual dependence, and loss of vibration sense at the ankles indicate sensory ataxia from dorsal column or large fiber loss. A cerebellar ataxia is not improved by looking at the floor or worsened by closing the eyes. Parkinsonian gait features short shuffling steps.
  57. The correct answer is A (No, avoid electrodes across the thorax and choose a non-electrical modality instead): Electrical current across the thorax can be sensed by a demand pacemaker and may inhibit or disturb its output. Lowering intensity, changing mode, or raising the carrier does not remove the risk. The safe approach is to avoid electrical stimulation near the heart and choose a non-electrical modality.
  58. The correct answer is D (Reduce training volume and intensity for several days while tracking resting heart rate): A rising morning resting heart rate, slower pace, poor sleep, and mood change after a rapid mileage jump are classic signs of overreaching or early overtraining. The remedy is a reduction in load with recovery while resting heart rate is monitored. Adding long running increases stress, and a maximal test places further demand on a fatigued athlete. Keeping mileage unchanged with cross-training does not reduce the total load.
  59. The correct answer is A (Reduce effort to a gentle contraction and cue a slow lower abdominal draw in): The transversus abdominis works at low effort, and high effort recruits the rectus abdominis and tilts the pelvis. Cueing a gentle, slow draw lets the deep muscle become the main contributor. Increasing effort worsens substitution, and a posterior tilt is the compensation being observed. A sit-up trains the dominant muscle instead.
  60. The correct answer is A (Medial glide tape pulling the patella toward the medial femoral condyle): The manual medial glide removed her pain, so taping should reproduce that correction by pulling the patella medially. Fat pad unloading tape suits infrapatellar fat pad irritation rather than lateral maltracking. A superior glide does not match the corrective direction that relieved symptoms, and nondirectional compression gives no positional correction.
  61. The correct answer is C (A water-based gel formulation of the drug mixed with a transmission gel): Media with high water content transmit ultrasound with little loss, so a gel formulation is the standard carrier. Thick ointments attenuate the beam. Powder leaves an air gap and foams contain air, both of which reflect energy before it reaches the tissue.
  62. The correct answer is A (Slow speeds around sixty degrees per second for strength, faster speeds above two hundred for function): Slow isokinetic speeds near 60 degrees per second yield high peak torque for strength, while speeds above about 200 degrees per second approximate functional limb speeds and fit velocity specificity. Reversing the order fails the same principle. Keeping slow speeds for function ignores the speed demand of sprinting. Zero degrees per second is an isometric setting.
  63. The correct answer is C (Repeated prone press-ups several times daily, with a lumbar roll when sitting): A response of centralization with extension defines an extension directional preference. Prone press-ups repeated frequently, along with maintaining lordosis in sitting, apply that direction at home. Flexion exercises and sustained long-sitting stretches load the posterior disc in the aggravating direction. Side gliding treats a lateral shift, which this patient does not show.
  64. The correct answer is B (The sprinter performing six all-out 10-second accelerations with brief rests): Creatine supplementation raises phosphocreatine stores, which helps repeated efforts of maximal power lasting about 10 seconds with short recovery. Marathon running and golf rely on oxidative metabolism and gain little. A 2000 m rowing race is mostly aerobic, so the gain is smaller than for repeated all-out sprints.
  65. The correct answer is D (Spastic plantarflexors blocking tibial advance of the shank): When the plantarflexors are spastic or short, the tibia cannot advance over the planted foot, so the force line pushes the knee back into hyperextension. The quadriceps are strong at 4 plus out of 5, tight hamstrings would tend to flex the knee, and gluteus maximus weakness produces a trunk thrust rather than knee recurvatum.
  66. The correct answer is B (Repeat the test with longer exposures until minimal erythema appears): The MED is the shortest exposure that produces minimal redness, and none of these patches did. The true MED is therefore above 100 seconds and still unknown. The test should be repeated with longer exposures. Using 100 seconds, a midpoint, or a multiple of a non-reddening patch all rest on a value that was never measured.
  67. The correct answer is B (Rhythmic initiation, progressing from passive to active assisted to resisted movement): Rhythmic initiation helps with difficulty starting movement and with rigidity by moving the limb passively first, then assisting, then adding light resistance. Rhythmic stabilization uses isometric holds for stability. Slow reversal needs the patient to start active motion. Hold relax is a flexibility technique.
  68. The correct answer is C (The left hand, on the side opposite the painful hip): A cane held opposite the painful hip creates a counter moment that reduces the abductor force needed during stance. The same side hand does not provide that moment and keeps demand high. A walker is more support than a unilateral hip problem requires. A lateral trunk lean unloads somewhat but is a compensation that the cane is meant to replace.
  69. The correct answer is B (Do not use it; ion transfer requires direct current with fixed polarity at the drug pad.): Iontophoresis moves charged ions by repulsion from an electrode of the same polarity, which needs unidirectional direct current. A symmetrical biphasic waveform reverses direction each phase, so ions are pushed in and pulled back with no net transfer. Pulse rate does not carry ions. Lengthening pulse duration does not make a biphasic waveform monophasic.
  70. The correct answer is A (Decreased muscle spindle firing, which lowers reflex muscle tone): Heat reduces spindle firing and lowers reflex tone, which eases guarding in a subacute strain. It does not increase spindle sensitivity. Heat speeds rather than slows conduction, and it dilates rather than constricts vessels.
  71. The correct answer is C (It stretches lengthwise to about 140 percent of its resting length): Standard kinesiology tape stretches lengthwise to roughly 140 percent of its resting length, which approximates skin elasticity. Rigid zinc oxide tape has almost no stretch. Elastic adhesive bandage stretches far more than kinesiology tape. A 180 percent stretch exceeds the tape's design.
  72. The correct answer is C (The left gluteus medius, strengthened with standing and side-lying hip abduction): When the pelvis drops on the right during left stance, the left gluteus medius is failing to hold the pelvis level, so it is the muscle to strengthen. The right gluteus medius is on the swing side. Stretching the right tensor fasciae latae does not address stance side abductor weakness.
  73. The correct answer is D (Defer the test and refer him for blood pressure management first): ACSM lists a resting systolic pressure above 200 or diastolic above 110 mmHg as a relative contraindication to exercise testing. He is asymptomatic, but two consistent high readings still call for deferral and medical management. A gentler protocol does not remove the concern, and a larger cuff would not explain two matching readings. A systolic pressure of 250 is a termination criterion during a test, not a reason to begin.
  74. The correct answer is D (Slow twitch fibers in antigravity muscles such as the soleus and quadriceps): Immobilization causes the most atrophy in slow twitch (type I) fibers of tonic antigravity muscles such as the soleus and quadriceps, because these muscles are active continuously in normal posture and lose that activation under a cast. Fast twitch fibers in the hamstrings, tibialis anterior, and gastrocnemius are used less steadily and lose less relatively. This is why the soleus often shows the most visible loss.
  75. The correct answer is C (Abductor pollicis longus and extensor pollicis brevis within the first dorsal compartment): De Quervain tenosynovitis involves the abductor pollicis longus and extensor pollicis brevis in the first dorsal compartment at the radial styloid. Finkelstein's test stretches these tendons with thumb flexion and ulnar deviation. The third compartment holds extensor pollicis longus, the second holds the radial wrist extensors, and the volar flexors lie on the opposite side.
  76. The correct answer is A (Chronic plaque psoriasis with thick scaling over the elbows and knees): Chronic plaque psoriasis responds to UVB because the radiation slows the overactive skin cell cycle. The other three conditions are photosensitive disorders in which ultraviolet exposure can flare disease or cause severe injury. Lupus can flare with ultraviolet light, xeroderma pigmentosum leaves skin unable to repair ultraviolet damage, and porphyria causes blistering after exposure. Each of these is a contraindication, not an indication.
  77. The correct answer is A (Upper trapezius overactivity, so cue scapular depression and train the lower trapezius): Early shoulder girdle elevation in Janda's abduction test shows the upper trapezius taking over for weak scapular depressors and upward rotators. Cueing depression and training the lower trapezius address that. Serratus weakness tends to cause winging, not a shrug. Middle trapezius work trains retraction, which is not the fault here. The upper trapezius is overactive in this pattern, so shrugs would reinforce the fault.
  78. The correct answer is B (Low load, sustained contractions of about ten seconds, repeated for several repetitions): Local stabilizers such as the multifidus work tonically at low load, so training uses low effort, sustained contractions repeated to build endurance. Brief maximal efforts and heavy loads recruit global muscles. Ballistic rotations train speed and power. The patient's problem is endurance of a tonic muscle.
  79. The correct answer is B (The electron transport chain, using NADH and FADH2 to drive ATP synthase): The Krebs cycle generates NADH and FADH2 and only a small amount of ATP or GTP directly. Those reduced coenzymes donate electrons to the electron transport chain, whose proton gradient drives ATP synthase and yields roughly 26 to 28 of the 30 to 32 ATP from a glucose. Glycolysis nets only two, and beta oxidation acts on fatty acids rather than glucose.
  80. The correct answer is D (It removes the air layer that would reflect nearly all energy at the skin surface): Ultrasound does not travel through air, and the mismatch between air and tissue reflects almost all of the energy back into the transducer, which then overheats. A water-based gel fills the gap and transmits the beam into the tissue. The gel does not change frequency, circulation or the radiating area.
  81. The correct answer is B (Withhold electrical stimulation over the lesion and coordinate pain care with oncology): Known malignancy near the treatment field is a contraindication to electrical stimulation, so the correct step is to withhold it and coordinate pain management with oncology. Sensory intensity may not heat tissue but the contraindication remains. Moving electrodes 10 cm away does not remove the concern in a spine with metastasis. Consent documents risk and does not make an unsafe treatment appropriate.
  82. The correct answer is A (Vigorous intensity, since 90 minutes weekly exceeds the 75 minute minimum): Three 30 minute sessions total 90 minutes per week. That exceeds the 75 minute vigorous minimum but falls short of the 150 minute moderate minimum. Light intensity does not count toward the guideline target. Frequency alone does not meet the recommendation, because total weekly time at the right intensity is what is counted.
  83. The correct answer is B (A lace-up ankle brace that she can adjust herself between halves): Rigid tape loses a large part of its support after about 10 to 20 minutes of exercise, and without a trainer it cannot be reapplied. A lace-up brace maintains support through activity and can be tightened by the athlete. Kinesiology tape and neoprene sleeves offer little mechanical restraint against inversion.
  84. The correct answer is C (Leg press with the feet planted firmly on the moving platform): A closed chain exercise fixes the distal segment against a surface so the limb bears load, as when the feet press on a leg press platform. In a standing extension the working foot moves freely, and standing on the opposite leg does not make it closed. A supine straight leg raise and a machine curl also let the distal segment move freely, so they are open chain exercises.
  85. The correct answer is B (Begin progressive resisted strengthening of the cuff and scapular muscles): With radiographic union, near-full motion, and prior phases complete, progressive resistance for the cuff and scapular muscles is the next step. Returning to passive motion delays recovery. Heavy maximal overhead lifting is too aggressive, and high-velocity thrust near a recently healed fracture in an older patient is inappropriate.
  86. The correct answer is A (Hip strategy, adopted because distal ankle input and strength are inadequate for control): Waist bending with hip motion is the hip strategy, which is recruited when ankle torque is limited by weakness, poor sensation, or a narrow or soft surface. A stepping response would move the feet. The ankle strategy works from distal to proximal and dominates on firm surfaces with small sway, which is not what this man is doing.
  87. The correct answer is C (Motor control retraining with feedback and progressive single-leg task practice): Normal strength, full range, and immediate correction with cueing show the problem is movement control rather than capacity, so feedback and graded task practice are the best fit. Maximal strengthening or stretching addresses deficits that were not found. Orthoses do not retrain the movement pattern.
  88. The correct answer is C (A submaximal cycle ergometer test, extrapolating VO2 max from heart rate at set workloads): A submaximal cycle ergometer test uses heart rate at fixed workloads to predict VO2 max and avoids weight bearing through the painful hip. The Rockport test could suit many adults, but she cannot walk a full mile. The Cooper run is impossible for her. A bench step test loads the arthritic hip with repeated stepping and is a poor fit.
  89. The correct answer is C (Avoid ultrasound over the laminectomy area and consider another approach): Ultrasound is contraindicated over a laminectomy site because the protective bone is missing, so the beam can reach the neural tissue and heat it. A closed skin incision does not mean the deeper tissue is healed. Frequency and speed do not remove the risk.
  90. The correct answer is B (Start at 42 lb and work toward a target of 84 lb): Twenty-five percent of 168 lb is 0.25 x 168 = 42 lb, and 50 percent is 0.50 x 168 = 84 lb, checked again by doubling 42. Starting at 17 lb and ending at 42 lb uses 10 and 25 percent, which is too low. A target of 168 lb is a full body weight pull and is not required. Starting at 84 lb skips the gradual progression and the 126 lb target is 75 percent of body weight.
  91. The correct answer is B (Scoliosis-specific exercise, continued sport and a recheck in 6 to 12 months): A curve under 25 degrees in a nearly mature adolescent has low progression risk and is managed by observation, with scoliosis-specific exercise offered. Bracing is for curves of about 25 to 40 degrees in the growing child, and surgery is considered near 45 to 50 degrees. Sport does not cause progression and can continue.
  92. The correct answer is B (500 mW/cm2, found by dividing 200 mW by 0.4 cm2): Irradiance is power divided by area, so 200 mW divided by 0.4 cm2 equals 500 mW/cm2, the same as 0.5 W/cm2. Multiplying by area is the wrong operation. Leaving the answer as 0.5 mW/cm2 loses the unit conversion, and squaring the area overstates the density.
  93. The correct answer is D (Have her tilt the pelvis through its full range and settle in the comfortable middle): Neutral is the pain-free midrange position found by moving the pelvis between anterior and posterior tilt and resting between the extremes. A flat back is the end of posterior tilt, not neutral. The maximum lumbar arch is the end of anterior tilt. The height of the anterior superior iliac spines is not used to find midrange.
  94. The correct answer is C (Slightly above 12 mA, where tingling is clear and no twitch occurs): Sensory-level treatment sits between the sensory threshold and the motor threshold, so tingling is clear and no twitch occurs. Slightly above 12 mA satisfies that. At 20 mA the intensity nears the motor threshold and is stronger than a sensory goal requires. At 26 mA the muscle is already contracting, and 35 mA gives a strong motor response, which belongs to low-rate protocols.
  95. The correct answer is C (The cognitive stage, where the learner is still working out what the whole task requires): In the Fitts and Posner model the first stage is cognitive. The learner must understand the task, so errors are large and variable, attention demand is high, and cues are needed often. In the associative stage performance is more consistent, and in the autonomous stage the skill needs little attention. Retention is a test of learning after a delay and is not one of the three stages.
  96. The correct answer is D (Grade 4, good, because moderate resistance was tolerated but maximal resistance was not): Holding against moderate resistance but not maximal resistance is grade 4 (good). Grade 5 requires holding against full examiner resistance. Grade 3 describes a patient who completes the range against gravity but tolerates no added resistance. Grade 2 describes motion with gravity eliminated, which is far below this performance.
  97. The correct answer is B (The gluteus maximus muscle at the back of the hip and pelvis): The gluteus maximus is phasic and tends toward inhibition and weakness when the hip stays flexed for long periods. The iliopsoas, rectus femoris and tensor fasciae latae are tonic muscles that tend to shorten. Sitting therefore produces tight hip flexors paired with a weak gluteus maximus.
  98. The correct answer is D (Suspected vertebrobasilar insufficiency with dizziness and visual blurring on neck positioning): Dizziness and visual blurring on neck positioning suggest vertebrobasilar insufficiency, and traction and positioning can reduce vertebral artery flow, making traction unsafe. Radiculopathy that eases with distraction predicts a good response. Mild disc narrowing with limited rotation is a common reason to use traction. Facet hypomobility without neurological signs is also a typical indication, so none of those is a contraindication.
  99. The correct answer is A (A high peak voltage drives current through skin resistance while brief pulses keep it comfortable): The high peak voltage can push current through the resistance of thick skin, and the very short pulse duration keeps charge per pulse low so the treatment stays comfortable. Average current is low, not high, so it does not heat the callus. High voltage pulsed current has no carrier, and its charge per pulse is small, not large.
  100. The correct answer is C (The portion of the transducer face that emits ultrasound, which is smaller than the metal head): ERA is the area of the crystal and head face that actually radiates sound, measured by the manufacturer, and it is always less than the area of the metal head. It is used to convert power in watts into intensity in W/cm2. The treatment field and the penetration depth are different concepts, and the peak to average ratio is the BNR.

Practice Test 2 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is A (The gluteus maximus, working with the hamstrings as secondary assistants): In a bridge the hips rise by hip extension against gravity, and the gluteus maximus is the main driver of that motion. The hamstrings assist but are shortened at the knee in hooklying, so they cannot lead. The adductor magnus has an extensor portion yet contributes less force, and the erector spinae mainly hold the trunk steady. These muscles are active in the bridge, which is why they tempt, but none is the prime mover.
  2. The correct answer is D (Begin with a partial correction of about one quarter inch, then increase gradually): Patients who have adapted to a structural difference for years tolerate a partial correction better, so a lift of about one quarter inch is added and raised gradually. Full immediate correction can provoke new back and hip pain. The lift always goes under the short side, which here is the left. Overcorrecting beyond the measured difference creates a new asymmetry.
  3. The correct answer is C (Shortened pectoralis minor, treated by stretching it and strengthening scapular retractors): A shoulder that rides forward and up off the table in supine points to a short pectoralis minor pulling the coracoid forward and down. Stretching it and strengthening the scapular retractors restores position. The other muscles named do not tilt the scapula forward in this way.
  4. The correct answer is A (Paraspinal electrodes at the L4 to S1 root levels, plus a pair along the dermatome.): Segmental placement puts electrodes over the root levels supplying the painful area, here the paraspinals at L4 to S1 plus a pair along the dermatome, so A-beta input gates nociception at the involved segment. Nerve-trunk placement at the knee and ankle is a real technique but does not address the root level. Opposite-side paraspinal placement lies off the affected segment. Spanning thoracic to sacral levels dilutes input across unrelated segments.
  5. The correct answer is D (The brachial plexus trunks together with the subclavian artery, but not the subclavian vein): The brachial plexus trunks and the subclavian artery pass between the anterior and middle scalenes. The subclavian vein runs in front of the anterior scalene, so it is not in the interscalene gap. The axillary vessels and cords belong to the retropectoral space under the pectoralis minor.
  6. The correct answer is A (Stretch the tensor fasciae latae in hip adduction and extension, and strengthen the gluteus medius): A positive Ober test points to a tight tensor fasciae latae and iliotibial band, which is lengthened with the hip adducted and extended. Weak hip abductors, especially the gluteus medius, let the thigh drift into adduction and load the lateral knee, so strengthening them completes the plan. Reversing the roles, targeting the hamstrings, or loading the knee with deep squats does not address these impairments.
  7. The correct answer is D (It creates back pressure that keeps small airways open and reduces air trapping): In emphysema the small airways collapse during exhalation because elastic recoil is lost. Exhaling through pursed lips adds resistance at the mouth, which transmits back pressure into the airways and holds them open, so less air is trapped. This slows exhalation, which lowers respiratory rate and breathlessness. It does not strengthen the diaphragm or raise carbon dioxide.
  8. The correct answer is C (Standing waves from the stationary head caused blood cell stasis and endothelial injury): A stationary sound head lets reflected waves combine into standing waves, whose pressure nodes stack red blood cells and injure vessel walls, producing bruising and soreness. Moving the head continuously prevents this. Cavitation needs far higher intensities or very low frequencies, periosteal heating would produce bone pain rather than calf bruising, and gel air pockets mainly reduce transmission instead of concentrating injury.
  9. The correct answer is B (Improvement will carry over only partly and appear smaller at the faster test speed): Strength gains are greatest at or near the trained velocity, so a program done at 60 degrees per second transfers only partly to a much faster test speed. Larger gains at the faster speed or equal carryover across speeds ignore this specificity. The dynamometer holds velocity constant but does not make adaptations transfer between velocities.
  10. The correct answer is B (Three sets of fifteen, twice daily for twelve weeks, with knee straight and bent): The Alfredson protocol uses eccentric heel drops from a step, three sets of fifteen, twice daily for twelve weeks. It is done once with the knee straight to load the gastrocnemius and once with the knee bent to load the soleus. Shorter or less frequent programs do not match the studied dosage, and training on flat ground removes the eccentric drop below neutral.
  11. The correct answer is D (D2 flexion, because it combines flexion with abduction and external rotation): D2 flexion combines flexion, abduction, and external rotation and finishes overhead and out to the side, matching the goal. D1 flexion shares flexion and external rotation but uses adduction. The two extension diagonals move in the opposite direction. Abduction is the detail that separates the correct pattern from D1 flexion.
  12. The correct answer is B (Grade 1, trace, because a contraction is palpable without joint movement): A palpable or visible contraction without joint motion is grade 1 (trace). Grade 0 means no contraction can be felt at all, and here a flicker was palpated. Grade 2 requires full range in the gravity-eliminated position, which did not happen. Grade 3 requires full range against gravity.
  13. The correct answer is A (Scar massage, early active finger and wrist motion, and tendon and nerve gliding exercises): Two weeks after release, scar massage with early active motion and tendon and nerve gliding limits adhesions and stiffness. The transverse carpal ligament is divided, not repaired, so long immobilization adds stiffness without protecting anything. Heavy putty grip stresses a tender palm too soon. Aggressive flexion stretching can irritate the incision and provoke scar reaction.
  14. The correct answer is A (Increase the exposure to 8 minutes at the unchanged lamp distance): At 60 degrees the cosine is 0.5, so the surface receives half the perpendicular intensity. Time must therefore be divided by the cosine: 4 minutes divided by 0.5 equals 8 minutes. Keeping 4 minutes underdoses by half. Multiplying by the cosine gives 2 minutes, which makes the underdose worse, and adding 50 percent gives 6 minutes, which is still short.
  15. The correct answer is B (Very light stretch, roughly 0 to 15 percent, with the tails fanned toward a node basin): Lymphatic applications use very light tension, about 0 to 15 percent, so the skin is lifted slightly and the tails direct fluid toward a lymph node basin. Moderate stretch is a mechanical correction tension and a fan pointed away from the nodes sends fluid the wrong way. A quarter stretch is too tight for drainage, and a single straight strip does not create a fan channel for fluid.
  16. The correct answer is D (Monitor blood pressure, since extensive cold can cause a sympathetic pressor response): Cooling a large area triggers sympathetic vasoconstriction that can raise blood pressure, so a patient with hypertension needs monitoring. A brief exposure still stimulates cold receptors and does not remove the need for checks. Towel protection and screening for cold intolerance are sensible, but they address skin injury and cold hypersensitivity rather than the pressor response that matters in this patient.
  17. The correct answer is B (The foot fails to resupinate in terminal stance, leaving a flexible lever): Normally the subtalar joint supinates in terminal stance so the midfoot locks into a rigid lever for push-off. In this runner the foot stays pronated and flat while the heel rises, so the lever stays flexible, which stresses the arch and the medial knee. Cavus supination, foot slap, and early heel rise do not fit the findings.
  18. The correct answer is A (Golgi tendon organ, producing autogenic inhibition of the muscle that is generating the tension): The Golgi tendon organ lies in series with muscle fibers at the musculotendinous junction and monitors tension. Its Ib afferents inhibit the alpha motor neurons of the same muscle, which is autogenic inhibition. The spindle monitors length and excites the muscle, reciprocal inhibition acts on the antagonist, and a Pacinian corpuscle senses vibration and deep pressure, not muscle tension.
  19. The correct answer is D (Standing or prone thoracic extension with scapular retraction, avoiding loaded spinal flexion): Patients with osteoporosis should avoid loaded spinal flexion and twisting because they raise the risk of vertebral compression fractures. Extension and scapular retraction strengthen the back extensors and counter the kyphotic posture. Crunches, toe touches, and weighted twists load the spine in the wrong direction.
  20. The correct answer is A (Phase I, the inpatient stage built around early mobilization, low-level activity, and discharge education): Phase I covers the hospital stay and centers on early, low-level mobilization, risk factor education, and discharge planning. The patient is still an inpatient, so any outpatient phase is premature. Telemetry is used in hospital too, so its presence does not make this the supervised outpatient stage. Later phases focus on progressive conditioning and finally long-term maintenance.
  21. The correct answer is C (Left gluteus medius, assisted by gluteus minimus and tensor fasciae latae): In single-leg stance the abductors of the stance hip hold the pelvis level against the weight of the body. The pelvis drops on the right when the left stance abductors are weak, which is the left gluteus medius with help from gluteus minimus and tensor fasciae latae. The right side is the swing limb here. Adductors and extensors do not control frontal plane pelvic drop.
  22. The correct answer is B (Balance the two channel amplitudes so the currents interfere with comparable strength): Interference forms a strong beat only where the two currents have comparable amplitude, so a 30 mA and 10 mA mismatch gives weak pulsing. Balancing the channels restores deep modulation. Polarity is irrelevant for alternating currents, and a higher carrier changes comfort and penetration rather than beat depth. Changing the sweep alters the beat frequency but leaves the amplitude imbalance in place.
  23. The correct answer is A (Stretch hip flexors and lumbar extensors, and activate gluteus maximus and abdominals): The lower crossed pattern pairs tight hip flexors and lumbar extensors with weak gluteus maximus and abdominals, so treatment lengthens the tight group and activates the weak one with pelvic control. Reversing the roles deepens the lordosis. Stretching hamstrings and gluteals targets the wrong muscles. Anchored full sit-ups recruit the hip flexors strongly, so they reinforce the anterior tilt.
  24. The correct answer is C (Hydrochlorothiazide, the thiazide diuretic listed among his medications): Thiazide diuretics such as hydrochlorothiazide are well known photosensitizers that lower the MED. Levothyroxine replaces a hormone and does not change ultraviolet response at a replacement dose. Famotidine is not recognized as photosensitizing. Acetaminophen does not thin the epidermis or lower the MED.
  25. The correct answer is D (A short, brisk session of petrissage and tapotement to stimulate the muscles): A short pre-event session should raise readiness, so brisk petrissage and tapotement stimulate the muscles without lowering tone. Slow effleurage and sustained compression relax tissue, which suits recovery rather than a sprint in 30 minutes. Deep stripping and long sessions can leave the muscles sore or less responsive. The stimulating approach fits the time and the goal.
  26. The correct answer is D (Neutral wrist night splint, activity modification, and tendon and median nerve gliding exercises): Nocturnal median-distribution numbness relieved by shaking and a positive Phalen's test with a negative Spurling's test indicates carpal tunnel syndrome. A neutral splint at night, activity changes, and gliding exercises are the usual conservative plan. Neck traction, scalene stretching, and elbow padding target other sites.
  27. The correct answer is A (After the first 48 to 72 hours, once acute inflammation has subsided): Heat is generally introduced once the acute inflammatory phase has settled, typically after about 48 to 72 hours, as in this patient on day 4. Heat in the first day risks more bleeding and swelling. Waiting six weeks delays a useful treatment, and alternating heat with cold at injury confuses the two phases.
  28. The correct answer is C (Burst mode, with pulses near 100 pps grouped into bursts about 2 times per second.): Burst mode delivers packets of high-frequency pulses, near 100 pps, repeated at a low rate of about 1 to 4 bursts per second. It combines a high-frequency component with a low-frequency burst envelope and can be more comfortable than twitching at motor level. Conventional and brief intense modes deliver continuous trains without burst grouping. Acupuncture-like mode is a continuous low-rate train.
  29. The correct answer is D (Lift only one limb at a time until the trunk stays quiet, then combine them): Lifting a single limb halves the perturbation to the trunk, letting the patient learn to hold neutral before the two limbs are combined. Shortening the range while lifting both limbs still produces the same rotational challenge. Prone lifts train the extensors in a different posture and do not regress the demand for anti-rotation control. A Swiss ball makes the task less stable and harder.
  30. The correct answer is D (Waddling gait from bilateral hip abductor weakness typical of muscular dystrophy): A wide base, lordosis and alternating pelvic drop reflect weak hip abductors on both sides, giving a waddling gait. With calf pseudohypertrophy and climbing the legs with the hands, this fits muscular dystrophy. A Trendelenburg gait is one-sided, and an antalgic gait is a limp driven by pain. Ataxia also has a wide base but shows irregular steps without the pelvic drop or Gowers maneuver.
  31. The correct answer is B (Prone or standing back extension with a neutral spine, avoiding loaded forward flexion): Loaded or repeated forward flexion raises compressive force on the anterior vertebral bodies and is linked with compression fractures when the T-score is this low. Back extension with a neutral spine strengthens the extensors and counters the stoop without that risk. Loaded rotation adds torsion to weakened bone. Crunches and end range cat-camel flexion both repeatedly load the spine in flexion.
  32. The correct answer is A (7 METs, found by dividing 24.5 by the resting value of 3.5): One MET equals 3.5 mL per kg per minute, so METs equal oxygen uptake divided by 3.5. Dividing 24.5 by 3.5 gives 7.0, and multiplying 7 by 3.5 returns 24.5 to confirm it. Using a divisor of 2.0 or 2.9 inflates the result to 12.3 or 8.4, while 4.9 deflates it to 5.0. Only the standard resting value of 3.5 gives the correct conversion.
  33. The correct answer is D (Clean and dry the skin, trim or shave the hair and apply underwrap): Clean, dry, hair-free skin covered by underwrap protects the skin on removal while letting the tape anchor securely. Taping over an elastic bandage loosens as the player sweats. Adherent spray over hair still pulls the hair when the tape is removed, and lubricant prevents the tape from sticking.
  34. The correct answer is B (Vary the load and volume in planned phases, then raise the heavy phase intensity gradually): A plateau in a well recovered trainee on a fixed program means the stimulus has become too familiar, so planned variation of load and volume in phases with gradual rises in heavy phase intensity restarts adaptation. Adding sessions at the same demand adds little. Changing exercises at every session removes progressive structure, and pushing linear increases on a stalled lifter risks overload without a new stimulus.
  35. The correct answer is C (Gently lift the pelvic floor, as if stopping the flow of urine): The pelvic floor and transversus abdominis co-contract at low effort, so a gentle pelvic floor lift can facilitate the deep abdominal muscle when isolation is poor. A buttock squeeze with a posterior tilt substitutes the gluteals and hip muscles. Curling the trunk recruits the rectus abdominis, the very muscle already dominating. A large chest breath expands the ribs and recruits superficial muscles.
  36. The correct answer is A (About 1.26 meters per second, from 75.6 meters per minute divided by sixty): Speed equals cadence times step length: 108 steps per minute times 0.70 meters is 75.6 meters per minute. Dividing by 60 seconds gives 1.26 meters per second, rechecked as 0.70 times 108 equals 75.6. Using stride length with a step cadence doubles the answer, and halving cadence or dividing by 100 gives the other wrong values.
  37. The correct answer is B (Wear UV-blocking goggles for the whole session, as the patient does): Ultraviolet radiation scatters from walls, equipment and the patient's skin, so a clinician in the room is exposed even when not facing the lamp. UV-blocking goggles protect the clinician's eyes for the full session. Tinted sunglasses may transmit ultraviolet radiation. Looking away does not stop reflected exposure, and the patient's goggles protect only the patient.
  38. The correct answer is A (Baseline of 20 mm Hg, progressing in 2 mm Hg steps up to 30 mm Hg): The craniocervical flexion test inflates the cuff to a 20 mm Hg baseline and targets five 2 mm Hg steps up to 30 mm Hg. The higher values in the 40 to 60 range are associated with lumbar pressure biofeedback. Starting at 30 or at 10 mm Hg changes both the baseline and the range away from the standard test.
  39. The correct answer is B (Scalene and pectoralis minor stretching with posture correction and scapular strengthening): Positive overhead stress testing with ulnar-sided symptoms suggests thoracic outlet compression, and initial treatment is conservative: stretch the tight scalenes and pectoralis minor, improve posture, strengthen the scapular muscles, and modify work. Surgery is considered only after failed conservative care. Wrist splints and heavy overhead loading do not help.
  40. The correct answer is C (The site where the motor nerve enters the muscle, where the stimulation threshold is lowest.): The motor point is the skin site over the place where the motor nerve branch enters the muscle, so the least current is needed to depolarize the nerve and recruit fibers. A pen electrode probe finds the site giving the strongest twitch at the lowest amplitude. The belly center may be nearby but is not defined by fiber bulk. Tendon junctions and bony origins do not have the lowest nerve threshold.
  41. The correct answer is A (Raise the load slightly after two extra repetitions in the last set across two sessions): The two-for-two rule says to increase the load by a small amount once the patient can perform two or more repetitions beyond the target in the last set on two consecutive sessions. A quarter jump after one easy session is too large. Waiting for soreness to vanish ignores performance. Adding repetitions to thirty shifts training toward endurance, which does not keep overloading strength.
  42. The correct answer is A (Neural sensitivity rather than hamstring shortness, so use gentle neurodynamic mobilization): Symptoms that change with ankle position and neck flexion, which alter neural tension but not hamstring length, point to neural tissue sensitivity. Gentle neurodynamic mobilization is the better fit, and aggressive stretching can aggravate the nerve. A tear or hip flexor shortening would not respond to those distant movements.
  43. The correct answer is A (Use a lower intensity and shorter duration with close monitoring, because heat clears slowly): Poor circulation clears heat slowly, so thermal ultrasound needs lower intensity, shorter duration and careful monitoring. A stationary head concentrates heat and risks burns. Heating does depend on blood flow, and raising the dose in poorly perfused tissue invites overheating.
  44. The correct answer is C (Delay sprinting and continue eccentric strengthening until testing is pain free and symmetrical): Return to sprinting requires pain free resisted testing and near symmetrical strength, because sprinting loads the hamstring eccentrically at high speed. Pain on manual resistance shows the tissue is not ready, even though jogging and range look good. Stretching alone does not rebuild the strength needed to protect against reinjury.
  45. The correct answer is D (Fast-twitch type II fibers, which grow more than type I fibers): Heavy resistance training produces greater hypertrophy in type II fibers, particularly type IIa, than in type I fibers. Both types can grow, but not equally. Hyperplasia contributes little in humans, so growth in fiber size is the main structural change.
  46. The correct answer is D (Reduced contact area raised the current density beneath the pad edge that stayed attached.): With a constant current device, total current stays at 45 mA when contact area falls. The same current through a smaller area raises current density, which causes a sharp sensation and risk of burn. The pad should be reattached or replaced and intensity reduced. Impedance changes do not add current in this device type, polarity does not reverse, and accommodation reduces sensation rather than sharpening it.
  47. The correct answer is C (40 s, found by dividing 2 J by 0.05 W): The energy needed per point is 4 J/cm2 times 0.5 cm2, which equals 2 J. Dividing 2 J by 0.05 W gives 40 s. Skipping the area gives 80 s, dividing by the area gives 160 s, and misreading the output as 5 W gives less than a second.
  48. The correct answer is A (Perform the side bridge with the knees bent and the lower legs on the table): Her hips drop within five seconds, so she needs less lever arm while keeping the same lateral trunk muscle focus. Bending the knees shortens the lever and lets her hold a true side bridge. Lifting the top foot and adding weight increase demand. A prone plank changes the pattern and moves emphasis away from the lateral trunk.
  49. The correct answer is C (Withhold exercise and contact her cardiology team the same day about possible decompensation): A gain of several pounds in days, orthopnea, and new ankle edema point to fluid overload and worsening heart failure. Exercise should be withheld and the cardiology team contacted promptly. Shortened or seated sessions still stress a decompensating heart. Normal saturation and heart rate do not exclude congestion.
  50. The correct answer is D (Release the traction, keep him supine, raise his legs, and monitor vital signs): Pallor, sweating, faintness, bradycardia and low pressure suggest a vasovagal reaction to traction. The belt must be released and the patient kept supine with legs raised while vitals are monitored. Sitting up risks a faint, and continuing at lower force or resuming soon exposes him to the same trigger.
  51. The correct answer is A (The extensor carpi radialis brevis tendon at its common extensor origin): Lateral epicondylitis most often involves degeneration of the extensor carpi radialis brevis origin on the lateral epicondyle. Extensor carpi ulnaris tendinopathy occurs at the ulnar wrist. The brachioradialis inserts far distally at the radial styloid, and the common flexor tendon is the medial elbow structure involved in golfer's elbow.
  52. The correct answer is D (Remove the unit from service for calibration, because the actual output is below the displayed setting): A unit delivering 3 watts at a 5 watt setting is out of calibration and must leave service until it is calibrated. Patient warmth is not a reliable dose measure. Raising the setting guesses at an unverified output, and treating now with calibration deferred still uses a known faulty device.
  53. The correct answer is D (About 24 to 48 hours after the session, then returning toward baseline): After a hard session, muscle protein synthesis rises and typically stays elevated for roughly 24 to 48 hours before returning toward baseline. Ten to twelve hours is too short, while four to five days or a week or more overstates the window and mistakes soreness duration or fiber turnover for the synthesis response.
  54. The correct answer is D (Progressive heavy resistance including eccentric hamstring loading, advancing toward sprint specific work): In the remodeling phase the tissue needs progressive loading to regain strength and tolerate sport demands, so heavy resistance with eccentric emphasis and then sprint-specific work is appropriate. Submaximal isometrics and unloaded motion belong to early phases. Delaying strengthening to twelve weeks with passive stretching leaves the muscle underloaded.
  55. The correct answer is D (The anterior and middle scalene muscles on the right side): The anterior and middle scalenes attach to the first rib and elevate it during inspiration, so tightness holds the rib elevated. The levator scapulae and upper trapezius are tight in neck pain but move the scapula, not the first rib. Pectoralis minor acts on ribs three to five, and serratus posterior inferior acts on the lower ribs. Treatment often releases the scalenes and mobilizes the first rib.
  56. The correct answer is C (Tilt the pelvis posteriorly and squeeze the glute before leaning forward): An arched back and anterior tilt let the pelvis slide forward so the iliopsoas never reaches end range. A posterior tilt with a gluteal squeeze fixes the pelvis and loads the hip flexor. Leaning farther adds range but keeps the compensation. Longer holds with the arch repeat the error. Moving the foot closer simply shortens the stretch.
  57. The correct answer is D (Saline-moistened gauze around the electrode, packed into the wound bed): Saline-moistened gauze gives a conductive, moist interface that conforms to the wound bed and spreads current evenly. A sterile cover holds it in place and keeps the wound clean. Dry gauze is a poor conductor and can cause a sharp sensation. Gel is not the usual interface for a wound, and an electrode on intact skin away from the wound does not stimulate the wound tissue.
  58. The correct answer is C (2,880 mL/min, from 18,000 mL multiplied by 0.16): Cardiac output of 18 L/min is 18,000 mL/min, and 16 mL per 100 mL equals 0.16. Multiplying gives 18,000 times 0.16, or 2,880 mL/min. Dividing by 16 gives 1,125, which is the wrong operation. Using 1,800 mL loses a factor of ten, and 2.88 is the answer in liters but is mislabeled as milliliters.
  59. The correct answer is D (Lift the leg only to the horizontal level of the trunk, keeping the pelvis level): Lifting above trunk level produces lumbar extension and pelvic rotation, so the leg should rise only to horizontal while the pelvis stays level. Cueing a deliberate lumbar arch trains the very extension that is the fault. Higher lifts increase the extension, and an ankle weight adds load to a faulty pattern.
  60. The correct answer is C (Withhold traction and refer for imaging because of suspected atlantoaxial instability): A positive Sharp-Purser test suggests laxity of the transverse ligament, which is a recognized risk in long standing rheumatoid disease, and traction or rotation could endanger the cord. The right step is to hold traction and obtain imaging. Low force traction, manual pull with rotation, or supine mechanical traction all load an unstable upper cervical segment.
  61. The correct answer is C (Soleus, stretched with the knee flexed so the gastrocnemius is slackened): With the knee flexed, the gastrocnemius is slack, so a limit that persists points to the soleus, which crosses only the ankle. The soleus is stretched with the knee flexed and the ankle dorsiflexed. Normal talar glides and an elastic end feel make a joint restriction unlikely. Stretching with the knee extended or hyperextended loads the gastrocnemius instead.
  62. The correct answer is D (About 7 minutes, found by dividing 4 degrees by 0.6 degrees per minute): Time equals target rise divided by rate: 4 degrees divided by 0.6 degrees per minute is 6.7, about 7 minutes. Using the 1 MHz rate of 0.2 would give 20 minutes, the multiplication yields 2.4, and averaging the rates gives 10. The 3 MHz beam heats a superficial tendon about three times faster at the same intensity.
  63. The correct answer is D (About 3 minutes of passive rest between each maximal sprint effort): Phosphocreatine resynthesis has a half-time of about 30 seconds, so recovery is roughly 50 percent at 30 seconds, 75 percent at 60 seconds and nearly complete at 3 minutes. Passive rest restores it fastest because resynthesis is oxygen dependent. Intervals of 10 to 45 seconds leave phosphocreatine only partly restored and power output declines on later sprints.
  64. The correct answer is C (Isolated glute squeezes before the lift, then bridges or quadruped hip extension with the knee bent): In Janda's description the gluteus maximus should fire first, and a late glute with dominant hamstrings and erectors marks substitution. Retraining begins with isolated activation and then low load patterns such as bridges and quadruped extension with the knee bent to reduce hamstring involvement. Strengthening the dominant muscles or stretching the weak glute reinforces the fault.
  65. The correct answer is B (Lumbar flexion substitutes for hip flexion, so practice a dowel hinge with a neutral spine): A dowel that leaves the sacrum shows the lumbar spine flexing first, a movement pattern error in which spinal motion replaces hip motion. Motor practice of the hinge with a neutral spine addresses it directly. Hip flexor stretching, crunches and thoracic flexion do not train the hip dominant pattern.
  66. The correct answer is B (Intensity at the skin increased about fourfold at the closer distance): Halving the distance multiplies intensity by two squared, which equals four. With unchanged time the dose rose fourfold, which is enough to turn a routine exposure into a second degree reaction. A linear estimate of twofold would understate the change, and eightfold overstates it. The beam angle was not described as altered.
  67. The correct answer is D (Weighted forward trunk flexion such as sit-ups or loaded toe touches): Loaded spinal flexion increases anterior vertebral compression and raises the risk of a compression fracture in osteoporotic bone. Low-load back extension and scapular retraction strengthen postural muscles safely. End-range rotation also stresses osteoporotic vertebrae, so it is a poor choice, but weighted flexion is the highest-risk movement and the one to avoid most clearly in this patient.
  68. The correct answer is A (Withhold treatment and arrange urgent evaluation for deep vein thrombosis): Unilateral calf swelling, warmth and tenderness after a long flight suggests deep vein thrombosis, so electrical muscle pumping is withheld and urgent evaluation arranged. Sensory-level current is not a safe alternative while a clot is possible. Compression and later motor-level current, or monitoring tenderness during pumping, delay diagnosis and risk embolization.
  69. The correct answer is C (Quadruped on hands and knees, starting with weight shifts before lifting any limb): The developmental sequence moves from rolling and prone on elbows to quadruped, then kneeling, half-kneeling, and standing. Quadruped adds weight bearing through the hands and knees with a smaller base than prone and prepares the trunk and limb girdles for later postures. Half-kneeling and standing each demand more upright control than this patient has shown. Single-leg stance on foam is a late balance progression.
  70. The correct answer is C (The right superior gluteal nerve to gluteus medius): The superior gluteal nerve supplies gluteus medius, gluteus minimus, and tensor fasciae latae, and its injury produces abductor weakness and a Trendelenburg sign. The inferior gluteal nerve supplies gluteus maximus, which tests normal here. Sciatic and femoral nerve injuries would weaken the hamstrings or quadriceps.
  71. The correct answer is B (Approximately 20 to 30 minutes of continuous application): A hydrocollator pack stays therapeutically warm for roughly 20 to 30 minutes, which is the usual dose for superficial moist heat. Forty-five to sixty minutes exceeds the time the pack holds heat and raises skin risk. A short exposure gives little warming even with a second pack, and extra toweling only reduces heat transfer without heating deeper tissue.
  72. The correct answer is A (A patellar stabilizing sleeve with a lateral buttress to guide the patella): A patellar stabilizing sleeve with a lateral buttress resists lateral glide and improves tracking in the trochlear groove, which matches this presentation. An infrapatellar strap compresses the tendon and is used for patellar tendinopathy, not tracking. A plain neoprene sleeve gives warmth and compression without guiding the patella. A functional brace limiting tibial translation protects a deficient cruciate ligament, which is a different problem.
  73. The correct answer is D (Begin light to moderate exercise, hydrate well, and recheck glucose during the session): With glucose above 250 mg/dL, the key question is ketones.
