When ketones are negative and the person feels well, light to moderate exercise with hydration and glucose monitoring is acceptable. Postponing is unnecessary here. Vigorous exercise can raise glucose further through counter-regulatory hormones, and a correction insulin dose just before exercise adds hypoglycemia risk.
  74. The correct answer is A (The anterior talofibular ligament on the lateral side of the ankle joint): With the foot plantarflexed and inverted, the anterior talofibular ligament is the primary restraint and is stretched first, so it is the most commonly torn lateral ligament. The calcaneofibular ligament usually fails later, with greater force or in more dorsiflexion. The deltoid ligament resists eversion, and the anterior tibiofibular ligament is injured by external rotation in a high ankle sprain.
  75. The correct answer is C (Grade 3, which is full range of motion against gravity with no added resistance tolerated): Grade 3 (fair) means the patient completes the full range against gravity but tolerates no added manual resistance. Grade 2 requires the gravity-eliminated position, so it understates this patient. Grade 4 requires holding against moderate resistance, which this patient cannot do. Grade 1 describes only a palpable flicker without joint motion.
  76. The correct answer is A (660 nm red light, absorbed mainly within the epidermis and superficial dermis): A 2 mm ulcer needs energy absorbed in the skin layers, which is where 660 nm red light deposits most of its energy. The 810 nm wavelength passes through to deeper structures and is chosen for muscle and joints. The 980 and 1064 nm wavelengths shift toward water absorption or deep delivery and are poorly suited to a superficial wound.
  77. The correct answer is A (A rigid structural kyphosis, so refer for imaging and use extension mobility with strengthening): A curve that does not flatten in prone press-up and has little active extension is rigid and may be structural, so imaging and a mobility based program are warranted. Postural exercises alone fit a flexible curve that corrects with positioning. Heavy loaded extension does not suit a rigid curve.
  78. The correct answer is A (Right gluteus medius weakness, targeted with side-lying abduction): In single-leg stance the stance-side gluteus medius holds the pelvis level, so weakness on the right allows the left pelvis to drop. Side-lying hip abduction and single-leg stance progressions strengthen it. The left muscle is not bearing weight, and extensor or adductor weakness does not produce this frontal plane drop.
  79. The correct answer is B (Additional, higher threshold motor units are recruited to replace fatiguing units and hold the force): During a sustained submaximal contraction the initially active slow units fatigue, so the nervous system recruits additional higher threshold units to keep the force constant, and EMG amplitude rises. Fiber area does not change within one hold, rate coding alone would not add recruited units, and derecruitment would lower rather than raise the signal. This pattern is a standard sign of muscle fatigue on surface EMG.
  80. The correct answer is A (A patient with diabetic peripheral neuropathy and absent temperature sensation in the area): A patient who cannot feel temperature cannot warn the therapist of overheating, so a burn can occur unnoticed. Chronic tightness and residual tightness are classic heat indications. Subacute joint stiffness is a standard indication for heat before stretching.
  81. The correct answer is B (Stop the exercise, seat him, and give his prescribed sublingual nitroglycerin per protocol): Angina at a rating of 3 on a 4-point scale is a criterion for stopping exercise. He should be seated and given his prescribed nitroglycerin, with symptoms reassessed. Lowering the load, waiting, or changing to arm work all keep a symptomatic heart working. Stable blood pressure does not make continuing safe.
  82. The correct answer is D (A 20 percent duty cycle with 15 contractions in the session.): One cycle lasts 12 plus 48 equals 60 seconds, so the duty cycle is 12 divided by 60, or 20 percent. Fifteen minutes is 900 seconds, and 900 divided by 60 equals 15 cycles, so 15 contractions. Using on divided by off gives 25 percent, and dividing 900 by 48 gives 19. Dividing 900 by 12 gives 75, which ignores the rest period.
  83. The correct answer is A (Avoid end-range stretching and prioritize strengthening and motor control within a controlled range): A Beighton score of 8 shows generalized hypermobility, and the sensation of tightness is usually muscle protective tension around lax joints. Stretching or mobilizing at end range adds laxity and instability, while strengthening and motor control within a controlled range protect the joints. Partner overpressure and grade IV mobilization push a hypermobile joint beyond a safe range.
  84. The correct answer is C (Reduce wear to demanding tasks only while starting a trunk stabilization exercise program): Prolonged corset use can reduce trunk muscle endurance, so wear is cut back to the most demanding tasks while a stabilization program rebuilds muscle support. A more rigid brace adds dependence. Waiting for a month before exercise lets deconditioning continue. Abrupt removal with six weeks of restricted lifting does not rebuild endurance either.
  85. The correct answer is A (The sciatic nerve as it passes deep to or through the muscle belly): The sciatic nerve passes deep to, or sometimes through, the piriformis, so a tight or spastic muscle can compress it. The obturator nerve supplies the medial thigh and does not pass here. The superior gluteal nerve exits above the piriformis, and an L5 root lesion would be expected to disturb lumbar movement or the straight leg raise.
  86. The correct answer is D (Supine isometric brace, quadruped limb lifts, standing split stance, then single-leg hops with rotation): A functional progression begins in supported, low demand positions and moves toward upright, dynamic tasks, so supine bracing comes first, then quadruped lifts, then standing split stance, and finally hopping with rotation. Putting standing before quadruped skips a step. Hops before split stance raise the demand too soon. Reversing the order begins with the most complex task.
  87. The correct answer is A (Two degrees of glenohumeral motion for each degree of scapulothoracic motion): Over the full range of elevation the glenohumeral joint contributes about 120 degrees and the scapulothoracic joint about 60 degrees, a 2 to 1 ratio. The reversed or equal ratios do not match measured motion. A larger early ratio near 3 to 1 appears in the first part of the range, not across the whole arc.
  88. The correct answer is C (Body weight compresses the toweling and reduces insulation, so add layers and recheck skin): When a patient lies on a pack, body weight flattens the toweling and squeezes out the trapped air, so less insulation remains and more heat reaches the skin. The therapist adds toweling and rechecks the skin. Removing layers, wetting the towels or ignoring a report of increasing heat each raise burn risk.
  89. The correct answer is A (A lace-up brace, because adhesive tape loses much of its restraint early in exercise): Adhesive tape can lose much of its restraint within the first 10 to 20 minutes of vigorous activity. A lace-up brace keeps its support through a long session of sprinting and cutting. Extra layers of tape still loosen, and a neoprene sleeve or elastic wrap add sensation without real restraint. The brace is the best match for a two hour practice.
  90. The correct answer is A (Bouts of at least 10 minutes each can be accumulated toward the goal): The classic ACSM guidance and the 2008 federal guidelines allowed exercise to be accumulated in bouts of at least 10 minutes, so three brisk 10 minute walks can add up to a 30 minute day. Newer guidance, such as the 2018 Physical Activity Guidelines, counts bouts of any length, which is why this question names the classic standard. A 20 minute minimum or a requirement for one continuous 30 minute session is stricter than either version. Five minute bouts fall below the classic minimum.
  91. The correct answer is A (Hip abductor and external rotator strengthening with squat movement retraining): Medial knee drift and pelvic drop on a single leg squat point to weak hip abductors and external rotators with poor frontal plane control, which raises patellofemoral stress. Her quadriceps strength and patellar tracking are normal, so those are not the priority. Strengthening the proximal muscles and retraining movement addresses the cause, while bracing and rest do not.
  92. The correct answer is A (Greater charge per phase, recruiting additional motor units and producing a stronger contraction.): Charge per phase is amplitude multiplied by phase duration, so doubling duration at the same amplitude doubles the charge. More charge depolarizes more and deeper nerve fibers and recruits more motor units, strengthening the contraction. Pulse rate is set separately by frequency. Short phase durations favor sensory fibers, so lengthening the phase moves away from sensory-only stimulation.
  93. The correct answer is A (An exposure of 225 seconds at the tested lamp distance): The MED is the shortest patch with minimal visible redness, which is 90 seconds. An E1 dose is 2.5 times that, and 90 times 2.5 equals 225 seconds. Using the 150 second patch ignores the calculation. Five times the MED gives 450 seconds, which is an E2 dose, and doubling gives only 180.
  94. The correct answer is B (Lower intensity gives a larger fat fraction, while higher intensity shifts toward carbohydrate): Fat contributes a larger share of energy at low to moderate intensity, and the share falls as intensity rises while carbohydrate takes over. Matching total energy does not match substrate mix. Catecholamines raise lipolysis at high intensity, but carbohydrate use rises more. Fat is used from the start of exercise, so waiting for depletion is wrong.
  95. The correct answer is B (Seven patterns scored 0 to 3 each, for a maximum composite of 21): The Functional Movement Screen scores seven fundamental patterns from 0 to 3, so the highest composite is 21. Five patterns would give only 15, and a 0 to 5 scale would raise the maximum to 35. Clearing tests record pain only and are not added to the composite.
  96. The correct answer is C (Shoulder flexion, abduction, and external rotation, ending with the arm overhead and out to the side): D2 flexion starts in extension, adduction, and internal rotation and ends in flexion, abduction, and external rotation, so the arm finishes overhead and out to the side. Ending across the face describes D1 flexion, which keeps adduction. Pairing abduction with internal rotation does not match either flexion diagonal. The downward finish is the D1 extension pattern.
  97. The correct answer is C (Graded joint mobilization applied to the glenohumeral joint capsule to restore accessory glides): A firm capsular end feel with reduced accessory glides indicates a joint restriction, so graded mobilization of the capsule is the direct treatment. Muscle stretching techniques act on contractile tissue and are chosen when muscle length tests are abnormal. Here muscle length is normal, so stretching the pectoralis, using bouncing, or using PNF on the subscapularis would not restore the lost glide.
  98. The correct answer is C (Output exceeds this patient's sensory threshold, so amplitude should be lowered until no tingling remains.): Microcurrent therapy is intended to be sub-sensory, so tingling means this patient's threshold is below the delivered amplitude. The clinician should reduce amplitude until the sensation disappears. Tingling is not required to confirm contact, a device does not switch modes on its own, and raising output to 1.2 mA moves beyond the microampere range and increases sensation.
  99. The correct answer is B (720 joules, from 6 W times 0.20 times 600 seconds): Average power is 6 W times 0.20, or 1.2 W, and 10 minutes is 600 seconds, so energy is 1.2 times 600, which is 720 J. Ignoring the duty cycle overstates it at 3,600 J. Check: 720 divided by 600 seconds returns 1.2 W.
  100. The correct answer is D (Defer traction and refer for urgent imaging because the signs suggest cervical cord compression): Upper motor neuron signs such as hyperreflexia and a positive Hoffmann sign, with gait disturbance and clumsiness, indicate cervical myelopathy, and traction could further stress the cord. Imaging and medical review come first. A low force or manual technique still loads the neck, and bilateral findings point to the cord, not away from it.

Practice Test 3 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is D (Withhold her from play, because balance remains worse than her baseline): Her balance score is double her own preseason baseline, which shows persistent postural control impairment even though symptoms have settled at rest. Return to play needs symptoms, balance and graded exertion all back to baseline. A population cutoff does not replace her own baseline, and resting symptom relief alone does not clear her. Scheduling a retest for later still leaves a current deficit unaddressed.
  2. The correct answer is A (A 38-year-old with leg pain, positive straight leg raise, and a disc protrusion on imaging): A radicular presentation with a positive straight leg raise and a posterolateral protrusion is the classic indication for lumbar traction. Severe osteoporosis with a recent compression fracture is a contraindication because of fracture risk. Pregnancy is a contraindication. A pulsatile abdominal mass with constant rest pain suggests an aortic aneurysm and requires medical referral rather than traction.
  3. The correct answer is A (Freezing stage, with escalating pain, night symptoms, and worsening restriction of shoulder motion): The freezing stage is the painful, progressive stage in which pain is present at rest and at night while motion is steadily lost. The frozen stage has less pain but a stable, firm restriction. The thawing stage is marked by motion returning, and a pre-adhesive picture would still show full passive motion, which this patient has lost.
  4. The correct answer is B (10 second contraction, 50 second rest, 3 second ramp, highest tolerable intensity): Strengthening with Russian stimulation uses about 10 seconds of contraction followed by 50 seconds of rest, a 1 to 5 ratio, with a ramp of about 3 seconds. Intensity is raised to the highest tolerable level because torque gains relate to the force of the stimulated contraction. A 60 second contraction with 10 seconds rest would fatigue the muscle quickly. Sensory-level intensity does not produce a training effect.
  5. The correct answer is A (Specificity, because adaptations are largest in the muscles and movement pattern trained): Specificity states that adaptations are greatest in the muscles and movement pattern used in training, so a swimmer tested on a treadmill uses less trained muscle mass and records a lower VO2. She trains six days a week, so reversibility does not apply. Progressive overload concerns raising training stress over time, not test mode. Genetic variability differs between people and does not explain two modes in the same athlete.
  6. The correct answer is D (Abductor force and joint compression needed to level the pelvis): Moving the trunk over the painful hip shifts the body's center of mass toward the joint, which shortens the lever arm the weight has on the hip. The abductors then need less force to hold the pelvis level, and the compressive load on the arthritic joint falls. The lean does not unload the knee, ankle, or hip extensors.
  7. The correct answer is B (3 MHz continuous at about 1.0 W/cm2, with a moving head, followed by stretching): In the chronic stage the goal is to raise collagen extensibility, which needs continuous thermal ultrasound at a frequency matching the shallow depth and a moving head. Stretching follows right away. Pulsed low doses are nonthermal, and 1 MHz held still or followed by ice defeats the goal.
  8. The correct answer is D (At least two to three days per week, with daily stretching giving the greatest gains): Guidelines for healthy adults call for stretching at least two to three days per week, with daily stretching giving the largest gains in range. Frequent but five-second holds are too short to change tissue length. A single weekly session is the pattern that already failed him, since gains fade between sessions. Ballistic bouncing is a different technique and does not give the sustained stretch used for flexibility.
  9. The correct answer is C (Add varied environments and dual tasks, such as outdoor uneven surfaces while carrying objects): A patient who walks accurately with little attention is at or near the autonomous stage. Progress then comes from variability and added attention demands, such as uneven outdoor terrain and carrying objects, which test whether the skill holds under new conditions. Step by step cues and manual guidance suit early learning, and repeating the same hallway walk adds no new challenge.
  10. The correct answer is A (Adaptation of the vestibulo-ocular reflex to reduce retinal image slip): Fixating a target while moving the head creates retinal slip that drives adaptive change in the vestibulo-ocular reflex gain, which improves gaze stability. Habituation uses repeated provocation of a symptom, substitution uses other senses, and canalith repositioning moves loose debris. None of these is the purpose of this drill.
  11. The correct answer is A (Heat followed by sustained stretching into the capsular restriction and joint mobilization to regain motion): In the frozen stage pain is low and stiffness dominates, so heat followed by sustained stretching and mobilization to the restriction is the emphasis. Gentle pain-limited oscillations with ice fit the painful freezing stage instead. Manipulation under anesthesia is reserved for cases that fail a trial of conservative care. Strengthening alone does not release a capsular restriction that blocks external rotation.
  12. The correct answer is A (Contract-relax followed by an agonist contraction, sometimes called contract-relax-agonist-contract): A concentric contraction of the tight muscle against resistance, followed by relaxation, is contract-relax, and the added quadriceps contraction adds the agonist contraction phase that uses reciprocal inhibition. Hold-relax would use an isometric hamstring contraction, and slow reversal hold alternates contractions without a passive stretch. Rhythmic stabilization uses isometric alternating resistance and is not used to take up new range.
  13. The correct answer is B (Cryotherapy, relative rest from impact, and gentle pain free motion with isometric quadriceps work): A warm effused knee in a flare needs cooling, relative rest from impact, and gentle pain free motion with isometrics to keep quadriceps activation. Prolonged rigid bracing promotes stiffness and weakness. Heat and heavy resisted extension aggravate an inflamed joint. Adding stair climbing to the walking program keeps loading a flaring knee, which suits stable osteoarthritis but not this flare.
  14. The correct answer is C (Repeat the MED test, because the drug may have lowered her MED): Tetracycline class antibiotics such as doxycycline are photosensitizers and can lower the MED substantially. The earlier MED no longer describes her skin, so it should be measured again. A guessed reduction might still burn or underdose, and waiting for redness risks an avoidable burn. The drug does not thicken the epidermis or raise tolerance.
  15. The correct answer is D (Peel slowly in the direction of hair growth while holding the skin down): Slow removal, with the tape folded back on itself and parallel to the skin, along the direction of hair growth, while the other hand supports the skin, lowers the chance of skin tears and hair pulling. Fast removal against hair growth and pulling straight up both increase skin trauma. Prolonged hot soaking can macerate the skin.
  16. The correct answer is A (During the heating or immediately after it ends, while collagen extensibility remains high): Heated collagen is more extensible, and the effect fades as the tissue cools, so the stretch is applied during or right after heating. Waiting 30 minutes wastes the effect. Stretching first gives no thermal advantage, and cooling beforehand stiffens the tissue.
  17. The correct answer is D (Idiopathic toe walking, with preserved ankle motion and no neurological signs): A child who can walk with the heels down on request, has normal tone and reflexes, and has full passive dorsiflexion most likely has idiopathic toe walking. Cerebral palsy would show increased tone or hyperreflexia, a contracture would limit passive range, and dystrophy would show proximal weakness.
  18. The correct answer is B (Midline cerebellar involvement producing truncal ataxia and sway): Sway that is equal with eyes open and closed, a wide based gait, and failure on tandem walking point to a cerebellar midline lesion rather than a sensory deficit. Posterior column disease or neuropathy worsens with eye closure. Vestibular loss typically produces unsteadiness mainly when vision and surface cues are removed.
  19. The correct answer is D (Improved motor unit recruitment and coordination, with little change in muscle size so far): During the first several weeks of resistance training, strength rises mainly through neural adaptations such as greater motor unit recruitment, firing rate and coordination, before measurable hypertrophy occurs. This fits a 25 percent increase with unchanged girth. Hyperplasia is minor in humans, and bone and tendon adapt too slowly to account for this change.
  20. The correct answer is B (Skin capacitive reactance falls as frequency rises, so less resistance occurs at 4000 Hz): Skin behaves partly like a capacitor, and its reactance is inversely proportional to frequency. At 4000 Hz the skin offers far less impedance than at 50 Hz, so more current passes with less stinging. Superficial nerves still respond to the beat, so sensation is not abolished. Interferential current is alternating and carries no net direct component.
  21. The correct answer is B (Patient two's target is higher, about 142 versus about 129 beats per minute): Patient one has a reserve of 128, so 60 percent is about 77, plus 52 gives about 129. Patient two has a reserve of 96, so 60 percent is about 58, plus 84 gives about 142. The higher resting rate is added back, which outweighs the smaller reserve. A fixed 108 beats per minute would result only from using percent of maximum, which is not the Karvonen method.
  22. The correct answer is A (Edema control, desensitization, graded motor imagery, and pain-free active motion): Burning pain out of proportion, swelling, skin color and temperature changes, and allodynia after a fracture indicate complex regional pain syndrome. Management combines edema control, desensitization, graded motor imagery, and pain-free active use. Passive stretching and vigorous massage provoke the sensitized limb, and an injection for median nerve entrapment treats a different problem even though carpal tunnel symptoms can follow distal radius fracture.
  23. The correct answer is C (Begin with low intensity bilateral jumps in place and progress to single-leg hops later): Plyometric training progresses from low to high intensity and from bilateral to unilateral, so in place bilateral jumps come first after a tendon flare. Good single-leg squat control does not replace the low load introduction that lets the tendon adapt. Box jumps with early depth work and bounding impose high eccentric load too soon. Frequent games add to the weekly tendon load, which supports a conservative start.
  24. The correct answer is C (290 to 320 nanometers, the band between UVC and UVA): UVB spans about 290 to 320 nanometers and sits between UVC and UVA. This band produces the strongest erythemal response and is the standard range for psoriasis lamps. The 200 to 290 band is UVC, which is germicidal, and 320 to 400 is UVA, which is paired with psoralen. The 400 to 500 band is visible light and is not ultraviolet.
  25. The correct answer is D (A soft accommodative insole with full contact and cushioning to spread plantar pressure): An insensate foot needs a soft, full-contact accommodative insole that spreads pressure over a large area and protects the skin. A rigid orthosis with a strong post concentrates force on skin that cannot report pain. A heavy forefoot post is intended to realign motion and may create pressure points. A raised bar placed under a callus increases pressure at the very site that needs relief.
  26. The correct answer is D (Six to eight layers of dry toweling, with the pack placed over them): A hydrocollator pack straight from the tank is far hotter than skin tolerates, so about six to eight layers of dry toweling provide the insulation. Dry layers trap air and slow conduction, whereas a thin towel would transmit too much heat. Damp toweling conducts heat better than dry toweling and increases burn risk. Plastic wrap adds no meaningful insulation.
  27. The correct answer is B (Controlled mobility, practiced through graded weight shifts within the quadruped posture): She has achieved stability in quadruped and now needs controlled mobility, which is moving within a weight-bearing posture while keeping control. Graded weight shifts train this. More holds repeat a stage she has mastered, and passive range of motion is a regression. Skill tasks in standing are a later step.
  28. The correct answer is D (Lumbar flexion, such as forward leaning or hugging the knees): Flexion increases canal and foramen dimensions and tightens the ligamentum flavum, which relieves pressure on the neural elements. That is why patients with stenosis tolerate sitting and leaning forward and flare with standing, extension, and downhill walking. Extension narrows the canal further. Rotation and side bending do not open the canal in a useful way.
  29. The correct answer is D (Shortened latissimus dorsi, so stretch it while maintaining a flat lumbar spine): A short latissimus dorsi limits overhead reach and pulls the lumbar spine into extension to compensate, and the negative Thomas test rules out the iliopsoas. Stretching it with the lumbar spine held flat isolates the muscle. The rhomboids and serratus anterior do not limit passive overhead reach.
  30. The correct answer is A (Supine strap-assisted hamstring stretch with the opposite leg flat and the lumbar spine neutral): Stretching supine with a strap, the opposite leg flat and the lumbar spine neutral isolates the hamstrings and removes lumbar flexion. Folding the trunk over a raised heel lets the spine round instead of lengthening the muscle. A quadriceps stretch treats a different muscle. A forward reach in long sitting repeats the pattern he already uses, even with contract-relax.
  31. The correct answer is B (The Cooper 12-minute run test, with VO2 max predicted from distance covered): The Cooper test fixes the time at 12 minutes and measures how far the athlete runs, then converts distance to estimated VO2 max. The Rockport test fixes distance at one mile and uses time plus heart rate. The Bruce protocol needs a treadmill and staged increases in workload. The YMCA test uses a cycle ergometer, so none of these fit a track-only setting.
  32. The correct answer is C (Raise the pulse rate to about 35 to 50 pps.): At low rates such as 10 pps, each pulse produces a separate twitch. At about 35 to 50 pps successive twitches summate and fuse into a smooth tetanic contraction, which is needed for strengthening. Lowering the rate separates twitches further. Ramp time and sensory-level amplitude do not change fusion.
  33. The correct answer is C (Between 24 and 72 hours after the bout, commonly around the second day): Delayed onset muscle soreness develops several hours after unaccustomed exercise, peaks between about 24 and 72 hours, and resolves within roughly 5 to 7 days. Pain felt during exercise or in the first hours is a different phenomenon. A peak at one week or later suggests a strain or another problem rather than typical DOMS.
  34. The correct answer is D (Prone with the lumbar spine in a neutral to slightly extended posture): When symptoms centralize with extension and worsen with flexion, traction is best delivered with the lumbar spine in neutral to slight extension, which is done in prone. The supine 90 degree position flattens the lordosis and would likely aggravate her leg pain. Sitting slouched and side-lying flexion also load the spine in the flexed direction she cannot tolerate.
  35. The correct answer is C (Sensory substitution training with gaze stabilization exercises as tolerated): With severe bilateral loss, recovery depends on using remaining senses and any residual vestibulo-ocular function. Substitution training and gaze stabilization are the base of treatment. The Epley maneuver treats debris and habituation addresses positional symptoms. Suppressants with rest delay adaptation.
  36. The correct answer is A (Movement takes the path of least resistance, so the most flexible segment moves first): Sahrmann holds that movement follows the path of least resistance, so the most flexible segment moves first and absorbs repeated stress. Nearness to the force does not set which segment moves. Weakness can contribute to faulty patterns but is not the guiding principle. Lever length affects torque yet does not define the principle of relative flexibility.
  37. The correct answer is A (25 s, found by dividing 200 J by 8 W): Total energy is 10 J/cm2 times 20 cm2, which equals 200 J. Dividing 200 J by 8 W gives 25 s. Ignoring the area gives barely over a second, a decimal slip on power gives ten times too long, and using 5 W does not match the device setting.
  38. The correct answer is B (Reduce the load or volume until symptoms return to baseline within twenty four hours): A tendon loading program is judged by the response over the next 24 hours, and symptoms above baseline the next morning mean the dose was too high. Exercise pain of 3 out of 10 alone does not show that. Plyometric loading is a later stage that adds demand after a flare, and six weeks without loading removes the stimulus the tendon needs.
  39. The correct answer is C (The distal iliotibial band as it glides over the lateral femoral epicondyle): Iliotibial band syndrome produces pain where the distal band passes over the lateral femoral epicondyle, most notably at about 20 to 30 degrees of flexion during running. Downhill running and repeated flexion cycles increase irritation. The popliteus, lateral meniscus, and biceps femoris do not match the epicondyle focus or the absence of swelling.
  40. The correct answer is B (2,500 Hz carrier in 50 bursts per second, 10 ms on and 10 ms off): Russian stimulation uses a 2,500 Hz alternating current interrupted into 50 bursts per second, each lasting 10 ms with a 10 ms gap, a 50 percent burst duty cycle. Each burst therefore holds 25 carrier cycles. The 4,000 Hz carrier belongs to interferential current. Swapping burst rate and duration, or dropping the carrier into the low-frequency range, no longer describes the Russian waveform.
  41. The correct answer is C (Rectus femoris shortening, stretched with the hip extended and the knee flexed): When the thigh stays flat but the knee extends, the rectus femoris is shortened, because it crosses both the hip and the knee. It is lengthened with the hip extended and the knee flexed. Iliopsoas shortening would lift the thigh with the knee flexed. The tensor fasciae latae is stretched with adduction and extension, and the hamstrings are not tested by this finding.
  42. The correct answer is B (Avoid continuous thermal doses over the open growth plate and use a gentle option instead): An open growth plate is a relative precaution for continuous thermal ultrasound, so the clinician should choose a gentle option such as relative rest, activity modification or cooling. Changing frequency or lowering intensity in a continuous mode still heats tissue near the physis. A stationary head concentrates energy and adds hotspot risk. The safest step is to avoid continuous thermal doses over the plate altogether.
  43. The correct answer is C (Loading response, the weight acceptance period at the start of stance): Loading response begins at initial contact and ends when the opposite foot lifts off, about the first 10 percent of the cycle. It is the first double support interval and the period of weight acceptance and shock absorption. Midstance follows as single limb support, terminal stance begins with heel rise, and preswing is the second double support interval.
  44. The correct answer is B (Single-leg bridges while keeping the pelvis level and the trunk still): Removing one foot from the floor doubles the load on the working gluteus maximus and forces the stabilizers to resist rotation and pelvic drop. That meets the goal of greater demand without added weight. Placing the feet far away shifts work to the hamstrings. Straight leg raises and prone extensions load the lumbar spine rather than train the target muscles.
  45. The correct answer is A (Demonstrate the whole movement, give simple cues, and provide frequent specific feedback on each trial): A learner in the cognitive stage needs to understand what the task is, so a demonstration, simple cues and frequent specific feedback help him build the movement plan. Dual task challenges, no feedback and heavy randomization suit later stages when errors are small, intrinsic feedback can be used and attention demand has fallen. The same approach is not the best fit once the movement is learned.
  46. The correct answer is C (Begin with a conservative test dose, check the skin reaction, and then progress): Photosensitizing drugs can raise skin reactivity to visible light as well as ultraviolet, so a cautious first dose with a skin check is the standard approach. The fact that a laser emits no ultraviolet does not remove the risk. The condition is a precaution rather than an absolute contraindication, and a high first dose adds needless risk.
  47. The correct answer is B (136 beats per minute, from the reserve of 110 times 0.60 plus 70): Heart rate reserve is 180 minus 70, or 110. Sixty percent of 110 is 66, and adding the resting rate of 70 gives 136 beats per minute. The value 108 is simple 60 percent of maximum, a different method. The value 66 omits the resting rate, and 178 mixes the two methods and exceeds a sensible target.
  48. The correct answer is A (Negative active electrode, pulse rate about 120 pps, intensity below the motor threshold): Acute edema control uses a negative active electrode, a rate near 120 pps and an intensity below the motor threshold so no contraction increases bleeding or swelling. Motor-level, low-rate stimulation is a pumping method for later, chronic swelling. Positive polarity is not the one chosen to limit protein leakage. Only the stated set keeps both polarity and intensity appropriate for a one-day-old injury.
  49. The correct answer is D (Treat the pelvic and hip muscle imbalance first, since the bone lengths are equal): Equal tape measured lengths mean the bones are the same length, so the low iliac crest probably reflects a functional pelvic or hip asymmetry, and the cause should be treated first. A lift under the left foot would assume a structural shortening that was not found. A lift on the right foot would worsen the asymmetry, and an orthotic does not correct muscular pelvic tilt.
  50. The correct answer is D (Combined end-range abduction and external rotation under load): Abduction with external rotation at end range is the position of anterior dislocation and places the greatest stress on the anterior capsule, so it is delayed. Scaption with the thumb up strengthens the cuff at much lower stress. Prone horizontal abduction and neutral rotation with tubing also load the cuff and scapular muscles without stressing the anterior restraints.
  51. The correct answer is C (Regress to an incline plank with the hands on a bench, keeping the trunk straight): A lumbar sag shows the trunk cannot resist extension at this lever length, so load should be reduced with an incline plank where the hands are on a bench. Adding weight or placing the feet on a ball raises the demand and worsens the fault. Continuing to fatigue rehearses the faulty pattern and builds compensation. Control comes before load.
  52. The correct answer is D (Keep the face flat against the surface, perpendicular to the skin, with ample gel): Sound reflects when it meets skin at an angle or across an air gap, so the face should lie flat and perpendicular with ample gel. Pulsed mode changes the duty cycle but not coupling, so reflection remains. Tilting does not enlarge the field, and pressing an edge in does not drive energy deeper. If flat contact is impossible on a curve, a water path or smaller head is the alternative.
  53. The correct answer is D (Keep the heel down with the toes pointing straight ahead and the arch supported): Pronation and a lifted heel let the stretch escape to the arch, so the correction keeps the heel down, toes straight ahead and arch supported with the knee extended. Bending the knee shifts the stretch to the soleus and the turned-out foot keeps the fault. Lifting the heel removes the load. Rolling inward reinforces the compensation.
  54. The correct answer is C (Falling muscle pH from hydrogen ion accumulation impairs glycolytic enzymes and cross-bridges): Rapid glycolysis releases hydrogen ions along with lactate, lowering muscle pH, which inhibits phosphofructokinase and interferes with calcium handling and cross-bridge cycling. Lactate itself is not the toxic agent. Phosphocreatine is depleted early but glycolysis supplies ATP in this effort, and mitochondria keep producing ATP during the race.
  55. The correct answer is B (Graded return with shorter sessions, plus technique, grip size, and string tension changes): A graded return with shorter sessions lets the tendon adapt, and changes to technique, grip size, and string tension reduce load on the extensor origin. Jumping to full volume risks recurrence. A counterforce brace can offload the origin but does not replace graded exposure, and limiting play to forehand strokes does not rebuild tolerance for all strokes.
  56. The correct answer is A (Superimpose stimulation on a maximal voluntary quadriceps contraction at the highest tolerable intensity.): Adding stimulation on top of a maximal voluntary contraction recruits motor units the patient cannot activate alone, increasing torque and training effect and improving voluntary activation. Voluntary effort adds to the current rather than interfering with it. Sensory-level stimulation adds little force, and alternating separately gives up the combined effect.
  57. The correct answer is A (Loss of pelvis and trunk counter-rotation from axial spine stiffness): Fusion or stiffness of the thoracolumbar spine from ankylosing spondylitis blocks the normal rotation between pelvis and shoulders, which also reduces arm swing. The early morning stiffness, limited chest expansion, and age support this cause. Parkinsonism would add tremor and short steps, and strong legs rule out abductor weakness.
  58. The correct answer is B (A plastic posterior leaf spring orthosis that holds the foot near neutral in swing): A posterior leaf spring orthosis flexes during stance to allow push-off and recoils to hold the foot near neutral during swing, which prevents toe drag from dorsiflexor weakness. A lace-up stabilizer does not lift the foot. A solid orthosis fixed in plantar flexion blocks push-off and does not correct the foot position. A medial wedge addresses pronation, not foot drop.
  59. The correct answer is A (Train the timing of anticipatory activation during limb movements, not only the strength): Hodges and Richardson showed a delay in anticipatory transversus abdominis activation before limb movement in people with low back pain. The deficit is feedforward timing, so training should rehearse timed activation during limb movements. Strength work for the rectus abdominis misses the target muscle, and endurance holds train capacity rather than timing. Stretching addresses length, not the activation delay.
  60. The correct answer is C (At several knee angles within the allowed range, holding each contraction several seconds): Isometric strength increases mostly near the trained angle, so the quadriceps should be trained at multiple angles within the surgeon's allowed range, submaximally and pain free. Using one angle leaves the rest of the range untrained. Full extension does not give universal carryover, and a daily maximal hold at one angle is neither comprehensive nor appropriate for a swollen joint.
  61. The correct answer is C (Check glucose at bedtime and again overnight, and treat or adjust intake as needed): Prolonged exercise depletes muscle and liver glycogen, and the body replenishes it for hours afterward, increasing insulin sensitivity and the risk of hypoglycemia that night. Checking at bedtime and overnight, with carbohydrate or intake adjustments as needed, addresses that risk. Adding insulin would worsen it. A normal reading at the end does not rule out a later drop.
  62. The correct answer is C (About 60 pounds less compressive load across the knee with each step): The estimate is a four pound reduction in knee load per step for each pound lost, so 15 pounds multiplied by 4 equals 60 pounds per step, and a recheck gives 4 times 15 equals 60 again. Over thousands of steps a day this is a large cumulative reduction, which is why weight loss is a core part of knee osteoarthritis care. The other values come from using the wrong multiplier.
  63. The correct answer is D (4.5 watts, found by multiplying 1.5 W/cm2 by the 3 cm2 ERA): Power equals intensity times ERA, so 1.5 W/cm2 times 3 cm2 equals 4.5 W. The current 6 W over 3 cm2 gives 2.0 W/cm2, which is higher than intended. Check: 4.5 W divided by 3 cm2 returns 1.5 W/cm2.
  64. The correct answer is B (Activating glycogenolysis in liver and muscle plus hormone-sensitive lipase in fat): Epinephrine acts directly and quickly by activating glycogenolysis in liver and muscle and hormone-sensitive lipase in fat, raising fuel availability within seconds. Glucagon and cortisol act through other routes or more slowly, and glycogen synthase and lipoprotein lipase favor storage rather than mobilization.
  65. The correct answer is A (Soleus and gastrocnemius contracting eccentrically to slow the advancing tibia): In midstance the tibia advances over the fixed foot while the soleus and gastrocnemius lengthen under tension to decelerate it, which delays heel rise. The tibialis anterior lowers the forefoot earlier, in loading response. Eccentric quadriceps work controls knee flexion at loading, not tibial progression. The gluteus medius does hold the pelvis level in stance, but that is a different task from controlling the tibia.
  66. The correct answer is B (6 J/cm2, from 0.1 W for 60 s over 1 cm2): Energy is 0.1 W times 60 s, which gives 6 J. Dividing 6 J by the 1 cm2 spot gives 6 J/cm2. Reading the output as 1 W inflates the result tenfold, dividing by time gives the wrong operation, and ignoring the 60 seconds leaves only a tiny value.
  67. The correct answer is C (Thermal ultrasound to the posterior ankle followed by sustained low load stretching): With healed bone and no swelling, the limiting factor is shortened collagenous tissue, which stretches best when heated to a deeper level and held under low load right after heating. Thermal ultrasound suits the deep capsule and tendon, and stretching must follow while the tissue is warm. Cold with ballistic stretch, nonthermal ultrasound, and edema stimulation do not match the problem.
  68. The correct answer is B (The tibia of the swinging limb reaches a vertical position): In the Rancho system initial swing runs from toe-off until the feet are side by side, midswing runs from there until the tibia is vertical, and terminal swing runs from vertical tibia until initial contact. Toe-off starts the whole swing phase. Heel contact ends terminal swing and begins the next stance phase.
  69. The correct answer is C (Hacking, in which the ulnar borders of both hands strike the muscle alternately): Tapotement includes hacking, cupping, clapping, beating and pounding, all rhythmic striking strokes. Hacking uses the ulnar borders of the hands alternately. Wringing and skin rolling are petrissage variations, and circular thumb pressure is a friction technique.
  70. The correct answer is A (On intact skin of the same limb, proximal to the wound): The dispersive electrode completes the circuit and belongs on intact skin of the same limb, proximal to the wound, so current passes through the wound region and nearby tissue. A pad on the opposite limb sends current across the trunk, which is undesirable. A pad on the wound edge compromises skin integrity and distribution. A lumbar position also crosses the trunk.
  71. The correct answer is B (Autonomous stage, where the movement is performed with minimal conscious attention): The autonomous stage is marked by automatic, consistent performance with little attention, which matches a patient talking during the task. The cognitive stage shows large errors and heavy attention. The associative stage still needs concentration. A retention test is a concept that checks learning after a delay, not one of the stages.
  72. The correct answer is B (Slowed nerve conduction velocity in sensory fibers, along with gate control inhibition): Cooling slows conduction velocity in sensory nerves and activates cold receptors that help close the spinal gate, which raises pain threshold. Cold lowers rather than raises metabolic rate and conduction speed. Cold also reduces capillary permeability, so washout of mediators by increased permeability does not apply.
  73. The correct answer is B (Creep, the progressive increase in deformation of tissue under a constant applied load): Creep is the time-dependent increase in length of a viscoelastic tissue under a constant load, which is what the hold with a steady strap load produces. Stress relaxation is the related drop in tension when the tissue is held at a fixed length, so it does not fit a constant load with changing length. Hysteresis describes energy lost over a load and unload cycle. Plastic deformation is the permanent change that may result, not the process observed during the hold.
  74. The correct answer is A (Rating of perceived exertion, with blood pressure checks after position changes and at peak effort): Cardiac autonomic neuropathy produces a high, fixed resting heart rate and a blunted response, so heart rate based zones are unreliable. Orthostatic hypotension also makes blood pressure checks around position changes important. Perceived exertion tracks effort independent of heart rate. Oxygen saturation stays normal and does not reflect workload.
  75. The correct answer is D (Cover the probe tip with clear plastic film and hold it just above the wound): A clear barrier film keeps secretions off the tip while passing the red light, and holding the probe close above the wound avoids contact with the bed. Pressing a bare tip into a draining ulcer risks cross contamination. A probe 15 cm away delivers far too little irradiance, and irradiating distant skin does not treat the wound bed.
  76. The correct answer is A (The left shoulder mobility pattern scoring 1 with a side to side asymmetry): The screen guidance is to correct asymmetries and the lowest scores before symmetrical patterns. The shoulder pattern is both the lowest score and asymmetric, so it comes first. The hurdle step is also asymmetric but scores higher, and the squat and lunge are symmetrical.
  77. The correct answer is C (Side-glide the trunk toward the painful right side, then add extension): In McKenzie management a visible lateral shift is corrected first, usually by moving the trunk toward the side of pain and the pelvis away from it, until extension becomes tolerable. Only then do extension exercises become effective. Gliding further into the shift or choosing flexion or rotation stretches does not reduce the deformity.
  78. The correct answer is A (The ball is too small, so a larger ball is chosen to put the thighs level): On a correctly sized ball the hips and knees are near ninety degrees and the thighs are parallel to the floor. Hips below the knees mean the ball is too small, so a larger one is needed. A larger ball would not help if the ball were too large. A poor fit is not a sound way to add difficulty, and inflation does not fix the sizing.
  79. The correct answer is B (One quarter of the original rate, since 20 degrees C equals two halvings): A 20 degree C fall is two 10 degree steps, and each step halves the rate. Rate is therefore 1/2 times 1/2, which equals 1/4 of the original. Applying one halving gives 1/2, and compounding per degree or using a 25 percent change both misuse the Q10 definition.
  80. The correct answer is A (Rate coding, an increase in motor unit firing frequency that raises force through summation): Rate coding is the nervous system's second way to grade force, after recruitment. Increasing the discharge frequency of already active motor units causes twitches to summate toward fused tetanus and raises force. Recruiting more units is spatial summation, synchronization concerns timing between units, and postactivation potentiation is a brief twitch enhancement after prior activity.
  81. The correct answer is A (Stop the walk and seat him to rest, then reassess heart rate and symptoms): Inpatient walking after a myocardial infarction commonly uses a heart rate limit of resting plus about 20 beats. His rise of 28 beats exceeds that limit even though he feels well, so activity should stop and he should rest. Low exertion ratings do not override the heart rate criterion. Continuing, even slowly, or setting a higher ceiling ignores the established guideline.
  82. The correct answer is C (Neutral position, with the ankle at about 90 degrees): Taping with the ankle in neutral, near 90 degrees, supports the joint without limiting motion needed for push-off or taping into stretch. End range dorsiflexion restricts function. Full plantar flexion tensions the anterior talofibular ligament and leaves the ankle in a vulnerable position. Inversion pre-loads the lateral ligaments in the injury position and risks further strain.
  83. The correct answer is A (Channel amplitudes vary automatically, shifting the area of maximal interference across a wider region): Vector scan changes the relative amplitudes of the two channels, which rotates or shifts the orientation of the interference field. The area of maximal modulation therefore moves across a larger region without repositioning pads. Varying the beat frequency is a separate feature called sweep. Interferential current has no polarity, and the carrier is not stepped to change depth.
  84. The correct answer is D (Pelvic belt, load modification, and gentle stabilization exercises): Positive pelvic provocation tests with a normal lumbar examination support pelvic girdle pain, which is treated conservatively with a belt, load management and stabilization. Ultrasound and thrust manipulation are not appropriate in late pregnancy. Lumbar traction targets a spinal problem, not the pelvic joints. Prolonged bed rest worsens deconditioning and does not help.
  85. The correct answer is C (Muscle spindle stretch reflex activation producing reflex contraction in the muscle being lengthened): Rapid, forceful lengthening excites the muscle spindles, and the stretch reflex then contracts the same muscle, which works against lengthening. Golgi tendon organ inhibition needs sustained tension, as in a held contraction or long static hold. Reciprocal inhibition belongs to agonist contraction methods. Creep needs a sustained load over time, so a few quick bounces do not produce it.
  86. The correct answer is C (Knowledge of performance, describing how the trunk and arms moved during the task): Knowledge of performance describes the movement pattern, such as the arm push-off and the trunk lean, and is suited to correcting quality when the goal is already achieved. Knowledge of results reports the outcome and adds nothing here because she succeeds every time. Intrinsic feedback alone has not led her to detect the error.
  87. The correct answer is C (Antalgic gait avoiding heel loading because of plantar heel pain): A short stance, a forefoot landing, and heel pain that peaks with the first steps indicate protective unloading of the painful heel. Passive dorsiflexion of 10 degrees is normal, so a tight gastrocnemius does not explain the forefoot landing. Steppage and calcaneal gaits come from weakness, not pain.
  88. The correct answer is B (Remove the pack, inspect the skin, and resume with added toweling if appropriate): Burning with blotchy redness signals that too much heat is reaching the skin, so the pack is removed and the skin inspected. Treatment resumes only if the skin is acceptable and more insulation is added. Continuing unchanged risks a burn, and putting a cold pack directly on irritated skin adds a second thermal insult.
  89. The correct answer is C (Apply moist heat to rewarm the skin, then take the neck through active motion): After spray and stretch the skin is cool, so moist heat is used to rewarm it, and active range of motion through the new range helps the nervous system accept it. Additional ice, deep friction and rest all prolong cooling or irritate the muscle and do not consolidate the gain in motion. This sequence ends treatment with a gain the patient can use in daily movement.
  90. The correct answer is D (VO2 max begins to decline within a few weeks of stopping training): Aerobic adaptations are reversible, and VO2 max begins to fall within roughly two to four weeks of stopping training, driven first by lower plasma volume and stroke volume. A three month delay does not occur. Plasma volume falls rather than expands with rest. Mitochondrial enzymes do decline early, but VO2 max declines alongside them, so it does not stay constant for months.
  91. The correct answer is C (80 s, found by dividing 8 J by 0.1 W): The protocol needs 8 J/cm2 over 1 cm2, which is 8 J. Dividing 8 J by 0.1 W gives 80 s, so the 10 second exposure delivered only 1 J/cm2. Treating the output as 1 W reproduces the error, halving the energy gives half the dose, and a decimal slip overshoots tenfold.
  92. The correct answer is D (3900 Hz, which is 100 Hz below the channel one carrier): The beat equals the difference between the carriers, so a 100 Hz beat needs the two channels 100 Hz apart. With channel two set below 4000 Hz, the value is 3900 Hz. A setting of 4100 Hz would also give 100 Hz but violates the instruction to set below. A 3990 Hz setting gives only a 10 Hz beat, and equal carriers give zero beat.
  93. The correct answer is B (Sustained standing and slow walking lasting many minutes with low force demands): Small fibers with dense mitochondria, myoglobin, capillaries, and slow ATPase are Type I fibers, suited to fatigue resistant, low force, long duration work such as standing posture and slow walking. Maximal jumps, near maximal squat sets, and thirty second all out intervals recruit larger fast motor units and rely on Type II fibers. Matching the fiber properties to the task demands is the key step.
  94. The correct answer is B (Graded activity and exercise with pain education, using modalities as brief adjuncts): Fear avoidance sustains chronic pain, and graded exposure with education and exercise is the evidence supported response. Passive modalities give short relief and serve only as adjuncts. Rest and bracing reinforce avoidance of movement. Repeat imaging and injection are not indicated for nonspecific pain with benign imaging and normal neurological findings.
  95. The correct answer is B (Left arm forward and right arm back, shoulders turned against the pelvis): At right initial contact the left arm swings forward and the right arm back, while the shoulders rotate opposite to the pelvis. Reversed arms or shoulders turning with the pelvis break reciprocal coordination, and shoulders held square omit the counter-rotation.
  96. The correct answer is D (Elevated fall risk, with walking assisted or aided by a device): On the Berg scale, 41 to 56 suggests independent mobility, 21 to 40 suggests walking with assistance or a device, and 0 to 20 suggests wheelchair mobility. A score of 38 falls in the middle band with elevated fall risk. The other options place the patient in the top or bottom band.
  97. The correct answer is B (Neural adaptation, including improved motor unit recruitment and firing coordination): In the first several weeks of resistance training, strength rises mainly through neural adaptation: more motor units are recruited and they fire more synchronously. Hypertrophy usually becomes the major contributor after about six to eight weeks, so unchanged girth fits a neural cause. Hyperplasia is not an established response, and tendon lengthening does not explain early gains.
  98. The correct answer is C (Inside the unit before delivery, so one circuit carries current already amplitude modulated): In premodulated mode the two carriers are combined inside the stimulator, so a single circuit delivers an already amplitude-modulated current through two electrodes. No crossing paths exist, so no interference occurs in the tissue. The four-pole setup is the technique that depends on tissue interference. Lead wires only conduct the current to the pads.
  99. The correct answer is B (Pulsed at 50 percent duty cycle with a peak of 1.4 W/cm2, averaging 0.7 W/cm2): Average intensity is peak times duty cycle: 3.0 times 0.20 is 0.6, 2.5 times 0.20 is 0.5, 1.4 times 0.50 is 0.7 and the continuous setting is 0.5. The 50 percent setting is highest despite its lower peak. Peak value alone is a poor guide to thermal dose.
  100. The correct answer is A (Effleurage from distal to proximal with the limb elevated above heart level): Light effleurage directed toward the heart with the limb elevated lets gravity and the stroke work together to clear edema without stressing healing ligament. Percussion and deep kneading risk re-injury and bleeding in recently injured tissue. Strokes directed away from the heart push fluid against venous flow.

Practice Test 4 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (Seek emergency evaluation today for a possible pneumothorax on that side): Pleuritic chest pain with dyspnea at rest after needling near the thorax suggests pneumothorax, which needs urgent imaging and emergency evaluation. Ordinary soreness is local and does not cause breathlessness at rest. Waiting two days, returning for gentler needling, or relying on home oximetry all delay care, and oxygen saturation can look normal early in a pneumothorax.
  2. The correct answer is B (Train relaxation by rewarding reduction of the resting signal during sitting tasks): High resting upper trapezius activity marks an overactive muscle, so the best use is downtraining, rewarding a lower resting signal so she learns to release tension during sitting. Uptraining with shrugs suits a weak or poorly recruited muscle, which is not her problem. A loaded carry adds demand to a muscle already working too much. Cueing a signal peak at end range trains recruitment at the limits of motion, a different goal.
  3. The correct answer is A (Refer for vascular assessment with ankle-brachial index testing first): Relief by standing still, weak pulses, cool skin, and hair loss point to peripheral arterial disease rather than spinal stenosis. Neurogenic claudication would ease with sitting or flexion and often vary with spinal posture. The clinician should arrange vascular testing. Spinal programs and modalities do not address arterial insufficiency.
  4. The correct answer is B (A smaller rise in pain threshold than the saline condition would be seen.): Low-rate acupuncture-like TENS is thought to release endogenous opioids such as beta-endorphin, so occupying opioid receptors with naloxone blunts the rise in pain threshold. Gate control does explain high-rate TENS, but it would not be reversed by naloxone, which is why it fails to fit this low-rate protocol. Naloxone is an antagonist, so it cannot sensitize receptors or enhance the effect. It also has no action at the neuromuscular junction, so twitching continues.
  5. The correct answer is D (Peripheral vasodilation that can lower blood pressure and cause lightheadedness or a headache): Nitroglycerin relaxes vascular smooth muscle, mainly venous, which reduces preload and can lower blood pressure. Reflex tachycardia, headache, and orthostatic lightheadedness are common, so pressure should be watched on standing. Slowing the sinus node is the action of beta blockers, not nitrates. The drug does not dilate airways or increase contractility.
  6. The correct answer is B (Avoid cryotherapy and use a nonthermal option such as stretching or taping): Paroxysmal cold hemoglobinuria is a recognized contraindication to cryotherapy, because cold can trigger hemolysis. Shorter or milder cold still delivers the trigger. A nonthermal approach such as stretching or taping treats the heel without cold exposure.
  7. The correct answer is C (Hip abductor and external rotator strengthening in closed chain such as band walks): Medial knee collapse with a contralateral pelvic drop reflects weak hip abductors and external rotators, so they are trained in closed chain to carry over to single-leg tasks. Isolated knee extension and calf work do not address the hip. Strengthening adductors and internal rotators reinforces the faulty alignment.
  8. The correct answer is D (Side-lying and banded exercises for the gluteus medius and gluteus maximus): Dynamic valgus in a squat with weak hip abductors and external rotators calls for strengthening the gluteus medius and maximus. Adductor squeezes build a muscle group that pulls the knee inward and does not address the weak abductors. Decline squats target the quadriceps, which tested normal here. Iliotibial band stretching is a real treatment for tightness, but the finding is weakness that needs loading.
  9. The correct answer is C (Liver glycogen was substantially reduced overnight, limiting glucose release as demand rose): The liver maintains blood glucose overnight by glycogenolysis, so liver glycogen is substantially reduced by morning. During a run, glucose demand rises and the liver may be unable to keep up, which causes weakness and light-headedness. Muscle glycogen is largely preserved, phosphocreatine is not affected by fasting, and fat stores are far from exhausted.
  10. The correct answer is B (Shoulder extension, adduction, and internal rotation, finishing with the arm across the opposite hip): D2 extension is the reverse of D2 flexion, so the arm travels from flexion, abduction, and external rotation into extension, adduction, and internal rotation. The arm ends across the opposite hip. Finishing down and out to the same side is D1 extension. Retaining external rotation or a flexion component would not reverse the starting position.
  11. The correct answer is A (Progressive heavy slow resistance for the cuff and scapular muscles, three sessions weekly): In the remodeling phase tendon adapts to progressively greater slow, heavy loading of the cuff and scapular muscles. Ultrasound with rest offers no loading stimulus. Staying with unchanged isometrics suits an earlier phase, and high speed plyometrics belong later, after a strength base has been rebuilt.
  12. The correct answer is B (Specificity of training, meaning adaptations reflect the demands imposed): Specificity, or the SAID principle, states that the body adapts specifically to the demands placed on it, so training should resemble the speed, contraction type, and pattern of the goal activity. Overload concerns how much stress is applied. Reversibility concerns detraining. Individuality concerns differing responses between people.

The soleus is a single joint muscle and would not limit motion differently with knee position, and toe curls and tibialis stretching do not address the finding.

Crouch gait is common in diplegia but shows persistent flexion in stance rather than crossing limbs.

  31. The correct answer is A (Cardiac output multiplied by the arteriovenous oxygen difference across the working tissues): The Fick equation states that oxygen uptake equals cardiac output times the arteriovenous oxygen difference. It combines delivery of blood with the extraction of oxygen by the tissues. Stroke volume times heart rate only gives cardiac output. Ventilation times the oxygen fraction difference is how gas analyzers calculate VO2 in practice, but that is a different method, and heart rate times arterial content leaves out extraction.
  32.   33. The correct answer is B (Reciprocal inhibition, in which agonist activation reduces antagonist motor neuron excitability): Reciprocal inhibition is the spinal reflex in which activation of an agonist, through Ia inhibitory interneurons, reduces activation of its antagonist. Autogenic inhibition acts on the same muscle after Golgi tendon organ firing, and recurrent inhibition is Renshaw cell feedback within a motor pool. Temporal summation concerns adding twitches to raise tension and does not describe antagonist relaxation.

Pulse rates are tens to a hundred or so per second, not above 10,000 Hz.

Returning to 25 degrees with more force ignores the response. Greater flexion is the direction that already aggravated symptoms. Extension narrows the foramina and can worsen radicular pain.
  90. The correct answer is C (Raised insulin suppresses lipolysis and promotes glucose uptake, shifting fuel use to carbohydrate): Carbohydrate intake shortly before exercise raises insulin, which inhibits hormone-sensitive lipase and lipolysis and promotes glucose uptake, so the respiratory exchange ratio rises toward 1.0. Epinephrine and growth hormone favor fat mobilization and would lower the ratio. Cortisol does not block fatty acid entry into mitochondria.
  91. The correct answer is C (Defer electrical treatment and refer back to the physician for the cardiac failure): Bilateral pitting edema in congestive heart failure is a systemic problem, and local electrical treatment does not address its cause. Pumping fluid toward a failing heart can also worsen cardiac load. The appropriate step is to defer treatment and refer the patient to the physician for medical management.
  92. The correct answer is B (The lactate threshold shifted to a higher percentage of her VO2 max): Lower lactate at the same absolute speed means the curve moved right, so the threshold now occurs at a higher fraction of VO2 max and a higher running speed. This is a common endurance adaptation and can occur with only a small rise in VO2 max. A leftward shift would raise lactate, a falling VO2 max would not lower lactate in this pattern, and worse clearance would raise it.
  93. The correct answer is C (Hemoglobin and melanin absorb weakly above 600 nm while water absorbs weakly below 1100 nm): Hemoglobin and melanin absorb strongly in the blue and green range, and water absorption rises steeply beyond about 1100 nm. Between these limits absorption is low enough that light can travel several millimeters or more. Photon energy actually rises as wavelength shortens, and ultraviolet and blue light are absorbed in the superficial skin layers.
  94. The correct answer is A (Terminal stance, which ends when the right foot makes contact): Terminal stance begins with heel rise and ends when the opposite foot contacts the floor. The raised heel excludes midstance, and the right foot still in the air means that double support has not begun, which excludes preswing. Loading response is the first part of stance.
  95. The correct answer is D (The right foot touches the floor for balance and carries almost no body weight): Touchdown, or toe touch, weight bearing lets the foot contact the floor for balance and proprioception while the limb supports almost no load. Partial weight bearing allows a defined percentage of body weight, non weight bearing allows no floor contact, and weight bearing as tolerated allows load up to pain limits. The fixation needs protection from axial load while early healing occurs.
  96. The correct answer is B (Walk 3 meters, turn, return, and sit; total time is recorded): The Timed Up and Go measures the seconds needed to rise from a chair, walk 3 meters, turn, walk back, and sit down again. Doubling the distance or counting steps changes the validated procedure and the cutoff values no longer apply. A straight 10 meter walk with speed in meters per second is a separate gait speed test.
  97. The correct answer is C (Sensory-level cathodal current with the ankle immersed in cool water): At 18 hours the injury is acute, so the goal is to limit swelling with sensory-level cathodal current and cool immersion. Motor-level pumping and warm water increase blood flow and swelling risk. Anodal current is not the polarity chosen to limit protein leakage in acute edema.
  98. The correct answer is B (The common peroneal nerve, where it wraps around the fibular head): The common peroneal nerve lies superficially at the fibular head, so prolonged cooling with compression can cause neurapraxia with foot drop and dorsal foot numbness. The tibial nerve lies deep in the popliteal fossa, the femoral nerve is in the groin, and the saphenous nerve runs medially. None of those sit under a lateral knee ice bag.
  99. The correct answer is A (Brace the abdomen to hold the pelvis flat and flex the knee only to first tension): Lumbar arching and hip lift show the pelvis is tilting, which takes tension off the rectus femoris, so the abdominals hold the pelvis flat while the knee flexes only to first tension. Pulling harder with a strap increases the compensation. Accepting the arch wastes the stretch. A hip flexed to 90 degrees slackens the rectus femoris, which crosses the hip.
  100. The correct answer is B (A medial longitudinal arch support with a medial rearfoot post): A flexible flat foot with excessive rearfoot eversion and a navicular drop responds to an arch support that raises the medial longitudinal arch together with a medial rearfoot post that limits calcaneal eversion. A lateral heel wedge shifts load the wrong way, cushioning absorbs impact but does not control pronation, and a rigid lateral forefoot post suits a rigid cavus foot.

Practice Test 5 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is A (It raises intra-abdominal pressure and gives a tactile reminder about trunk posture and motion): A flexible corset compresses the abdomen to raise intra-abdominal pressure and provides skin contact that cues the wearer about posture and gross motion. It does not hold individual lumbar segments rigid, and measured segmental motion is only modestly reduced. It does not lengthen the erector spinae or fascia. It also does not strengthen the trunk, and prolonged use can contribute to deconditioning.
  2. The correct answer is B (Gently draw the lower abdomen up and in toward the spine without moving the pelvis): The drawing-in maneuver is a low effort, gentle inward and upward draw of the lower abdomen that selectively recruits the transversus abdominis while the pelvis and spine stay still. Maximal effort and breath holding recruit the superficial muscles and rib cage instead. A brace is a global co-contraction, which is a different strategy. A posterior pelvic tilt is a movement of the pelvis, not an isolated deep contraction.
  3. The correct answer is D (Slow eccentric wrist extension with a light weight or flexible bar, progressively loaded): Slow, progressively loaded eccentric wrist extension is the best supported exercise for chronic lateral epicondylar tendinopathy. Stretching alone does not load the tendon. Flexor work targets the wrong muscles, and maximal rapid curls to fatigue overload an irritable tendon.
  4. The correct answer is C (Withhold TENS and refer him for medical evaluation until a diagnosis explains the pain.): Unexplained, worsening abdominal and flank pain may signal visceral disease, and TENS can dampen a warning symptom and delay diagnosis. Withholding it and referring is correct. Paraspinal placement still reduces perceived pain from the same problem. Low-intensity sensory settings are less risky electrically but do not change the diagnostic concern. Treating once before referral postpones evaluation.
  5. The correct answer is A (Reduce the workload to a perceived exertion near 12 to 13 despite the low heart rate): The beta blocker holds his heart rate down, so the heart rate zone no longer reflects real effort. His rating of 16 means he is working hard, above the usual 12 to 13 target, so workload should come down. Raising resistance to hit the zone would overload him. A heart rate of 92 on a beta blocker is expected and not a reason for an urgent ECG.
  6. The correct answer is A (Roughly 100 to 120 steps per minute for a healthy adult walking freely): Cadence is the number of steps per minute, and healthy adults walking at a comfortable self-selected pace average about 100 to 120. Values near 60 to 80 appear in frail or cautious walkers. Rates around 85 to 95 are seen with shortened strides but fall below the usual adult norm. Cadences of 130 to 150 belong to very fast walking or jogging.
  7. The correct answer is A (Reduced muscle spindle sensitivity, giving a short window of lower spasticity): Cooling lowers muscle spindle sensitivity and slows afferent conduction, giving a short window of reduced spasticity in which stretching and reaching can be practiced. Collagen warming and extensibility belong to heat, not cold. The effect lasts minutes rather than resetting motor neuron excitability, and cold slows conduction instead of speeding it.
  8. The correct answer is B (Hold a slow sustained stretch for about 30 seconds so spindle firing adapts and fades): Rapid bouncing stretches the muscle quickly, so the spindle fires and the calf contracts reflexively, which feels like tightness. A slow sustained hold lets spindle firing adapt and fade, allowing the muscle to lengthen. A contraction beforehand is a real facilitation method but does not make ballistic bouncing safe. Warming helps tissue extensibility yet does not remove the reflex, and smaller faster bounces still stimulate spindles.
  9. The correct answer is C (Eccentric contraction, in which the muscle lengthens while tension can exceed concentric maximum force): Controlling the descent of a load means the biceps produces tension while being lengthened, which is an eccentric action. Eccentric force capacity is higher than concentric, which is why eccentric work is efficient for loading tissue. A concentric action shortens the muscle. An isometric action has no change in length, so the box could not be moving.
  10. The correct answer is D (Horizontal canal repositioning such as the barbecue roll): A negative Dix-Hallpike with a positive roll test showing horizontal geotropic nystagmus indicates horizontal canal BPPV. The barbecue roll or similar maneuver moves the debris out of that canal. The Epley maneuver targets the posterior canal, and exercises or suppressant medication do not remove the debris.
  11. The correct answer is A (Radiculopathy is highly likely, so begin traction, deep neck flexor work and neural mobilization): Rotation under 60 degrees toward the involved side, a positive tension test, positive Spurling and symptom relief with distraction form the cluster that makes radiculopathy highly likely. Conservative care with traction, deep neck flexor training and nerve mobility is appropriate when neurological signs are stable. Myelopathy would show upper motor neuron findings, which are absent.
  12. The correct answer is D (Hold each stretch about 30 seconds and repeat two to four times per muscle group): Exercise guidelines for healthy adults advise holding static stretches roughly 15 to 30 seconds, with about 30 seconds common, repeated two to four times per muscle group. Very short or bouncing holds do not allow enough creep and stress relaxation to build range. Ten-second holds are closer to guidelines but still fall short of the accepted dose. Sixty-second holds repeated many times add time and fatigue without extra benefit.
  13. The correct answer is A (Goggles are required, since eyelids still transmit near-infrared light to the retina): Eyelids transmit a meaningful fraction of near-infrared light, so closing them leaves the retina exposed. Treatment near the face makes scattered light likely whatever the probe position. No distance or power rule waives eyewear for a class 3B device, and cloth is not a rated barrier.
  14. The correct answer is B (Urgent referral for chest imaging and evaluation of a possible apical lung tumor): Shoulder and medial arm pain with C8-T1 deficits, Horner syndrome and weight loss in a smoker suggest a Pancoast tumor invading the lower brachial plexus and sympathetic chain. This is a red flag requiring urgent chest imaging, not a trial of cervical or shoulder rehabilitation. Delay worsens prognosis.
  15. The correct answer is C (Do not apply ultrasound over the area, and use another modality or refer): Ultrasound over known malignancy is contraindicated because heating and cavitation may stimulate tumor growth or spread and may mask worsening disease. Changing frequency or duty cycle does not remove the concern. Pain relief here belongs to the oncology team or a modality not directed over the lesion.
  16. The correct answer is C (Circumduction, an outward curved path of the swing limb): Circumduction is an outward arc of the swing leg used when knee flexion and foot clearance are inadequate, as in hemiparesis. Hip hiking lifts the pelvis instead of swinging the leg out, and vaulting raises the body on the stance limb. Steppage is a high-lift pattern for foot drop, not an outward curved path.
  17. The correct answer is C (Double-leg floor stance, then single-leg floor stance, single-leg foam stance, finally wobble board with eyes closed): Balance training advances by narrowing the base, reducing surface stability and then removing vision, one demand at a time. The correct order starts with the widest, firmest stance and ends with the unstable board under eyes-closed conditions. Starting single-leg skips the basic double-leg stage. Using foam before single-leg floor stance adds a surface change before the base is narrowed. Placing the eyes-closed board before foam makes the last task easier than the one before it.
  18. The correct answer is D (Gluteus maximus and the contralateral latissimus dorsi, linked by the thoracolumbar fascia): The posterior oblique sling crosses the sacroiliac region by linking the gluteus maximus on one side with the opposite latissimus dorsi through the thoracolumbar fascia. Its tension compresses the joint during gait. The same muscles on one side do not form a crossing sling. External oblique with the opposite adductors forms the anterior oblique sling.
  19.
