The correct answer is B (Petrissage, a kneading stroke that lifts, rolls and squeezes the muscle belly between the hands): Lifting, rolling and squeezing the muscle belly between the hands is the pattern of petrissage. Effleurage glides over the surface without lifting tissue, and friction works small focal areas deeply. Tapotement uses rhythmic percussion rather than compression. Each is a real stroke, but only kneading matches this description.
  20. The correct answer is B (Half-kneeling with the pelvis tilted posteriorly while shifting weight forward): Stretching the iliopsoas requires the pelvis to stay posteriorly tilted while the hip moves toward extension, which a half-kneeling position with weight shift achieves. Leaning back lets the lumbar spine extend and hides the shortness. Knee-pulled-behind quadriceps stretching mainly loads the rectus femoris, and double knee to chest stretches the lumbar extensors rather than the hip flexors.
  21. The correct answer is A (Reposition the pairs diagonally so both current paths cross over the hip joint): In a four-pole setup the interference occurs only where the two circuits cross, so the crossing point must lie on the painful joint. Parallel pairs never cross, so the patient feels only the surface. Premodulated mode would give a modulated current but no tissue crossing. A higher carrier does not move the crossing, and a higher intensity increases discomfort under the pads.
  22. The correct answer is D (3.5 milliliters of oxygen per kilogram of body weight per minute at quiet rest): One MET is the resting oxygen uptake, defined as 3.5 mL per kg per minute. Dividing an exercise oxygen uptake by 3.5 gives the workload in METs. The value is scaled to body mass, so an absolute liters-per-minute figure does not define it. A reading of 35 is ten times too high and 7.0 is the cost of roughly two METs.
  23. The correct answer is D (The higher-output 810 nm probe, giving more surface energy so enough remains at depth): Only a small fraction of surface energy reaches 5 cm, so a deep target needs a near-infrared wavelength with low absorption and enough output to leave a useful dose at depth. The 810 nm probe at higher output does both. Red light is absorbed strongly by superficial pigment and blood, so little reaches the nerve. Shorter wavelengths scatter more, not less. Lowering output reduces what remains at depth.
  24. The correct answer is D (A contoured insert with medial arch support and a cushioned heel cup): Plantar fasciitis in a flexible pronated foot responds to a contoured insert with medial arch support and a cushioned heel cup that offloads the fascia and heel. A plain heel lift with no arch contour leaves the fascia strained. A rigid rocker plate suits great toe stiffness. A lateral wedge shifts load away from the medial side and does not support the arch.
  25. The correct answer is B (Alpha gamma coactivation, in which gamma motor neurons shorten the intrafusal fibers during contraction): When alpha motor neurons contract the extrafusal fibers, gamma motor neurons are activated at the same time and shorten the polar ends of the intrafusal fibers. This keeps the central sensory region taut so the spindle continues to report length. The Golgi tendon organ does not act on intrafusal fibers, and blocking afferent input would silence the spindle rather than preserve it.
  26. The correct answer is C (Twenty seconds each, with at most 10 errors per trial): Each BESS trial lasts 20 seconds with the eyes closed and the hands on the iliac crests, and the examiner counts errors up to a maximum of 10 for that trial. Three stances on two surfaces give six trials and a possible total of 60 errors. The 30 second duration and the lower error ceilings do not belong to this test.
  27. The correct answer is C (Shortened hamstrings limit anterior pelvic rotation, so lengthen them and train hip hinging): Short hamstrings restrict anterior pelvic rotation, so the lumbar spine supplies the motion instead. Lengthening the hamstrings and retraining hip flexion addresses the cause. The hip flexor explanation does not fit a limited pelvic tilt, and the erector spinae are not blocking lumbar flexion since the lumbar spine already flexes freely.
  28. The correct answer is B (High rate near 100 Hz, pulse near 100 microseconds, comfortable tingling intensity): Conventional TENS uses high frequency of about 80 to 120 Hz, short pulse duration and intensity that produces tingling without contraction, targeting the gate control mechanism. The low rate with twitch describes acupuncture-like TENS. Strong contraction and carrier-frequency beats describe motor level stimulation and interferential current.
  29. The correct answer is B (Decline passive dorsiflexion stretching and teach protected motion within the surgeon's limits): A fresh tendon repair needs protected range, and stretching into dorsiflexion beyond neutral can elongate or rupture the repair. The therapist follows the surgeon's protocol and teaches motion and positioning within the allowed range. Gentle stretching, half-effort PNF, and heel drops all lengthen the repaired tendon too early. Night tightness is addressed by positioning and the allowed exercises.
  30. The correct answer is A (Ultrasound to the wrist is acceptable, because the pregnancy contraindication concerns fields over the uterus): The concern in pregnancy is exposing the fetus, so the contraindication is for fields over the gravid uterus, including the abdomen and low back and pelvis. A wrist field is far from the fetus. Frequency does not change the distance to the uterus and no gestational cutoff applies.
  31. The correct answer is B (It imitates the natural current of injury and encourages repair cells to migrate into the wound.): Injured skin generates an endogenous electrical current, and microcurrent is thought to mimic it, supporting migration of cells involved in repair toward the wound. Sub-sensory amplitude is too low to cause muscle pumping or measurable heating. Pain gating is the rationale for TENS, not for wound-healing microcurrent.
  32. The correct answer is C (Sustained 20 to 30 minute tempo efforts near the current threshold pace): To raise lactate threshold as a share of VO2max, the best stimulus is sustained work at or just below current threshold pace, such as 20 to 30 minute tempo efforts. All out sprints train anaerobic capacity, and easy long rides build aerobic base with little threshold stress. Four minute efforts at VO2max pace mainly raise maximal oxygen uptake, which is a different adaptation from the fraction of it she can sustain.
  33. The correct answer is D (Ingest 15 to 30 grams of carbohydrate, then recheck glucose before starting): For a person on insulin, a pre-exercise glucose under about 100 mg/dL leaves little margin, because exercise adds insulin-independent glucose uptake. Taking 15 to 30 grams of carbohydrate and rechecking lowers hypoglycemia risk. Starting without fuel, adding insulin, or waiting for symptoms all invite a low during or after the walk.
  34. The correct answer is B (The left to right ratio is about 0.67, indicating a left lateral core deficit): Sixty divided by ninety is 0.67, which means the left side lasted about two thirds as long as the right, a difference near 33 percent. That far exceeds the 5 percent guideline and identifies the weaker side as the left. The right side is the stronger side and is not the deficit.
  35. The correct answer is B (Stop treatment and arrange emergency evaluation for a possible subarachnoid hemorrhage): An abrupt worst-of-life headache with neck stiffness on flexion is a thunderclap pattern concerning for subarachnoid hemorrhage and demands stopping care and emergency evaluation. A referral within days is too slow, ligament testing does not address it, and managing it as migraine or his usual headache risks missing a life-threatening bleed.
  36. The correct answer is D (187 lb, found by multiplying the 220 lb maximum by 0.85): The load equals the 1RM times the target fraction, so 220 times 0.85 equals 187 lb. Multiplying 220 by 0.70 gives 154 lb, which uses the wrong percentage. Taking fifteen percent gives 33 lb, the complement of the intended load. Multiplying by 0.90 gives 198 lb, which exceeds the prescribed intensity. Checking again, 220 times 0.85 is 187 lb.
  37. The correct answer is C (Defer ultraviolet treatment and refer him back to the dermatologist for clearance): A history of skin cancer is a recognized contraindication to ultraviolet therapy because ultraviolet radiation is carcinogenic. The therapist should hold treatment and ask the dermatologist to clear it or choose another approach. Lower doses, a different band or shielding the scar do not remove the added carcinogenic risk. Treatment should not begin before the physician has weighed in.
  38. The correct answer is A (Three errors, one for each separate error committed during the trial): In the BESS each separate error such as stepping, opening the eyes, or lifting the forefoot or heel is counted, up to 10 per trial. This trial contains three distinct errors, so three are scored. Eye opening is a scored error, so a count of two is wrong. The maximum of 10 is a ceiling for many errors, not an automatic penalty for three, and later errors are not ignored.
  39. The correct answer is D (Functional brace, intended to support a ligament deficient or reconstructed knee during sport): A functional brace is used by athletes with an injured or reconstructed ligament to add support while they return to sport. Prophylactic braces are for uninjured knees. Rehabilitative braces with range of motion stops protect a repair in the first weeks, which does not fit a patient 14 months out with full motion. Unloader braces treat compartment osteoarthritis rather than cruciate ligament deficiency.
  40. The correct answer is D (Stop the current, inspect the skin, and resume at a lower amplitude only if comfortable.): Burning under a direct current electrode signals a developing chemical skin reaction, so the current should be stopped and the skin inspected. Treatment can resume at a lower amplitude only if the skin is intact and the patient is comfortable. Mild redness alone can be expected, but burning is a reason to stop. Raising current worsens the injury, and reversing polarity changes which pad is the acid or alkaline site without removing the risk.
  41. The correct answer is C (Refer for imaging and orthopedic evaluation for suspected full-thickness rotator cuff tear): A traumatic onset, inability to hold abduction, and a positive drop arm test with preserved passive motion point to a full-thickness cuff tear that needs imaging and surgical opinion. Resisted work could aggravate it. Passive range is not restricted, so adhesive capsulitis does not fit, and waiting a month delays care.
  42. The correct answer is B (Use submaximal isometric contractions with continuous breathing and no Valsalva maneuver): Maximal isometric contractions with breath holding produce a Valsalva effect and a sharp pressor response, so the fix is submaximal contractions with continuous breathing. Cueing exhalation helps, but maximal effort still raises pressure considerably. Monitoring before each repetition records the problem without removing its cause. Ballistic bouncing avoids the contraction but adds strain risk, and her resting 138/86 mmHg does not call for stopping stretching.
  43. The correct answer is A (Unit A, because a lower ratio means peak intensity sits closer to the average intensity): Beam nonuniformity ratio is the spatial peak intensity divided by the spatial average intensity. A ratio near 1:1 means a uniform beam, so Unit A at 2:1 is safer. Unit B at 7:1 has peaks seven times the average, creating hot spots even when the displayed output looks modest. Equal average output does not mean equal distribution. A larger ratio does not mean a bigger or cooler beam.
  44. The correct answer is B (Increased motor unit synchronization, which can improve force production and rate of force development): The recordings show motor units firing closer together in time, which is synchronization. Trained lifters are thought to show more of it, and it may help force production and rate of force development independent of muscle size. The equal cross sectional area rules out hypertrophy. Training generally raises, not lowers, firing rates, and spindle afferents excite rather than inhibit the agonist.
  45. The correct answer is A (It absorbs impact, with eccentric quadriceps work in early stance): Knee flexion of about 15 degrees in loading response absorbs the shock of heel contact, and the quadriceps lengthen under load, an eccentric action. Foot clearance happens in swing, not stance. Concentric hamstring work would not resist the knee bending under body weight. The tibialis anterior does act eccentrically to lower the forefoot, but that controls the ankle and not what knee flexion accomplishes.
  46. The correct answer is B (Stop the session, have him sit, and notify the physician before any further exercise): Three or more consecutive ventricular beats at a rapid rate is nonsustained ventricular tachycardia, a recognized reason to stop exercise and notify the physician. Absence of symptoms and spontaneous return to sinus rhythm do not make it safe to continue. Reducing the workload or deferring the issue risks sustained arrhythmia. Further exercise waits for medical review.
  47. The correct answer is A (Peak voltage above about 150 V, but average current stays low as pulses are brief): High voltage pulsed current is defined by a peak voltage above roughly 150 V. The pulses last only microseconds with long gaps between them, so average current stays low, usually well under a few milliamperes. That low average current makes the current comfortable and carries little risk of chemical skin damage. A low peak voltage does not define this waveform, and high average current describes continuous galvanic output.
  48. The correct answer is B (Type III collagen is progressively replaced by stronger type I collagen aligned along lines of stress): Remodeling is marked by replacement of the early type III collagen with stronger type I collagen, and by reorientation of fibers along the lines of applied stress. Controlled loading therefore guides scar quality. The clot and macrophage events belong to inflammation, and fibroblast proliferation with capillary budding belongs to the proliferative phase.
  49. The correct answer is C (An 810 nm near-infrared probe applied perpendicular to the lateral hip skin): A target 3 to 4 cm deep needs a wavelength inside the optical window where absorption is lowest, which favors near-infrared such as 810 nm. Red at 660 nm is absorbed more in superficial layers and suits wounds. Green and violet light are absorbed strongly by hemoglobin and melanin and barely get past the skin.
  50. The correct answer is A (Neck flexed, side bent to the left, and rotated to the right): To stretch a muscle, the position must oppose all of its actions. The right upper trapezius extends, side bends right, and rotates left, so stretch is created by flexion, side bending left, and rotation right. Positions that extend the neck or bend toward the tight side bring the attachments closer together. Rotating left while bending left leaves rotation in the muscle's own direction and reduces the stretch.
  51. The correct answer is C (It restricts motion very little, serving mainly as a warmth and reminder cue): Studies of cervical orthoses show a soft foam collar permits most of the normal range of motion, so its main value is warmth, comfort, and a reminder to move less. A Philadelphia collar restricts flexion and extension noticeably more. A soft collar does not meaningfully unload the discs, and it cannot protect an unstable fracture, which needs a rigid device.
  52. The correct answer is B (20 percent, calculated as on-time divided by the sum of on-time and off-time): Duty cycle is the percentage of the pulse period during which sound is emitted. Here 2 ms divided by the 10 ms total (2 on plus 8 off) gives 0.20, or 20 percent. Dividing by the off-time alone gives 25 percent, and using the silent portion gives 80 percent.
  53. The correct answer is C (Standing on a thick foam pad with eyes closed and head still): Foam degrades ankle and foot input and closing the eyes removes vision, so the vestibular system becomes the main reliable source. On a firm floor with eyes closed the somatosensory system still supplies accurate information. Eyes-open conditions leave vision available to compensate.
  54. The correct answer is B (Depletion of muscle and liver glycogen stores limiting carbohydrate oxidation): At about 75 percent of VO2max, glycogen is a major fuel, and stores typically run low after roughly 90 to 150 minutes, so carbohydrate oxidation falls and pace collapses. Phosphocreatine is depleted within the first minute of hard effort and recovers quickly. Lactate remains steady at a sustainable pace, and acetylcholine supply does not fail in this setting.
  55. The correct answer is D (A hot moist pack with towel layers applied for 20 minutes to the lumbar muscles): In the subacute stage, moist heat with several towel layers for about 20 minutes increases superficial blood flow and reduces guarding. Hydrocollator units are kept near 160 to 165 degrees F, and toweling prevents burns. Prolonged bare skin ice stiffens muscle and risks injury. Cold spray is a different technique and does not provide the heating effect wanted.
  56. The correct answer is C (Strengthening the serratus anterior and lower trapezius as scapular upward rotators): Relief when the scapula is assisted into upward rotation points to deficient scapular upward rotation, driven by the serratus anterior and the upper and lower trapezius. Training the serratus and lower trapezius addresses it. Capsule stretching does not explain the relief with scapular assist. Cuff strengthening and pectoralis minor stretching help in some shoulders but leave the weak upward rotators untrained.
  57. The correct answer is A (Use a split table so the lower section glides with the patient): Much of the applied pull is used up by friction between the lower body and the table, so a table split into sections that glide on rollers lets more force reach the spine. Raising the head end changes gravity components but not table friction. A lumbar roll alters lordosis and does not reduce friction. A tighter pelvic belt improves grip but leaves table friction unchanged.
  58. The correct answer is D (Two-pole premodulated mode, with both electrodes on one circuit near the epicondyle): Premodulated mode needs just one circuit and two electrodes because the beat is created inside the unit. That fits a small area such as the lateral elbow. A four-pole setup requires two independent pairs, and vector scan requires four poles as well. Electrodes cannot be shared between channels.
  59. The correct answer is C (Each stage lasts three minutes, then both speed and grade increase): The Bruce protocol uses three minute stages, and each new stage raises both speed and incline, which makes the workload jump relatively steeply. One minute and two minute stages belong to other ramp or modified protocols. Five minute stages resemble steady state cycle tests. Changing only speed or only grade does not describe the Bruce design.
  60. The correct answer is A (Near resting length, where actin and myosin filament overlap is optimal): Active tension is greatest near resting length because the thick and thin filaments overlap so the most cross bridges can form. When fully stretched the overlap is poor, and passive elastic tension is not active force. When very short, filaments collide and tension falls. Both extremes reduce active force.
  61. The correct answer is C (Right hip flexor tightness limiting hip extension in terminal stance): A positive Thomas test shows a shortened hip flexor group, which stops the thigh from extending behind the trunk in terminal stance, so the pelvis tilts forward, the lordosis increases, and the opposite step shortens. Gastrocnemius or hamstring tightness would not lift the thigh in the Thomas test. Gluteus maximus weakness causes a trunk thrust.
  62. The correct answer is A (Immerse hand and head in degassed water, holding the head a short distance from skin): In the immersion method the water is the coupling medium, so the head stays 1 to 2 cm from the skin, perpendicular to the surface. Degassed water is used to avoid bubbles that scatter the beam. The head should not press on the part, and tilting it causes reflection.
  63. The correct answer is A (Insulin falls while glucagon rises, favoring liver glucose release): During sustained exercise insulin secretion is suppressed while glucagon, epinephrine and cortisol rise, which increases liver glycogenolysis, gluconeogenesis and lipolysis. This keeps blood glucose stable while muscle uptake rises through contraction-driven pathways. The fed-state pattern and the both-fall or both-rise patterns do not match the physiologic response.
  64. The correct answer is D (The Epley maneuver, moving displaced debris out of the posterior semicircular canal): Brief positional vertigo with upbeating torsional nystagmus on Dix-Hallpike points to debris in the posterior canal, and the Epley canalith repositioning maneuver is the matching treatment. The log roll is for horizontal canal involvement. Gaze stabilization and Cawthorne style exercises target unilateral or bilateral vestibular hypofunction, not loose debris.
  65. The correct answer is D (Pain-free full motion, strength near that of the opposite arm, and a normal scapular pattern): Interval throwing should start only when range is full and pain free, strength nears the other arm and scapular control is normal. A normal shoulder at rest and a nontender tendon are findings he already has, so they do not show readiness for load. Velocity near preinjury level is the end point of the throwing program, not a starting condition. Deferring strength testing risks loading a weak cuff and scapula.
  66. The correct answer is C (UV-opaque goggles that fit snugly over the eyes of the patient): Ultraviolet radiation reaches the cornea even through closed eyelids, so goggles made of UV-opaque material are required. Tinted sunglasses are designed for visible light and may transmit ultraviolet radiation. Wet pads and a loose towel can shift and leave gaps. Goggles that fit snugly give a dependable barrier.
  67. The correct answer is D (A 20 mmHg baseline, raised in 2 mmHg steps to a 30 mmHg target): The test inflates the cuff to 20 mmHg and asks the patient to nod to 22, 24, 26, 28 and 30 mmHg while holding each stage for 10 seconds. The target range reflects the small pressure change produced by the deep neck flexors without superficial muscle substitution. The 70 mmHg value belongs to lumbar stabilizer testing, and starting at 10 mmHg or lowering the pressure misdescribes the staged increase.
  68. The correct answer is D (Short right iliopsoas, with additional rectus femoris tightness): A thigh that cannot rest on the table shows limited hip extension, which implicates the iliopsoas. Knee extension as the thigh is lowered adds the two-joint rectus femoris. The hamstrings do not limit hip extension in this position, and both findings are on the tested right side, not the held left.
  69. The correct answer is B (D1 extension, because it combines hip extension, abduction, and internal rotation): D1 extension reverses D1 flexion, so the hip moves from flexion, adduction, and external rotation into extension, abduction, and internal rotation. D2 extension uses adduction and external rotation. The two flexion patterns move the leg forward. The start position given matches the end of D1 flexion.
  70. The correct answer is A (Shorten the rest time to move toward an on to off ratio of 1 to 3.): As fatigue resistance improves, the training load is increased by reducing rest, for example moving from a 1 to 5 ratio to 1 to 3. Longer rest reduces the stimulus and a shorter contraction time does the same. Continuous contraction without rest would cause rapid fatigue and is not a progression.
  71. The correct answer is B (Erythema ab igne from chronic heat, so stop heating the area and monitor the skin): A reticulated brown pattern after prolonged low level heat is erythema ab igne, a sign of repeated thermal exposure. The heat source is stopped and the skin monitored. Ordinary hyperemia fades within minutes and does not leave pigmentation, a cover allergy would not form a net pattern, and frostnip is a cold injury.
  72. The correct answer is C (Release the pull, lay him supine, and hold further traction until vascular screening): Dizziness and visual blurring during neck traction are warning signs of reduced vertebral artery flow, so the pull should be released, the patient placed supine, and further traction withheld pending vascular assessment. The fact that arm symptoms improved does not outweigh a possible vascular sign. Reducing the weight, changing the angle, or switching modes all keep the neck loaded.
  73. The correct answer is B (Oxford technique, starting at one hundred percent of 10RM and decreasing each set): The Oxford technique reverses DeLorme: 100, 75 and 50 percent of 10RM, so the load drops as fatigue builds. DeLorme rises from 50 to 75 to 100 percent. DAPRE adjusts the working load according to performance from session to session rather than descending within the session. Circuit training uses fixed timed stations and is not a set by set loading protocol.
  74. The correct answer is D (The Borg rating of perceived exertion scale, aimed at about 12 to 14): A beta blocker lowers resting and exercise heart rate, so heart rate zones from formulas underestimate real effort. Perceived exertion is unaffected by the drug and tracks workload reliably. A target of about 12 to 14 corresponds to somewhat hard effort. The age-predicted and reserve methods both depend on a heart rate response that the drug has flattened, and the resting-plus-20 rule belongs to early inpatient walking.
  75. The correct answer is D (Stay active within pain limits with short walks and daily tasks, avoiding bed rest): Nonspecific acute low back pain without red flags recovers best with early return to normal movement, because prolonged rest causes deconditioning and delays recovery. Short walks and daily tasks within tolerance are the baseline advice. Bed rest is tempting but prolongs disability. Heavy strengthening is a later stage goal, and full time bracing weakens trunk muscles without improving outcome.
  76. The correct answer is D (Gastrocnemius, stretched with the knee extended and the heel on the floor): Dorsiflexion improves from 5 to 18 degrees when the knee flexes, which slackens the two-joint gastrocnemius, so that muscle is the main limiter and the stretch is done with the knee extended. If the soleus were limiting, range would stay similar with the knee bent. A capsular limit would not change with knee position.
  77. The correct answer is B (Reactive stepping practice using large cued recovery steps): A backward pull producing tiny rapid steps with no recovery shows poor reactive stepping. Practice with large cued recovery steps trains the response he lacks. Anticipatory reaching trains a different control mode, and dual-task walking trains attention. Ankle strategy sways fit small perturbations, not a pull that exceeds the ankle's reach.
  78. The correct answer is B (Cryoglobulinemia, in which blood proteins precipitate when exposed to cold): Cryoglobulinemia is a recognized contraindication because proteins precipitate in cold and can occlude vessels. A warm rheumatoid flare, diabetes with intact sensation and circulation, and tendinosis with normal skin all tolerate local cooling, so none requires withholding cryotherapy.
  79. The correct answer is C (Gradually reduce the frequency of feedback so the patient learns to detect errors alone): Frequent augmented feedback helps early performance, but constant feedback can create dependence and weaken retention. Gradually reducing its frequency forces the patient to use intrinsic sensory information and detect errors, which improves learning that lasts. Removing feedback after one session is too abrupt, and giving feedback only on correct trials withholds the error information needed.
  80. The correct answer is A (Add a gradual five to ten minute active cool-down of progressively lighter pedaling): Dizziness right after stopping vigorous leg exercise reflects venous pooling once the muscle pump stops, which drops preload and blood pressure. A gradual active cool-down keeps the pump working while heart rate and vessel tone come down. A longer warm-up does not address the post-exercise drop. Switching interval format or drinking large amounts of fluid leaves the abrupt stop in place.
  81. The correct answer is A (When the beam strikes the surface perpendicularly, an angle of incidence of zero degrees): Intensity at the surface is proportional to the cosine of the angle of incidence measured from the perpendicular. The cosine is largest, equal to one, at zero degrees, so a perpendicular beam delivers the most. At 45 degrees the cosine is about 0.71 and at 60 degrees it is 0.5. Near 90 degrees the cosine approaches zero and almost nothing is delivered.
  82. The correct answer is B (Costochondritis, treated with reassurance, heat and gentle pectoral stretching): Sharp chest wall pain that is reproduced at the costochondral junctions without swelling, with a normal ECG and no systemic signs, fits costochondritis managed with reassurance, heat and gentle stretching. Tietze syndrome is similar but involves swelling, which is absent. Muscle strain would be tender in the muscle belly instead. Pericarditis pain varies with position and often comes with fever, and its work up does not fit this local, reproducible picture.
  83. The correct answer is C (Do not give traction and arrange emergency referral for suspected cauda equina compression): Saddle numbness, urinary difficulty and progressive bilateral leg weakness are signs of cauda equina syndrome, a surgical emergency where delay risks permanent loss of function. Traction cannot decompress the cauda equina. Centralization strategies and a one week wait both delay urgent surgical evaluation. Light traction force does not change the need for referral.
  84. The correct answer is A (Connect the drug pad as the negative electrode over the tendon.): Like charges repel, so a negatively charged ion is pushed away from the negative electrode and into the tissue beneath it. The drug pad over the tendon is therefore connected as the negative electrode. A positive pad would attract the ion back toward the pad. Alternating current reverses polarity and produces no net ion movement, and a remote pad would not deliver drug to the tendon.
  85. The correct answer is D (Cervical manual therapy with cervical proprioceptive training and head-eye coordination drills): Neck-related dizziness after whiplash with a negative positional test points toward the cervical spine. Treatment targets joint and muscle dysfunction and cervical proprioception. The Epley maneuver treats debris that the negative test excluded, and the other options do not address the neck.
  86. The correct answer is A (About 50 degrees of upward rotation of the scapula on the thorax): A 2 to 1 ratio divides the total into three parts, so 150 divided by 3 equals 50 degrees for the scapulothoracic joint. The glenohumeral joint gets the other 100 degrees. The 75 degree value assumes an equal split, and 100 degrees is the glenohumeral share.
  87. The correct answer is D (Dynamic medial stabilization of the patella against the lateral pull of the quadriceps): The vastus medialis oblique gives dynamic medial stabilization of the patella against the lateral pull of the rest of the quadriceps. Hip flexion through the anterior inferior iliac spine belongs to the rectus femoris. The VMO does not pull the patella laterally, and it does not selectively initiate the first degrees of knee extension.
  88. The correct answer is B (Evaluate for cervical radiculopathy first and treat the trigger point only as an adjunct): Dermatomal numbness, a reduced reflex and a positive Spurling test point to nerve root involvement, which a trapezius trigger point cannot explain. Evaluation of the radiculopathy takes priority, and myofascial treatment can be added for the coexisting muscle tenderness. Treating the muscle as the cause would delay needed care.
  89. The correct answer is C (Roughly 160 to 170 degrees F (about 71 to 77 degrees C)): Hydrocollator tanks are kept around 160 to 170 degrees F so the silica gel packs store enough heat for a 20 to 30 minute treatment through toweling. Water near 130 to 140 degrees F would leave packs too cool to be effective. Water near boiling is hotter than the pack and cover are designed for. Tissue temperature goals are far lower than tank temperature, so the 105 to 115 range misreads the two.
  90. The correct answer is D (Cortisol, which promotes protein catabolism and gluconeogenesis from amino acids): Cortisol is released from the adrenal cortex and mobilizes amino acids from muscle for gluconeogenesis, so chronic elevation favors protein loss. Aldosterone is also cortical but regulates sodium and potassium. Growth hormone supports protein synthesis, and epinephrine comes from the adrenal medulla and mainly drives glycogenolysis and lipolysis.
  91. The correct answer is D (Shorten the pulse duration to about 50 to 100 microseconds and adjust intensity to tingling.): Short phases of about 50 to 100 microseconds preferentially excite large sensory fibers before motor fibers, so tingling can be produced without contraction. A 300 microsecond phase recruits motor fibers at lower amplitude. Lengthening it further worsens contractions, and lowering the rate changes the mode, not selectivity.
  92. The correct answer is B (An index at or below 0.90 measured while the patient rests supine): The ankle-brachial index is the ratio of ankle to brachial systolic pressure, and a resting value of 0.90 or lower indicates peripheral artery disease. Values of 1.00 to 1.40 are normal. Readings above 1.40 suggest noncompressible, calcified arteries and are unreliable rather than diagnostic. Values near 0.40 reflect severe disease, and post-exercise testing is used as an additional measure, not the diagnostic threshold.
  93. The correct answer is A (The supraspinatus tendon, which runs under the acromial arch to the greater tuberosity): The supraspinatus passes through the subacromial space and is the tendon most often compressed and degenerated in impingement. The painful arc between 60 and 120 degrees fits its position. The subscapularis, infraspinatus, and teres minor are cuff muscles but lie outside the narrowest part of the space.
  94. The correct answer is A (Ear lobe, acromion, greater trochanter, just anterior to knee axis, anterior to lateral malleolus): In the lateral view the plumb line should fall through the ear lobe, the acromion, the greater trochanter, slightly in front of the knee axis, and in front of the lateral malleolus. Landmarks such as the iliac crest or a line behind the malleolus describe other views or deviations. Deviation of any segment from the line helps identify the muscle imbalance responsible.
  95. The correct answer is D (Hip flexion mobility work to expand available range before deep squatting): Passive hip flexion of 95 degrees is well below the typical 120, so the hip runs out of range and the lumbar spine rounds to finish the squat. Gaining hip range addresses the cause. Normal glute activation argues against a strength problem, and more lumbar flexion would worsen the rounding.
  96. The correct answer is D (A low rate of 2 to 10 pps with intensity strong enough for visible twitching): Endorphin-mediated analgesia is pursued with a low pulse rate, about 2 to 10 pps, and an intensity high enough to produce visible twitching. A higher rate at sensory intensity repeats the gate control approach that has already helped only briefly. Tetanic contraction at 100 pps fatigues the muscle, and a subsensory low rate gives no effective stimulus.
  97. The correct answer is C (Cytochrome c oxidase absorbs the light, raising ATP output and releasing inhibitory nitric oxide): The accepted photobiomodulation mechanism begins with absorption by cytochrome c oxidase in the mitochondrial respiratory chain. This raises ATP output and displaces nitric oxide that had been inhibiting the enzyme. Heating of water, hemoglobin, or collagen describes thermal modalities and does not explain the effect at typical therapy doses.
  98. The correct answer is D (Increase intensity toward 1.5 W/cm2 and allow adequate time, keeping the head moving): A dose of 0.5 W/cm2 at 1 MHz heats very slowly, so no warmth is expected. The target is deep, so 1 MHz stays, and intensity is raised toward the thermal range with enough time. A 3 MHz beam is absorbed superficially and a stationary head risks hot spots.
  99. The correct answer is B (Gentle low-load static stretching without manual overpressure, with attention to bone and skin fragility): Long-term corticosteroids weaken skin, tendon, muscle and bone, so stretching should be gentle, low-load and free of manual overpressure. High loads, ballistic partner stretching and end-range mobilization with maximal contractions increase the risk of tears, bruising and fracture. Range can still improve gradually with low-load holds.
  100. The correct answer is C (About 50 percent of body weight, which is roughly 90 lb for this patient): Separation of lumbar vertebral bodies is generally reported to need tension near 50 percent of body weight, which is 90 lb for a 180-lb patient. About 25 percent of body weight is the usual starting force and mostly overcomes friction of the lower body on the table. Ten percent is too low to overcome table friction, let alone separate segments. A pull equal to full body weight is not required for separation and would raise the risk of an adverse reaction.

Practice Test 6 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is C (Manual ischemic compression with the patient positioned supine): Ischemic compression treats the trigger point without any needle exposure, and lying supine protects a patient with a fainting history. Cervical thrust manipulation is a real technique but the neck is otherwise normal and the problem is a soft tissue point. Dry needling after reassurance, even when supine, still exposes a needle phobic patient to the trigger for syncope. Seated needling adds fall risk, so observation does not make it safer than a needle free option.
  2. The correct answer is A (Cervical nerve root compression, usually involving the C5, C6 or C7 root): Placing the hand on the head slackens the lower cervical nerve roots and often relieves radicular arm pain, which supports root compression at C5 to C7. Thoracic outlet syndrome tends to worsen when the arm is elevated. Carpal tunnel syndrome would not change with shoulder position, and impingement pain usually increases with overhead elevation.
  3. The correct answer is B (The score meets the 12 second screening cut-off, so start gait and balance training): A Timed Up and Go of 12 seconds or more is a commonly used screening threshold for increased fall risk, and her fall in the past year adds concern. A static Romberg does not test dynamic balance or gait, so a normal result does not rule out a deficit. A much longer cut-off belongs to frailer groups, and calling the time normal for age overlooks the positive screen. Starting gait and balance training addresses the risk now.
  4. The correct answer is C (Dispersive electrode larger than the active electrode, giving lower current density under the dispersive pad): The treatment effect is meant to occur at the active electrode, so the dispersive electrode is larger. A larger pad spreads the same current over more skin, lowering density and sensation there. A smaller dispersive pad would concentrate current and risk discomfort at the return site. Equal sizes give equal density and defeat the purpose of the active and dispersive roles.
  5. The correct answer is B (Transition to a maintenance program with self-monitoring and periodic follow-up in a community setting): A low-risk patient has an ejection fraction of 50 percent or more, capacity above 7 METs, and no ischemia or arrhythmia. After the supervised outpatient phase he can move to a maintenance program with self-monitoring and periodic follow-up. Repeating supervised sessions with continuous ECG adds no benefit. Stopping structured exercise forfeits the maintenance goal, and the inpatient phase is far behind him.
  6. The correct answer is D (The hamstrings, contracting eccentrically to brake the thigh and the extending knee): In terminal swing the limb is moving forward fast, and the hamstrings lengthen under tension to decelerate hip flexion and knee extension and position the foot for initial contact. Concentric quadriceps or iliopsoas activity would speed the limb further, and the gluteus medius works mainly in stance.
  7. The correct answer is A (The exercise intensity above which lactate appears in blood faster than the body can clear it): The lactate threshold is the intensity beyond which production by working muscle outpaces oxidation and removal, so blood concentration climbs abruptly. It is a submaximal marker of endurance capacity. A heart rate plateau and the VO2max plateau are different markers, and phosphocreatine depletion is a separate energy system event. None of those describe the point where lactate entry exceeds clearance.
  8. The correct answer is C (Stop forceful stretching and deep massage, and refer for imaging to assess for heterotopic ossification): Worsening pain, falling flexion and a firm warm mass after vigorous massage and passive stretching suggest myositis ossificans, a form of heterotopic ossification. Provocative stretching and deep massage must stop and imaging should confirm. Cold and compression suit the acute phase, not week three. Heat, ultrasound or massage may aggravate ectopic bone, and a firm mass is not an aspirable hematoma.
  9. The correct answer is C (Cold with compression and elevation to limit bleeding, with no heat or massage): Rapid girth increase signals ongoing bleeding, so cold with compression and elevation limits hemorrhage. Heat and massage increase blood flow and can enlarge the hematoma. A contrast bath includes a warm phase that vasodilates the injured muscle.
  10. The correct answer is B (Graded community exercise and balance training to rebuild confidence): Fear of falling with a good Berg score and normal strength is a confidence and avoidance problem, so graded community exercise and balance training rebuild activity. Home hazard removal is useful but does not reverse avoidance. Vitamin D and calcium support bone health and fracture risk, not fear. Intensive strengthening targets a deficit she does not have.
  11. The correct answer is A (No passive plantarflexion of the foot occurs when the calf muscle belly is squeezed): In a healthy leg, squeezing the calf pulls on the intact Achilles tendon and produces passive plantarflexion. When the tendon is completely ruptured, the force is not transmitted and the foot stays still, which is a positive Thompson test. Pain, dorsiflexion, or inversion are not the diagnostic response.
  12. The correct answer is B (Performance in the fourth working set, which guides the load for the next session): In DAPRE the repetitions achieved in the third set decide the weight used for the fourth set, and the repetitions achieved in the fourth set decide the load for the next session. This makes the program responsive to daily performance rather than fixed percentages. The first warm-up set is submaximal and gives little information, a second-set count is not the deciding set, and a fixed increment ignores how the patient performed that day.
  13. The correct answer is C (A delivered dose of 300 millijoules per square centimeter at the plaque): Dose equals intensity times time. Here 5 mJ per second per square centimeter times 60 seconds equals 300 mJ/cm2. Adding the two numbers gives 65, which is meaningless dimensionally. Reporting 5 ignores the exposure time, and 30 is a decimal slip.
  14. The correct answer is A (Refer for a standing posteroanterior spine radiograph to measure the Cobb angle): A rib hump with a scoliometer reading of 7 degrees or more calls for a standing radiograph, since the Cobb angle drives every later decision. Risser 0 means much growth remains, so six months of observation risks missed progression. Exercise therapy can be useful but should follow measurement of the curve. MRI is kept for pain, neurological signs or atypical curves, none of which are present here.
  15. The correct answer is B (Add low-load craniocervical flexion and scapular endurance work with gradual progression): Once full motion returns with mild end-range pain and a normal neurological exam, the subacute stage moves to low-load deep flexor and scapular endurance exercises with gradual progression. Maximal effort strength training is too aggressive at ten days. Waiting for zero pain with motion alone delays recovery and conditioning. Return to contact sport needs functional criteria, not just confidence.
  16. The correct answer is D (Roughly sixty percent of the cycle in stance and forty percent in swing): In normal free-speed walking one limb spends about 60 percent of the cycle in stance and 40 percent in swing. The swing percentage is smaller because the other limb carries the body during that time. A 70 to 30 split appears with slow, cautious walking, and the reversed or equal splits do not describe walking at all.
  17. The correct answer is A (The lumbar multifidus, confirmed by slow deep swelling under the fingers without motion): Palpation beside the spinous process in prone lets the therapist feel the deep multifidus swell slowly during a gentle contraction while the spine stays still. A global extension or hip hiking is a movement, not an isolated contraction. Drawing the abdominal wall away from the table describes the transversus abdominis. The spinous process location points to the multifidus.
  18. The correct answer is A (Suspected deep vein thrombosis in the calf of the affected leg): Massage can dislodge a clot, so suspected deep vein thrombosis in a limb is a contraindication until it has been excluded. Chronic tension, ordinary soreness and stiffness after a healed fracture are usual indications for massage. A clinic list separates conditions needing medical clearance from those that benefit from treatment.
  19. The correct answer is A (It roughly doubles, because charge depends on amplitude multiplied by duration): Phase charge is the product of current amplitude and pulse duration. With amplitude fixed, doubling the duration from 40 to 80 microseconds doubles the charge. Charge is not diluted by longer pulses, and duration does matter alongside amplitude. The relationship is linear, not squared, so quadrupling is wrong.
  20. The correct answer is B (A systolic pressure that falls by more than 10 mmHg while effort keeps climbing): Systolic pressure normally rises about 10 mmHg per MET. A drop of more than 10 mmHg despite increasing load suggests the heart cannot raise output and is a standard reason to end exercise. A steady heart rate rise and a moderate exertion rating are expected normal responses. The proper systolic rise per MET is also normal and not a reason to stop.
  21. The correct answer is D (Agonist contraction: the patient contracts the quadriceps while the therapist stretches the hamstrings): Agonist contraction relies on reciprocal inhibition from quadriceps activity, so the injured hamstrings are never contracted against resistance. Contract-relax and hold-relax both need a hamstring contraction, which is painful here. Slow reversal also loads the hamstrings against resistance, so it carries the same problem.
  22. The correct answer is A (Class 3B, which covers continuous outputs between 5 mW and 500 mW): An output of 250 mW lies between 5 mW and 500 mW, which is the class 3B band. Class 3R tops out at 5 mW, so it is too low for this device. Class 4 begins above 500 mW, and class 2 is limited to visible beams under 1 mW that rely on the blink reflex.
  23. The correct answer is D (1.5 W/cm2, found by dividing total power by the effective radiating area): Intensity in W/cm2 is power in watts divided by the ERA in cm2, so 6 W divided by 4 cm2 equals 1.5 W/cm2. Multiplying gives 24, inverting gives 0.67 and adding gives 10, none of which have the correct units. Check: 1.5 times 4 returns 6 W.
  24. The correct answer is B (Neck flexed about 20 to 30 degrees, with the pull applied along that line): Flexion of roughly 20 to 30 degrees opens the posterior elements and the intervertebral foramina at the lower cervical segments, so the line of pull should follow that angle. A neutral position places relatively more stress on anterior structures and gives less foraminal opening. Extension narrows the foramina and tends to aggravate radicular symptoms. Flexion near 60 degrees goes far beyond the range used clinically and over-stretches the posterior ligaments.
  25. The correct answer is B (It converts muscle activity into sound or visual signals so patients learn to modify it): Surface electromyographic biofeedback records muscle electrical activity and shows it as a tone or display, so the patient can sense and adjust a weak contraction. Joint angle feedback uses goniometric sensors and reports range, not muscle activity. A device that produces the contraction with current is electrical stimulation. Dynamometry reports force output, which is a result rather than the muscle activation the patient needs to feel.
  26. The correct answer is D (Raise total daily intake to about 1.6 to 2.2 g per kg, spread across meals): For people lifting to gain lean mass, total daily protein of about 1.6 to 2.2 g per kg is the best supported target, with timing a smaller refinement. At 0.8 g per kg he is at the sedentary recommendation and timing cannot make up the shortfall. Intakes far above the range add little, and carbohydrate does not substitute for amino acids in synthesis.
  27. The correct answer is C (Stride length, which equals the sum of one right step and one left step): A measure running from one heel contact to the next contact of the same foot spans a full gait cycle, which is stride length. It equals one right step plus one left step. Step length covers only half of that distance. Cadence is a rate in steps per minute, and step width is a side-to-side distance, so neither describes the distance of a full cycle.
  28. The correct answer is D (Avoid forcing extension and refer promptly for orthopedic evaluation of a possible displaced meniscal tear): A knee stuck short of extension with a firm end range block after a twisting injury and catching suggests a displaced meniscal fragment. Forcing motion can worsen the tear, so the joint should be protected and the patient referred for orthopedic evaluation. Heat with exercise treats soft tissue tightness, not a mechanical block. Strong mobilization risks damaging the meniscus, and casting delays the diagnosis the block calls for.
  29. The correct answer is B (Train each major muscle group two to three nonconsecutive days per week): Muscle needs about 48 hours of recovery between hard sessions for the same group, so two to three nonconsecutive days per week is the standard recommendation. A single weekly session is closer to maintenance than progression. Four consecutive days and daily training both leave no recovery, which fits his plateau and aching elbow. Lighter loads reduce but do not remove the need for rest days.
  30. The correct answer is B (Most is oxidized for energy in muscle and heart, with a smaller share becoming glucose): During recovery most lactate is taken up by slow-twitch fibers, the heart and other tissues and oxidized for energy, while a smaller share, roughly 20 percent, returns to glucose through the Cori cycle. Making the liver pathway dominant reverses those proportions. Only trace lactate is lost in sweat and urine. Fatty acid conversion is minor.
  31. The correct answer is B (Subtract the person's age in years from 220 beats per minute): The traditional prediction subtracts age from 220, so this man would be estimated near 168 beats per minute. The 208 minus 0.7 times age and 211 minus 0.64 times age equations are later regressions, not the traditional rule asked about. A constant of 200 is not part of the standard handout formula. All age-based equations carry a standard deviation of roughly 10 beats per minute, so they remain estimates.
  32. The correct answer is B (The 100 Hz beat created by the interference of the two carriers): Nerves cannot respond to each cycle of a medium-frequency carrier, but they follow the amplitude envelope, which pulses at the beat frequency. Here the beat is 4000 minus 3900, or 100 Hz, and that is the effective stimulation rate. Neither carrier alone, nor their average or sum, describes what the nerve experiences.
  33. The correct answer is B (Hold-relax, using an isometric hamstring contraction at the pain-free end of range): Hold-relax uses an isometric contraction, so the joint does not move, which makes it the best fit when motion is painful but static effort is not. Contract-relax and slow reversal both require moving through range or repeated concentric work. Rhythmic initiation uses passive and active-assisted movement through the painful range, so these choices load the painful arc.
  34. The correct answer is C (Withhold ultrasound and refer for evaluation of possible deep vein thrombosis): Swelling, warmth and calf tenderness after prolonged immobility fit a deep vein thrombosis, and ultrasound over a thrombus is contraindicated because energy and heat could dislodge it or promote clotting. No parameter change makes the treatment safe. The patient needs medical evaluation first.
  35. The correct answer is A (Hip abductor and external rotator strengthening added to the knee program): Regional interdependence says a remote impairment can drive local pain, and here weak hip abductors and external rotators allow knee valgus under load. Adding hip strengthening to the knee program addresses that contributor directly. Quadriceps work treats the symptomatic region only. Taping with foam rolling lowers symptoms without correcting the weakness, and orthoses with a delayed hip focus leave the measured deficit untreated.
  36. The correct answer is C (A deep muscle that provides segmental control and stiffness across individual lumbar vertebrae): The multifidus is a deep muscle spanning short segments that gives segmental control and stiffness across individual lumbar vertebrae. Large superficial flexors and global extensors produce gross torque instead. The transversus abdominis is also a deep stabilizer but works by raising intra-abdominal pressure, so it is a different muscle.
  37. The correct answer is C (Everyone wears goggles labeled for the laser wavelength with adequate optical density): Eyewear is wavelength specific, so each pair must be labeled for the laser in use with an optical density that suits its output. Anyone in the room can be exposed to direct or scattered light, including the clinician and bystanders. Closed eyelids transmit some light, and ordinary sunglasses are not rated for the laser wavelength.
  38. The correct answer is C (Stop treatment and arrange same-day medical evaluation for possible spinal infection): IV drug use, fever, night sweats, progressive pain and exquisite percussion tenderness suggest vertebral osteomyelitis or discitis. Mechanical-type treatment is inappropriate, and the patient needs medical evaluation the same day. Waiting weeks risks neurological harm and sepsis. Manipulation could spread infection, and deferring to a later imaging visit delays the urgent workup.
  39. The correct answer is D (A rigid or semi-rigid orthosis with a deep heel cup and medial arch support): Stage II dysfunction has a flexible deformity, and a rigid or semi-rigid orthosis with a deep heel cup and medial arch support unloads the posterior tibial tendon by controlling the rearfoot and arch. A soft insole does not provide control. A lateral wedge shifts load in the wrong direction. A heel lift shortens the calf but leaves the arch and rearfoot unsupported.
  40. The correct answer is C (Electrodes placed on the anterior neck, directly over the carotid sinus on either side.): Pads over the carotid sinus can trigger the baroreceptor reflex, causing bradycardia, hypotension or fainting, so anterior neck placement over the sinus is the one to avoid in this question. Transthoracic placement is also avoided but because of the risk of arrhythmia. Placement over the throat is avoided for laryngeal spasm and airway compromise. Current across the head is avoided for cerebral risks, and none of these is the carotid reflex.
  41. The correct answer is B (Hamstring shortening, because normal passive straight leg raise reaches roughly 80 degrees): With the pelvis stabilized and the knee straight, passive straight leg raise normally reaches about 80 degrees, so a limit at 55 degrees indicates hamstring shortening. The 120 degree figure applies to hip flexion with the knee bent. Neural tension needs symptoms or response to ankle or neck position, which are absent. A 50 degree norm is not the accepted range.
  42. The correct answer is B (Hot spots at the beam peaks and standing waves, which can injure tissue and cause pain): A stationary head concentrates the beam peaks in the same tissue and builds standing waves, with blood cell stasis, hot spots and periosteal pain. Slowly moving the head spreads the energy through the field. The beam does not broaden or absorb less when the head is still.
  43. The correct answer is C (Anterior wedging of at least 5 degrees in three or more adjacent vertebrae): Sorensen's criteria define Scheuermann disease by anterior wedging of 5 degrees or more in at least three consecutive vertebrae, usually with endplate irregularity. Postural kyphosis shows normal vertebral shapes and flexes freely, and a Cobb angle of 10 degrees describes scoliosis. Fracture produces acute height loss, not gradual wedging across several levels.
  44. The correct answer is A (Greatest hypertrophy in Type II fibers, with a smaller increase in Type I fiber area): Heavy resistance training recruits high threshold motor units, and the Type II fibers they supply show the largest increases in cross sectional area, with smaller increases in Type I fibers. Selective Type I growth reverses this pattern. Equal growth ignores the recruitment difference, and Type I only growth fits endurance training better. Fiber type specific growth is a well described adaptation.
  45. The correct answer is A (Serratus anterior, which holds the scapula flat against the thorax): Serratus anterior protracts the scapula and pins its medial border to the rib cage, so weakness lets the border lift during push-up loading. Rhomboid weakness changes scapular retraction and can contribute to a different scapular posture, but not the classic medial winging. The upper trapezius elevates the scapula and the supraspinatus abducts the arm.
  46. The correct answer is B (Reduce the current and inspect the skin frequently, because he may not sense burning.): Direct current can cause chemical burns under the electrodes, and a patient with impaired sensation may not report the warning sensation. Lowering current, using adequate electrode size, and checking the skin often reduce the risk. Raising current or using smaller electrodes increases current density in an insensate foot. Drug polarity is determined by the ion charge, not by neuropathy.
  47. The correct answer is D (Release tight muscles first, then activate weak ones, then integrate into functional tasks): The Janda sequence lengthens or inhibits overactive tight muscles first, then retrains the inhibited weak ones, then integrates the pattern into movement. Strengthening before releasing leaves the overactive antagonists dominating. Heavy shrugs overload the tight upper trapezius.
  48. The correct answer is C (Convert one session to intervals, alternating hard efforts with easy recovery periods): A plateau after four months at the same intensity means the body has adapted, so the stimulus needs a new challenge. With only 30 minutes available, intervals raise intensity within the time limit. Lower intensity, split bouts or a change of mode at the same pace do not add overload. Those choices would likely maintain or reduce fitness rather than improve the five-kilometer time.
  49. The correct answer is B (The 1 MHz beam heats superficial tissue slowly, so change to 3 MHz): At 1.0 W/cm2, 3 MHz heats superficial tissue about three times faster than 1 MHz, so a shallow tendon warms slowly with 1 MHz. Collagen absorbs ultrasound readily, and gel is needed for coupling. Therapeutic units offer 1 and 3 MHz, not 0.5 MHz.
  50. The correct answer is A (Across the fibers, to mobilize scar and keep collagen mobile): Deep transverse friction runs across the fibers to mobilize scar and keep healing collagen from adhering. A longitudinal stroke glides along the fibers and does not break cross links between them. Deep lengthening along the fibers is a stretching goal, not friction. Rapid strokes aimed at muscle spindles belong to tone techniques, not tendon friction.
  51. The correct answer is B (A patient with type I skin who burns easily and does not tan): Type I skin has little melanin, burns readily and does not tan, so it has the lowest MED. Type II also burns easily but tans minimally, so its MED is slightly higher. Type III tolerates more exposure before burning, and type V has more protective pigmentation and a much higher MED. Skin type only predicts the trend, and an individual MED test still sets the dose.
  52. The correct answer is A (Two net ATP per glucose molecule, with no oxygen required for this pathway): Anaerobic glycolysis produces four ATP gross and spends two in the investment phase, giving a net of two per glucose without oxygen. The older figure of 38 belongs to complete aerobic oxidation. Four ignores the investment cost, and three is the true net only when glycogen supplies the glucose unit.
  53. The correct answer is D (Ankle strategy, dominant for small slow sway on a firm surface): The ankle strategy shifts the body as a single inverted pendulum by activating muscles about the ankle from distal to proximal, and it dominates when sway is small and the surface is firm and wide. The hip strategy takes over with faster or larger perturbations or a narrow or compliant surface. A step is reserved for displacements that carry the center of mass beyond the base of support.
  54. The correct answer is B (Light to moderate loads performed for fifteen or more repetitions with short rest periods): Endurance training uses lower loads with high repetitions, commonly fifteen or more, and short rests so the muscle works against fatigue. Heavy loads for six or fewer repetitions develop maximal strength. Eight to twelve repetitions at moderate load with longer rests fit hypertrophy. Explosive low repetition sets with full recovery develop power.
  55. The correct answer is A (Posterior pelvic tilts, hip flexor stretching, and abdominal strengthening in flexion): Reducing lordosis with posterior pelvic tilts, lengthening tight hip flexors, and strengthening the abdominals widen the canal and improve walking tolerance. This matches the flexion bias of stenosis. Extension exercises and passive extension stretching narrow the canal and provoke symptoms. Building lordosis counters the goal of the program.
  56. The correct answer is D (Steppage gait from dorsiflexor weakness at the L4 and L5 roots): Weak dorsiflexors cause foot slap in the loading response and toe drag in swing, and the patient compensates with extra hip and knee flexion, a steppage gait. L4 and L5 supply tibialis anterior, and the other options need abductor weakness, a stiff knee, or spasticity, none of which are present.
  57. The correct answer is B (Brief intense mode: rate near 100 pps, long pulse, highest tolerable intensity for short periods.): Brief intense TENS uses a high rate with long pulses at the highest tolerable intensity for short periods, such as a 10 to 15 minute painful procedure. It produces rapid analgesia that can last through the procedure. Acupuncture-like TENS has a delayed onset of 20 to 30 minutes. Conventional and sensory-level burst settings are weaker than brief intense stimulation for acute procedural pain.
  58. The correct answer is A (In parallel sweeps toward the referral zone while the muscle is passively stretched): The vapocoolant is applied in slow, parallel sweeps in the direction of the muscle fibers and toward the referral zone while the muscle is stretched gently, so that the cold distracts from stretch discomfort. Circular spraying or holding in one spot does not link the stimulus with the stretch and can chill the skin excessively. Spraying a slack muscle gives no stretch benefit.
  59. The correct answer is D (Between 11 and 14, spanning light to somewhat hard effort): Patients with stable heart failure train at light to moderate effort, roughly 11 to 14 on the 6 to 20 scale, which suits a blunted heart rate response. Ratings of 15 to 17 impose more cardiac load than early training should. Ratings of 17 to 19 approach maximal effort. Ratings of 6 to 9 are too light to produce a training effect.
  60. The correct answer is A (Keep the hip in slight extension and avoid internal rotation while abducting): Keeping the hip in slight extension without internal rotation shifts work from the tensor fasciae latae to the gluteus medius. Rolling the pelvis forward is the substitution itself. Hip flexion during abduction favors the tensor fasciae latae. Moving resistance to the thigh changes the force but leaves the substituting position unchanged.
  61. The correct answer is D (Right gluteus medius, the hip abductor of the supporting limb): In single limb stance the abductors of the supporting hip hold the pelvis level against the weight of the swinging side. When the right gluteus medius is weak, the left pelvis drops. The left abductors are not working in this task, and gluteus maximus acts mainly in the sagittal plane.
  62. The correct answer is A (Reduced antagonist coactivation, so less opposing hamstring torque works against the quadriceps during extension): The EMG shows the hamstrings, the antagonist, are working less against the quadriceps. Lower antagonist coactivation reduces opposing torque and raises net knee extension torque without any change in quadriceps drive. The finding is the opposite of greater coactivation, and higher Golgi tendon organ sensitivity would increase inhibition rather than remove it.
  63. The correct answer is C (Shoulder flexion, adduction, and external rotation, finishing with the arm across the face): D1 flexion moves the shoulder into flexion, adduction, and external rotation, so the hand finishes across the face. The overhead and out to the side finish belongs to D2 flexion, which uses abduction instead of adduction. Internal rotation does not occur in either flexion diagonal for the shoulder. The extension, abduction, and internal rotation combination is the opposite diagonal, D1 extension.
  64. The correct answer is B (Sacroiliac mobilization or muscle energy technique, then active exercise): A hypomobile sacroiliac joint with good muscle control calls for restoring motion through mobilization or muscle energy technique and then active movement. Belts and force closure training are for hypermobile or unstable joints. Restricting motion further, loading heavily, or resting for weeks would not restore the missing movement.
  65. The correct answer is B (A pregnant patient whose planned field lies directly over the gravid uterus): Ultrasound should not be directed over the gravid uterus because of fetal exposure risk. Metal implants such as rods, plates and joint components are not contraindications, because metal does not absorb the beam in the way that bone and collagen do, although the plastic or cement parts of an implant deserve care. The three metal cases are acceptable fields.
  66. The correct answer is D (Overdosing likely inhibited tissue response, so return to the earlier 4 J/cm2 dose): Improvement at 4 J/cm2 followed by a worse response at ten times that dose fits the biphasic dose response, where excessive dose inhibits healing. The sensible step is to go back to the dose that worked. Escalating further moves deeper into the inhibitory range, and tolerance to light is not a recognized phenomenon here.
  67. The correct answer is C (Near terminal extension, in the last thirty degrees before the knee is straight): In open chain extension the lever arm of the resistance grows as the knee straightens, so quadriceps force rises while patellar contact area is small, and stress peaks near terminal extension. Closed chain squats do the opposite and load the joint more in deeper flexion. For many patients with pain, limiting open chain work to the pain free range of mid flexion is therefore common practice.
  68. The correct answer is D (The left hand, so the cane reduces the abductor force needed at the right hip): A cane in the hand opposite the painful hip provides a lever on the far side of the body, so the right abductors need less force to level the pelvis and the joint compression drops. A cane on the painful side does not create that opposing moment. The effect is not equal on both sides, and trunk rotation is not the goal.
  69. The correct answer is A (One electrode directly over the trigger point and the other over a nearby acupuncture point.): Acupuncture-like TENS is commonly applied over trigger points, motor points or acupuncture points, with intensity high enough to produce local twitching. One electrode over the trigger point and one over a nearby acupuncture point fits that approach. Thoracic paraspinal placement reflects conventional segmental technique. Crossing the muscle belly with high-rate sensory current is also conventional. The forehead is where pain is referred, not the source.
  70. The correct answer is A (In slow sweeps in one direction, from the trigger point toward its referred pain zone): Spray and stretch uses parallel sweeps in one direction, starting at the trigger point and moving toward the referred zone, while the muscle is passively lengthened. The brief skin cooling lets the muscle stretch with less pain. Holding the spray in place risks frosting the skin, and reversing the direction or sweeping back and forth departs from the technique.
  71. The correct answer is A (The curl-up, because anchored sit-ups load the lumbar spine through strong psoas activity): McGill found that full sit-ups load the lumbar spine through the psoas and the flexion cycle, so the curl-up with a neutral lumbar spine is preferred for patients with disc injury. A Swiss ball crunch encourages more flexion range. Straight leg raises load the lumbar spine through the psoas. Freeing the feet does not remove the psoas contribution.
  72. The correct answer is D (It reduces the lumbar lordosis, which favors separation of the posterior vertebral elements): Flexing the hips and knees to about 90 degrees tilts the pelvis posteriorly and flattens the lumbar lordosis, which favors opening of the posterior elements and facet joints. The lordosis is decreased, not increased, so the anterior disc space is not the area favored. Friction reduction is accomplished by a split table, not by the leg position. The facets are drawn toward a flexed, open position rather than locked in extension.
  73. The correct answer is A (Push-up plus, adding full scapular protraction at the top of the push-up): The serratus anterior protracts and upwardly rotates the scapula, and the push-up plus adds extra protraction at the end of a push-up to activate it strongly. Rows and prone horizontal abduction train the retractors and posterior shoulder muscles. Shrugs work the upper trapezius and levator scapulae.
  74. The correct answer is C (Repeated short walking bouts with rest breaks, lengthening the work portions over weeks): He stops at eight minutes, so the program should use repeated short bouts with rest and lengthen the work portions over weeks. This builds toward 20 minutes within his tolerance. Staying at eight minutes gives no progression, and 10 minute continuous bouts exceed what he can do now. Near-maximal intervals are too intense for severe deconditioning.
  75. The correct answer is B (Refer for cervical radiographs because age 65 or older is a high-risk factor): The Canadian C-spine rule checks high-risk factors first, and age 65 or older mandates radiography. A simple rear-end collision and active rotation of 45 degrees count as low-risk features only when no high-risk factor is present. Waiting for two days, manipulating before imaging, or withholding imaging on the low-risk mechanism all skip the age criterion that decides the pathway here.
  76. The correct answer is D (Layer syndrome, a combination of both crossed patterns in one person): Janda used layer syndrome for the combination of upper and lower crossed syndromes in one person, giving alternating weak and tight layers along the trunk. Upper crossed syndrome is only the upper half of that combination. Flat back and sway back are separate sagittal posture types and are not combined crossed patterns.
  77. The correct answer is D (Set the monitor top at or just below eye level, arm's length away): A monitor with its top at or just below eye level and about an arm's length away lets the head sit over the shoulders with minimal neck flexion or extension. A screen well above eye level forces extension and one off to the side adds sustained rotation. A screen far below eye level promotes the forward head posture already present.
  78. The correct answer is B (Greater stroke volume delivers the same cardiac output at a lower heart rate): Training raises stroke volume, so the same cardiac output at 100 watts needs fewer beats. Oxygen consumption was unchanged, which rules out lower oxygen demand. Maximal heart rate does not rise with training. Vagal tone contributes, but it does not account for the change when stroke volume is held fixed.
  79. The correct answer is D (Periodic vasodilation during prolonged cooling of the extremities that limits tissue freezing): With prolonged cold, vessels in the fingers and toes alternate between constriction and brief dilation, which delivers warm blood and helps protect against freezing. It is a normal protective reflex rather than an allergic reaction. It is not a sustained constriction, and it does not occur as a swelling response in deep muscle.
  80. The correct answer is B (Piriformis stretching in hip flexion and adduction plus rotator strengthening): The pain pattern changes with hip movements and not lumbar movements, and the straight leg raise is negative, so the source is the piriformis rather than the spine. Stretching in flexion and adduction lengthens the muscle, and strengthening addresses weakness that overloads it. Lumbar traction and press-ups target a spinal source. Ballistic hamstring stretching misses the deep external rotator.
  81. The correct answer is B (The plantar heel, delivered with standard eye safety and dose precautions): Laser energy is not applied over the abdomen or pelvic region during pregnancy, so the symphysis, abdominal wall, and iliac fossa are avoided. The heel is remote from the uterus and can be treated with ordinary precautions. Reducing the dose does not turn a contraindicated region into a safe one.
  82. The correct answer is A (The repeated bout effect, a protective neural, connective tissue and cellular adaptation): A single bout of unaccustomed eccentric exercise protects against damage and soreness from later bouts for weeks to months, which is called the repeated bout effect. Proposed mechanisms include improved motor unit recruitment, stronger connective tissue and cellular remodeling. Fiber type conversion, enzyme depletion and rapid hypertrophy are not accepted explanations for the milder response.
  83. The correct answer is D (Skin rolling and small circular friction along and around the healed scar): Once a scar is closed, dry and cleared by the surgeon, skin rolling and small circular friction mobilize the adherent layers and improve pliability. Hacking transmits force across the fresh scar, and gliding strokes do not engage the adhesion. Waiting six months is not required once the wound is stable.
  84. The correct answer is D (Negative polarity repels negatively charged plasma proteins, limiting leakage from capillaries into the interstitium): The textbook rationale for negative polarity in acute edema is that negatively charged plasma proteins are repelled from the electrode, so less protein leaks into the interstitium and less fluid follows. Ion attraction does not explain the effect. High voltage pulsed current at sensory level produces no meaningful heat. Muscle pumping needs motor-level contraction, which is not the goal in acute swelling.
  85. The correct answer is B (The dead bug in supine, lowering opposite arm and leg while holding lumbar neutral): The dead bug in supine trains lumbopelvic control with opposite arm and leg movement and keeps the painful knees unloaded. The side bridge builds lateral trunk endurance but has no opposite limb pattern. The bird dog fits the pattern but loads the knees. The prone lift unloads the knees but holds extension rather than neutral.
  86. The correct answer is D (Graded long-axis distraction and capsular mobilization, followed by pain-limited internal rotation range work): Limited internal rotation, flexion, and abduction with a firm capsular end feel and normal muscle length tests fit the capsular pattern of the hip, so treatment targets the capsule with graded distraction and mobilization and pain-limited range work. Aggressive or ballistic muscle stretching does not change capsular restriction and can irritate the joint. Long piriformis holds target a different tissue.
  87. The correct answer is C (Shortening of the tensor fasciae latae and iliotibial band): A leg that stays abducted during the Ober test indicates shortening of the tensor fasciae latae and iliotibial band on the lateral thigh. The adductors would restrict abduction, the deep rotators are tested with other positions, and the rectus femoris and iliopsoas are assessed with the Thomas test.
  88. The correct answer is B (Between 104 and 113 degrees F (40 to 45 degrees C) in the treated tissue): Raising tissue to about 104 to 113 degrees F increases blood flow, metabolism and collagen extensibility. Tissue above roughly 113 degrees F risks cell injury, so that range is not therapeutic. Temperatures near 98 to 102 degrees F barely exceed normal body temperature and produce little change. The 125 to 140 degree range would cause burns.
  89.
