The correct answer is B (Sensory stimulation that reduces pain perception and promotes muscle relaxation): Vibration provides a large, fast sensory input that competes with pain signals at the spinal level and encourages relaxation of the muscle. It does not meaningfully heat deep tissue like ultrasound, does not train muscle, and does not shear collagen the way deep friction is intended to. It is most useful as a comfort measure early in a program, before active work begins.
  90. The correct answer is C (Active electrode submerged in the basin near the foot, dispersive electrode on the proximal leg): In the immersion technique the active electrode goes into the water bath, which acts as a large conductive interface around the foot. The dispersive electrode is placed on the skin of the proximal leg to complete the circuit through the limb. Placing both in the water bypasses the tissue, and reversing the roles changes the intended polarity effect. Water is a conductor in the circuit, not a ground.
  91. The correct answer is C (The 3-hour trail marathon, where effort exceeds about 90 minutes at moderate to high intensity): Glycogen loading helps when effort exceeds about 90 minutes at moderate to high intensity, which describes a 3-hour trail marathon. A 45-minute race rarely depletes stores, so loading adds little. High intensity does use glycogen, but not enough to run out. The benefit does not wait for ultra events lasting several hours.
  92. The correct answer is D (The center of mass moved past the limits of the base): A step is triggered when a perturbation moves the center of mass beyond the limits of the base of support, so ankle and hip responses cannot restore balance without moving the feet. Weak plantar flexor torque would produce a hip strategy attempt, not a step into a larger base. Delayed onset from reduced sensation is not described here. The step is driven by the size of the displacement rather than by a vestibular reflex.
  93. The correct answer is B (Cautious gait, slow short steps with long double support): A cautious gait is an adaptive response to perceived instability: slow speed, short steps, a slightly wider base, and extra double support, with normal neurological findings. Parkinsonian gait needs rigidity or tremor, cerebellar ataxia needs irregularity and dysmetria, and sensory ataxia needs proprioceptive loss.
  94. The correct answer is B (Neuromuscular stimulation of the quadriceps during isometric contractions near sixty degrees): Persistent quadriceps lag after ACL surgery reflects arthrogenic muscle inhibition, and neuromuscular electrical stimulation combined with a voluntary isometric contraction recruits more motor units than exercise alone. The knee at about sixty degrees protects the graft. TENS, thermal ultrasound, and interferential pain currents do not generate the contraction needed to improve activation.
  95. The correct answer is D (Increase the exposure to 4.5 minutes at the 30 inch distance): Intensity falls with the square of distance, so time must rise with the square of the ratio. The ratio is 30 over 20, or 1.5, and 1.5 squared is 2.25, so 2 minutes times 2.25 equals 4.5 minutes. A linear scaling gives only 3 minutes, which underdoses. Inverting the ratio gives about 53 seconds and cubing it gives 6.75 minutes.
  96. The correct answer is A (Lengthen the warm-up to 10 to 15 minutes of gradually increasing low-intensity activity): Early exertional symptoms that fade with continued effort show warm-up ischemia, and a longer gradual warm-up of 10 to 15 minutes lets coronary flow adapt before the target pace. Reducing intensity for the whole session sacrifices training and does not target the early minutes. Static stretching does not raise heart rate gradually. Starting at target pace worsens the early ischemia.
  97. The correct answer is C (Alkaline reaction from sodium hydroxide formation, with tissue softening and liquefaction.): Under the cathode, sodium ions combine with water to form sodium hydroxide, an alkaline reaction that softens and liquefies tissue. Under the anode, hydrochloric acid forms and tissue hardens. Mixing the acid reaction with the softening effect is inconsistent. Chemical products do not cancel in direct current because each pole produces its own product.
  98. The correct answer is A (Slowed conduction in pain fibers and sensory gate control reduce perceived pain): Brief intense cold slows conduction in pain fibers and engages gate control, so perceived pain falls and exercise is easier. Lowered metabolism protects tissue in acute injury but does not explain relief in chronic tendinopathy. Cold-induced vasodilation is a minor effect. Spindle changes affect tone, not the pain relief reported here.
  99. The correct answer is A (Shortened iliopsoas limiting full extension of the tested hip): In the Thomas test, a thigh that lifts off the table while the knee stays flexed points to shortened iliopsoas, because the muscle holds the hip in flexion. Rectus femoris shortening shows instead as the knee extending, since the muscle crosses the knee. Tensor fasciae latae shortening shows as abduction or lateral drift. Gluteus maximus is a hip extensor, so its shortening would restrict hip flexion rather than lift the thigh.
  100. The correct answer is D (It holds the gastrocnemius and soleus slightly shortened, lowering tensile strain on the tendon): Raising the heel places the ankle in slight plantar flexion, which holds the gastrocnemius and soleus slightly shortened and lowers tensile strain on the Achilles tendon. It does not lengthen the calf unit. A heel lift may shift some pressure forward, but that is not the mechanism of symptom relief. Dorsiflexion demand at heel strike is reduced, not increased, so the tibialis anterior is not asked to share the work.

Practice Test 7 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is D (Gentle sliders that glide the nerve without added tension, in short sessions): Pain of 7 of 10 that lingers an hour and disturbs sleep marks a highly irritable nerve root, so loading must stay low. Sliders move the nerve through its bed without adding tension and fit this stage. Tensioners and sustained end-range stretches are reserved for low irritability and can flare this patient. Reflex normalization is not a prerequisite for starting gentle neural mobilization.
  2. The correct answer is D (A supervised program of progressive balance and lower limb strengthening exercise): Across community dwelling adults over 70, supervised exercise that progresses balance and leg strength has the most consistent evidence for fewer falls. A single home hazard visit helps mainly people with a fall history and does not improve capacity. Vitamin D supplements help those who are deficient, not residents with normal levels. A multifactorial package is a bundle of several measures and is not the single category with the strongest evidence for this general group.
  3. The correct answer is D (Side-lying with pillows between the knees and supporting the abdomen): In late pregnancy, the weight of the uterus can compress the vena cava when supine, lowering venous return and causing dizziness, so side-lying with pillow support is used. Flat supine and slightly raised supine both retain that compression, and prone positioning with a pillow places pressure on the abdomen. Pressure and stroke choices should also be gentle over the calves and lower limbs.
  4. The correct answer is B (Lower motor neuron denervation, with the curve shifted toward longer pulse durations.): Denervated muscle has an elevated rheobase and a markedly prolonged chronaxie, so the strength-duration curve shifts upward and to the right. Normal innervated muscle has a chronaxie well under 1 ms, so 12 ms is clearly abnormal. An upper motor neuron lesion leaves the lower motor neuron intact and does not shift the curve. Disuse atrophy alone does not prolong chronaxie.
  5. The correct answer is C (124 beats per minute, from the reserve fraction plus the resting rate): Maximum heart rate is 220 minus 60, or 160. Reserve is 160 minus 70, or 90. Sixty percent of 90 is 54, and adding the resting 70 gives 124 beats per minute. Taking 60 percent of 160 gives 96, leaving out the resting rate gives 54, and using 80 percent gives 142.
  6. The correct answer is C (Increased stretch tolerance, meaning the patient accepts greater tension before sensing discomfort): When range improves without a change in passive stiffness or torque at the old end-range angle, the gain reflects greater tolerance of stretch, a sensory change in how much tension the patient accepts. Structural adaptations such as added sarcomeres or tendon elongation would change stiffness and generally take longer. Higher spindle sensitivity would increase reflex resistance and work against range.
  7. The correct answer is C (Consume carbohydrate with roughly 20 to 25 g of protein within one to two hours): After heavy resistance work, carbohydrate refills glycogen while roughly 20 to 25 g of protein supplies amino acids for muscle protein synthesis, and both work best when taken within a couple of hours. A very large protein meal without carbohydrate leaves glycogen low and adds little beyond the useful dose. Carbohydrate by itself restores glycogen but gives muscle repair less support. Delaying carbohydrate until morning wastes the early window for glycogen resynthesis.
  8. The correct answer is A (Rectus femoris overactivity limiting knee flexion in swing): Stiff-knee gait is a common poststroke pattern in which the rectus femoris stays active in preswing and initial swing and resists knee flexion, so peak flexion falls and the patient circumducts. Hip flexion strength and dorsiflexion are normal here, and weak hamstrings would not stiffen the knee.
  9. The correct answer is B (The unstable base forces continuous small trunk muscle adjustments to keep the body centered): A ball moves under the patient, so the trunk muscles must adjust continuously to keep the body centered, which raises stabilizer activation compared with a firm chair. Hip height changes may alter hamstring or hip flexor work but do not explain the added trunk demand. Ground reaction force is not meaningfully reduced and does not lower the need for trunk muscle contraction. The instability itself is the training stimulus.
  10. The correct answer is A (Ice massage, because it cools a small area quickly and allows ongoing movement): Ice massage cools a small area to numbness in about 5 to 10 minutes and lets the patient move during treatment, which suits an epicondylar origin before exercise. Prolonged bagging and whole arm immersion cool more than needed. Holding vapocoolant in place frosts the skin.
  11. The correct answer is A (Continue pendulums and therapist-guided passive range of motion within limits, avoiding active elevation): Early after cuff repair the tendon is weak, so motion is passive and pendulum based and active elevation is withheld. Pulleys with dumbbell scaption, resisted tubing work, and push-ups all load the repair too soon. Active and resisted work follows once healing allows, per the surgeon's protocol.
  12. The correct answer is B (Right gluteus medius, strengthened in side-lying and standing hip abduction exercises): Leaning the trunk over the stance hip moves the center of mass closer to the joint, which reduces the abductor torque needed, so the lean signals a weak stance-side gluteus medius. The right side is the one to strengthen. The left gluteus medius is on the swing side, quadratus lumborum is not the primary pelvic stabilizer in stance, and adductors oppose the needed action.
  13. The correct answer is C (Submaximal pain-free isometric wrist flexion at several joint angles, then light active motion): Ten days after injury the tissue is in the proliferative phase with weak, immature collagen. Submaximal pain-free isometrics and light active motion stimulate healing without overloading it. Heavy eccentric work belongs to remodeling, aggressive stretching can disrupt the scar, and prolonged casting causes stiffness.
  14. The correct answer is C (20 s, found by dividing 5 J by 0.25 W): Time equals energy divided by power. With 5 J and 0.25 W the result is 20 s. Multiplying instead of dividing gives a meaningless 1.25, a decimal slip gives ten times too long, and a 100 mW assumption does not match the stated output.
  15. The correct answer is B (Loading response, weak eccentric control of the left tibialis anterior): Foot slap happens in the loading response when the forefoot falls to the floor because tibialis anterior cannot lengthen under control. The toes clear in swing, so the dorsiflexors are strong enough concentrically. Soleus works in midstance, the hamstrings brake the knee in terminal swing, and gastrocnemius contributes at push-off.
  16. The correct answer is A (Deep cervical flexors, lower trapezius, serratus anterior and rhomboids): Upper crossed syndrome pairs tight upper trapezius, levator scapulae, pectorals and suboccipitals with weak deep neck flexors, lower trapezius, serratus anterior and rhomboids. Strengthening goes to the weak, inhibited side while the tight side is stretched or released. Training the tight muscles would deepen the postural imbalance.
  17. The correct answer is C (A brief local twitch response of the taut band): A brief local twitch response of the taut band is the expected sign that the needle has reached an active trigger point. Radiating paresthesia suggests nerve contact, so the needle should be redirected. Bony contact means the needle is too deep and risks periosteal irritation, and a fascial give only shows the needle passed through a layer without confirming anything about the taut band.
  18. The correct answer is A (Head turned 45 degrees right, then lowered supine with the neck extended about 20 degrees): To test the right posterior canal, the head is turned about 45 degrees to the right and the patient is lowered quickly to supine with the head hanging about 20 degrees below horizontal. This aligns the canal with the plane of gravity. Turning left tests the opposite ear, and the other positions fail to align the canal.
  19. The correct answer is C (1 percent, since 100 pulses of 100 microseconds equal 10,000 microseconds per second): There are 100 pulses each second, and each lasts 100 microseconds, so the current flows 100 times 100, or 10,000 microseconds per second. One second is 1,000,000 microseconds, so the on time is 10,000 divided by 1,000,000, which is 1 percent. The other values come from a pulse ten times too long, a unit slip, or ignoring the gaps between pulses.
  20. The correct answer is A (Cold applied briefly to the joints, since active inflammation and warmth are present): Warm, red, swollen joints indicate active inflammation, so brief cold is the safer choice during a flare. Heat suits chronic stiffness without inflammation, not an active flare. A contrast bath that ends in heat still raises temperature in inflamed joints.
  21. The correct answer is C (Use loads of about 67 to 85 percent of 1RM for eight to twelve repetitions): Hypertrophy is best driven by moderate to heavy loads, about 67 to 85 percent of 1RM, for eight to twelve repetitions. Loads above 85 percent for few repetitions favor maximal strength. Loads near 50 to 60 percent for fifteen to twenty repetitions remain an endurance stimulus similar to what he does now. Light loads moved explosively train power.
  22. The correct answer is A (Somewhat hard, a moderate effort within the usual range for aerobic training): On the 6 to 20 Borg scale, 11 is light, 13 is somewhat hard, 15 is hard, and 17 is very hard. The numbers approximate heart rate in healthy young adults when multiplied by ten. A rating of 13 sits in the moderate range commonly prescribed for aerobic conditioning and cardiac rehabilitation. The other descriptions match 11, 15, and 17.
  23. The correct answer is A (The temporomandibular joint, through pressure of the chin strap against the mandible): A chin strap transmits part of the pull through the mandible, so the temporomandibular joint can become sore, especially when the jaw is clenched or the strap is tight. The transverse ligament of the atlas is a concern in rheumatoid instability, not a typical result of halter pressure. The hyoid lies behind the chin strap and is not loaded by a properly placed halter. The sternoclavicular joint is not meaningfully loaded by a cervical pull.
  24. The correct answer is D (Use the side-lying test, which avoids neck extension, in place of standard Dix-Hallpike): The side-lying test moves her from sitting to side-lying and provokes the posterior canal without sustained neck extension, protecting the myelopathic cord. Standard Dix-Hallpike needs extension. The roll test examines the horizontal canal. Brandt-Daroff is a treatment, so using it first skips diagnosis.
  25. The correct answer is A (Muscle spindle, whose primary afferents trigger alpha motor neuron contraction through the myotatic reflex): The muscle spindle sits parallel to the extrafusal fibers, and its primary afferents respond to both stretch and the velocity of stretch. They excite the alpha motor neuron of the same muscle, the myotatic reflex, which explains the brisk contraction after a bounce. The Golgi tendon organ lies in series and senses tension, and autogenic inhibition belongs to it rather than the spindle.
  26. The correct answer is D (Treat the posterior facet region and avoid direct irradiation over the anterior thyroid gland): Direct irradiation over the thyroid gland is avoided as a standard precaution, so the anterior neck is left untreated. The painful facet joints can still be treated from the posterior side. Lowering the dose or switching to red light still delivers energy to the gland, and the posterior neck does not require waiting for surgery.
  27. The correct answer is D (Quadriceps avoidance from inhibition after surgery with effusion): Effusion and pain inhibit the quadriceps, so the patient keeps the knee nearly straight in loading response instead of flexing about 15 degrees. The effusion and poor quadriceps activation fit quadriceps avoidance directly. Hamstring weakness and calf tightness can alter stance mechanics but do not explain missing flexion at contact with an effusion, and a backward trunk lean is a hip extensor pattern.
  28. The correct answer is D (Side-lying external rotation with a small towel roll under the elbow): Infraspinatus and teres minor are the main external rotators, and side-lying external rotation with a towel roll trains them with high activation while keeping the arm at the side. Empty can raises target the supraspinatus in a stressful position. Resisted internal rotation works the subscapularis. Rows work the scapular retractors and latissimus.
  29. The correct answer is B (Around the forearm over the muscle bellies, about one to two inches distal to the epicondyle): A counterforce brace is worn over the proximal forearm muscle bellies a short distance below the epicondyle, where it is thought to dissipate contraction force before it reaches the tendon origin. Compressing the epicondyle itself irritates the origin. A wrist or upper arm position does not change extensor loading.
  30. The correct answer is D (A monosynaptic stretch reflex arc from muscle spindle Ia afferents to alpha motor neurons): The tendon tap briefly stretches the quadriceps, exciting muscle spindles. Ia afferents synapse directly on the alpha motor neurons of the same muscle, forming the only monosynaptic reflex in the body, so the quadriceps contracts. Flexor withdrawal and Golgi tendon organ circuits use interneurons, and a Renshaw loop provides inhibitory feedback rather than a stretch response.
  31. The correct answer is A (Do not start electrical stimulation until the bone infection is treated and cleared): Untreated osteomyelitis is a contraindication to electrical stimulation for wound healing, because the underlying bone infection must be treated first. Polarity and pulse rate choices do not make stimulation safe over an active bone infection. The treatment should wait until the physician clears it.
  32. The correct answer is C (Two to three minutes between sets so phosphagen stores recover and load quality holds): Heavy sets near 85 percent of one repetition maximum draw mainly on the phosphagen system, which needs roughly two to three minutes to replenish so the next set can match the load. Very short rests leave the lifter fatigued and force a lighter load, which shifts the stimulus toward endurance. Rests of about 60 seconds suit hypertrophy blocks, and ten minutes adds no strength benefit while consuming most of the session.
  33. The correct answer is C (3 MHz pulsed at 20 percent duty cycle at low intensity, with the head moving): In the acute phase, heat increases blood flow and edema, so the goal is nonthermal pulsed ultrasound at low intensity. The target is superficial, so 3 MHz fits, and the head keeps moving. Both continuous options deliver thermal doses, and 1 MHz held still reaches too deep and concentrates energy.
  34. The correct answer is D (Painful limb stance phase is shortened, so the other limb steps sooner): Antalgic gait is a protective pattern in which the patient spends less time bearing weight on the painful limb, so the stance phase on that side is shortened and the opposite limb advances sooner. A prolonged stance on the painful side would be the reverse of protection. Symmetric slowing is not specific to a painful side.
  35. The correct answer is D (Ten 10-second nodding holds at the 22 mmHg level with minimal sternocleidomastoid activity): Training starts at the highest pressure she can sustain with the deep flexors and without superficial muscle compensation, here 22 mmHg, using 10-second holds for about ten repetitions. Aiming for 30 mmHg or adding resisted head lifts encourages sternocleidomastoid substitution. The goal is motor control first, then endurance and strength.
  36. The correct answer is C (Photokeratitis from ultraviolet exposure of the cornea during the session): Unprotected ultraviolet exposure burns the corneal surface, and symptoms of photokeratitis appear several hours later with pain, gritty irritation, tearing and light sensitivity in both eyes. Gauze is not a reliable ultraviolet barrier, so goggles were needed. Allergic conjunctivitis typically itches and does not start hours after a session. Infrared cataract is a chronic lens change, and lid closure does not cause acute painful irritation.
  37. The correct answer is A (Return to the split squat and add only one new challenge at a time): He lost neutral spine only when rotation and load were added together, so the sound principle is to progress one variable at a time from the pain-free split squat. Swapping variables while adding a surface still changes several things at once. Going back to bilateral squats then re-adding both repeats the error, and cueing through the failure keeps training with poor control.
  38. The correct answer is C (Pulse rate near 100 pps with sensory intensity and no visible contraction): Gate control analgesia relies on a relatively high rate, about 80 to 120 pps, at a sensory intensity that produces comfortable tingling. A low rate of about 10 pps with twitching is the endorphin-oriented method for chronic pain. Tetany at 100 pps would fatigue an acutely strained muscle. A subsensory 1 pps setting gives the patient no afferent input to gate the pain.
  39. The correct answer is D (126 over 80 at rest rising to 170 over 112 at peak effort): Systolic pressure normally rises with workload while diastolic stays about the same or changes by a few mmHg. A diastolic increase of more than 10 mmHg, or a value above 110, is an abnormal hypertensive response that needs medical review. The other three show appropriate systolic rises with stable or slightly lower diastolic readings.
  40. The correct answer is B (It supplements but cannot replace dynamic muscular control, so training should continue): Functional braces can add some restraint at low loads and may improve proprioception, but they do not control high speed rotation and they do not restore the ligament. Dynamic stability depends on neuromuscular control, so training continues. A brace does not strengthen the hamstrings, and conditioning alone does not address the instability.
  41. The correct answer is D (3 MHz continuous at about 1.0 W/cm2 with a moving head, then stretching): Adhesions in a shallow, chronic tendon respond to heating that increases extensibility, so continuous 3 MHz at about 1.0 W/cm2 with a moving head is appropriate, followed immediately by stretching. A stationary head risks hot spots, and pulsed low-intensity settings do not heat enough. A 1 MHz beam would pass beyond a tendon this shallow.
  42. The correct answer is D (Maximal wrist flexion held about 60 seconds with the backs of the hands pressed together): Phalen's test holds the wrists in full flexion with the dorsal surfaces together for up to about 60 seconds, and reproduced tingling suggests median nerve compression. The prayer position uses extension. Percussion over the wrist is Tinel's sign, and direct pressure over the tunnel is the carpal compression test.
  43. The correct answer is B (The mTORC1 signaling pathway, which increases translation initiation in muscle fibers): Mechanical tension and leucine activate mTORC1, which increases translation initiation and ribosome activity and so raises muscle protein synthesis. AMPK is activated by low energy and inhibits mTORC1, which is why heavy endurance work can blunt hypertrophy. Myostatin limits muscle growth, and cAMP signaling mobilizes glycogen rather than driving translation.
  44. Protocols emphasize protected passive and assisted motion and delay resisted strengthening until later, often after about ten to twelve weeks. Band work, maximal isometrics, and weight bearing push-ups load the repair too early.
  45. The correct answer is A (Weak push-off at terminal stance shortening the opposite step): The S1 root supplies gastrocnemius and soleus, which produce push-off in terminal stance and preswing. When they are weak, the heel rise is delayed, the forward drive is lost, and the opposite limb takes a shorter step. Foot slap, pelvic drop, and trunk thrust belong to other muscle groups.
  46. The correct answer is B (Raise the rate to about 100 pps with intensity below the motor threshold): Wound protocols commonly use a pulse rate near 100 pps at an intensity below the motor threshold, which gives stimulation without painful contractions. A low rate such as 10 pps produces individual twitches, and 1 pps produces even more distinct ones. Staying above the motor threshold at 100 pps would cause tetany. Raising the rate and lowering intensity addresses the problem directly.
  47. Traction and manipulation risk cord injury, so they are withheld pending flexion-extension imaging and specialist review. Gentle traction still pulls on lax upper cervical ligaments. Exercise or end-range flexion stretching would still stress the upper cervical segments.
  48.   49. It does not hold the patella laterally, which is the job of a patellar stabilizing brace. It does not limit terminal extension, and it does not control tibial translation, which is the function of a functional ACL brace.
  50. The correct answer is A (Wear UV-blocking sunglasses and cover exposed skin until the drug effect clears): Oral psoralen leaves the skin and the lens of the eye sensitive to ultraviolet radiation for many hours after the session. She needs UV-blocking sunglasses and clothing that covers exposed skin until the effect fades. Sunscreen alone is not adequate protection. The controlled clinic dose does not shorten the period of sensitivity.
  51. The correct answer is D (Cold pack with compression and elevation, as dependent immersion can raise swelling): In a fresh sprain, cold with compression and elevation limits swelling. A hanging leg in a whirlpool lets gravity pull fluid into the ankle, which can raise edema despite the cold. A contrast bath and moist heat both include warming that increases blood flow and swelling.
  52. The correct answer is C (Static balance, holding the center of mass over a stationary base): Static balance is the ability to keep the center of mass within a stationary base of support, as in quiet standing. Dynamic balance requires control while moving, reactive balance answers an unexpected disturbance, and anticipatory balance prepares posture before a voluntary movement. Nothing moved or disturbed this patient during the 30 seconds.
  53. The correct answer is C (Tensor fasciae latae and the iliotibial band on the tested side): The Ober test lets gravity adduct an abducted, extended hip, and a leg that stays up indicates shortened tensor fasciae latae and iliotibial band. Adductors shortened would limit abduction, not adduction. Piriformis and the deep rotators restrict rotation and are assessed with other tests. Gluteus maximus fibers that insert onto the iliotibial band are less relevant than the tensor fasciae latae and band itself.
  54. The correct answer is C (Contraction moves GLUT4 transporters to the membrane through an insulin-independent pathway): Muscle contraction moves GLUT4 to the membrane through an insulin-independent pathway involving AMPK and calcium signaling. That explains glucose uptake with low insulin. Exercise lowers rather than raises insulin, and increased sensitivity is a later effect rather than the mechanism during the walk. Greater blood flow helps delivery but transport still depends on GLUT4.
  55. The plan is to stop impact, protect the bone, and confirm with imaging. Compartment syndrome gives exertional tightness, and a quick return to mileage risks a complete fracture.
  56. The correct answer is C (The rectus femoris, a two-joint hip flexor and knee extensor): Flexing the knee in prone stretches the rectus femoris across both joints, and a short muscle pulls the hip into flexion and the pelvis into anterior tilt. The iliopsoas crosses only the hip. The biceps femoris is slackened by knee flexion, and the tensor fasciae latae is tested differently.
  57. The correct answer is A (Avoid electrodes across the abdomen, low back and pelvis during pregnancy and use another approach): Electrical stimulation is avoided over the abdomen, lumbar region and pelvis in pregnancy because the effect on the fetus and uterus is not established. Sensory intensity, a single circuit, or a high beat does not remove that concern. Another approach should be chosen for the sacroiliac pain.
  58. The correct answer is A (A two-piece Philadelphia-type collar with rigid front and back supports): A Philadelphia-type collar uses a rigid front and back with chin and occiput support and restricts flexion and extension much more than foam while remaining wearable. Extra padding does not make a soft collar restrictive. A halo vest is for unstable injuries and is far more restriction than a stable strain needs. A traction harness delivers a pull and is not a motion limiting orthosis.
  59. The correct answer is C (Approximately 8 METs, from 28 divided by 3.5): One MET equals 3.5 mL of oxygen per kilogram per minute, so 28 divided by 3.5 gives 8 METs. The result is not converted automatically, because mL/kg/min is a different unit. Using 4.0 or 4.5 as a divisor ignores the standard resting value and understates capacity. Age does not change the defined MET constant.
  60. The correct answer is B (Light to moderate loads lifted as fast as possible with controlled lowering): Power is force times velocity, so power training moves a light to moderate load with maximal intended speed. Slow heavy lifts develop maximal strength, slow moderate sets to fatigue develop hypertrophy, and thirty repetitions with brief rest develop endurance. None of these emphasize the speed of movement she needs for the chair rise.
  61. The correct answer is B (Hemiplegic gait, with a circumducting stiff leg and a flexed upper limb): Unilateral spasticity after a middle cerebral artery stroke leaves the arm flexed and the leg extended, so the patient circumducts the leg to clear the floor. Steppage gait produces high knee lift to clear a dropped foot but has no arm posture. Spastic diplegia affects both legs with scissoring, and parkinsonian gait features short shuffling steps and reduced arm swing without circumduction.
  62. The correct answer is B (Avoid repeated lumbar flexion soon after rising, when the discs are most hydrated): Discs are most hydrated after a night of rest, and McGill advises avoiding repeated spinal flexion in the first period after rising because the hydrated disc tolerates bending stress less well. Late in the day the discs have lost height and are stiffer. Training flexion in the morning is the opposite of the advice.
  63. The correct answer is C (A patient with a chronic knee flexion contracture from capsular tightness after immobilization): Chronic capsular contracture is a classic indication, since heating dense collagen increases extensibility before stretching. An acute warm effusion is a poor candidate for heat. A swollen calf after travel raises concern for thrombosis, and a known tumor is a contraindication.
  64. The correct answer is C (Radial nerve gliding and activity changes to reduce repeated forearm rotation near the supinator): Tenderness several centimeters distal to the epicondyle with pain on resisted supination and normal wrist extension suggests radial nerve irritation at the supinator, so gliding and rotation avoidance fit. Eccentric extensor loading and the counterforce strap treat the common extensor origin, which is not tender. Median nerve care misses the radial nerve.
  65. The correct answer is C (Eccentric contractions generate high force per active fiber, disrupting myofibrils): During descent the quadriceps lengthen under load, and eccentric contractions produce high tension across relatively few recruited fibers, causing structural disruption and an inflammatory response that peaks after about a day or two. Eccentric work also costs less oxygen and produces less lactate than concentric work. Temperature changes do not explain the pattern.
  66. The plan lengthens the pectoralis minor and strengthens the lower trapezius and serratus anterior to restore posterior tilt and upward rotation. Upper trapezius shrugs add to elevator dominance, and stretching the rhomboids or serratus muscles worsens the imbalance.
  67. The correct answer is A (Terminal stance, just before the heel rise, as the tibia advances): As the tibia advances over the planted foot the ankle dorsiflexes to about 10 degrees by terminal stance, just before the heel rises. In loading response the ankle plantarflexes after heel contact. Midstance has dorsiflexion still building. In preswing the ankle moves rapidly toward plantarflexion.
  68. The correct answer is B (20 mW, by multiplying peak power, pulse width, and frequency): Average power equals peak power times pulse width times frequency. That is 10 W times 0.0000002 s times 10,000 per second, which equals 0.02 W or 20 mW. Quoting the peak as the average overstates output by a factor of 500, and misreading width or frequency shifts the answer by ten.
  69. A cerebellar lesion produces sway with the eyes open as well. Vestibular loss on a firm surface is usually compensated by vision and somatosensation, and a visual deficit would not worsen when the eyes are closed.
  70. The correct answer is C (Avoid cold over the elbow because regenerating nerve may not signal tissue stress): Regenerating nerve has unreliable sensation, so cold over it can injure tissue without a warning signal. Cold is therefore avoided. A smaller area or a milder temperature does not change that. Ice for 20 minutes adds a prolonged exposure.
  71. The correct answer is D (Return to short wear periods and build up gradually, rechecking arch height and fit): New semi-rigid orthoses commonly cause arch and shin aching when worn all day at once, because tissues need time to adapt. The fit and contour are already correct, so the fix is a graded break-in, starting with short periods. Adding arch height or padding changes a correct device, and abandoning or replacing it is premature. Gradual build-up with rechecks is the standard approach.
  72. The correct answer is B (A low pulse rate of about 10 pps that yields individual twitches): A muscle pump needs alternating contraction and relaxation, which low pulse rates around 10 pps provide as individual twitches. Rates of about 60 to 120 pps fuse into tetany, which holds the muscle contracted and does not move fluid rhythmically. Very high rates produce no useful muscle response. The elevated leg lets the twitches empty the limb proximally.
  73. The correct answer is D (Withhold stretching during the acute inflammatory phase, then add gentle pain-free stretching after about 72 hours): Stretching is withheld during the acute inflammatory phase, then gentle pain-free stretching is added after about 72 hours. Waiting six months delays remodeling guidance unnecessarily. Vigorous stretching or maximal PNF contractions on day two load fragile fibers and risk reinjury.
  74. The correct answer is D (Rating of perceived exertion, aiming for about 12 to 13 on the 6 to 20 scale): Beta blockers lower resting and exercise heart rate, so heart rate based formulas no longer match true effort. RPE reflects perceived work independent of the drug, and 12 to 13 corresponds to moderate intensity. Reducing a predicted maximum by 10 beats or recomputing the Karvonen zone from a medicated resting rate still depends on a blunted heart rate signal. A fixed percent of 220 minus age has the same weakness.
  75. The correct answer is B (Cervical proprioceptive training, including laser-guided repositioning and gaze drills): Reduced head repositioning accuracy after whiplash with negative vestibular testing points to cervical proprioceptive dysfunction, so laser-guided repositioning and gaze drills are targeted. Vestibular adaptation treats inner ear hypofunction, which testing does not show. Mobilization addresses range rather than joint position sense, and neutral isometric strengthening does not retrain it.
  76. The correct answer is A (Bilateral standing on a wobble board with support available): Early proprioceptive training after a sprain starts with supported bilateral tasks that restore ankle position sense without overloading healing ligaments. Single-leg foam with eyes closed, hopping, and cutting skip several steps of progression. Skipping steps raises the risk of re-injury.
  77. The correct answer is D (Delayed gluteus maximus activation with early hamstring and lumbar erector spinae activation): The faulty pattern shows the gluteus maximus firing late while the hamstrings and lumbar erector spinae take over the lift, often with excess lumbar extension. The early gluteal pattern is the desired one. The abdominal and tensor patterns are not the classic fault in this test.
  78. The correct answer is B (Ultrasound suits patient one, while patient two is better served by a hot pack): Ultrasound heats only a few times the ERA at a time, so it fits small, localized dense targets such as a tendon attachment. A 200 cm2 field would take an impractical number of sections. A hot pack heats that area better, and ultrasound does heat tendon and muscle effectively.
  79. The correct answer is A (High risk, with continuous ECG monitoring and direct staff supervision for an extended number of sessions): An ejection fraction below 40 percent, a history of ventricular tachycardia, and a capacity under 5 METs each place a patient in the high-risk category. High risk requires continuous ECG monitoring and close supervision until stability is shown. Lower categories under-monitor him. A low ejection fraction is a reason to supervise exercise carefully, not to withhold it.
  80. The correct answer is D (Medial epicondylitis involving the flexor-pronator origin, most often pronator teres and flexor carpi radialis): Golfer's elbow is a tendinopathy at the flexor-pronator origin, and resisted wrist flexion with pronation loads pronator teres and flexor carpi radialis. Lateral epicondylitis is the extensor-side problem. Cubital tunnel syndrome causes ring and small finger paresthesia, and distal biceps problems hurt with resisted supination.
  81. The correct answer is C (Avoid irradiating the skin over the untreated carcinoma and keep treatment away from that region): Photobiomodulation can raise cell energy metabolism, so irradiation over an active malignancy is contraindicated. A low dose still delivers light to the lesion, and gauze does not block the beam. A shallow red wavelength is absorbed in the very skin layers that contain this carcinoma.
  82. The correct answer is C (Just below the anterior superior iliac spines, across the greater trochanters): A sacroiliac belt works by compressing the pelvic ring, so it is worn low, just below the anterior superior iliac spines and across the greater trochanters. At the iliac crests it acts as a lumbar support and does not compress the sacroiliac joints well. A belt at the tenth thoracic level is a thoracic position.   83. The correct answer is C (Repeat the tap with reinforcement, such as hooking the clasped hands and pulling them apart): A reflex that is absent may simply be suppressed by tension, so the examiner should use reinforcement before documenting. Hooking the clasped hands and pulling apart is the Jendrassik maneuver and facilitates the reflex. Changing knee position or comparing sides does not facilitate it. Ordering nerve conduction studies before reinforcement skips a simple step.
  84. The correct answer is B (Set intensity by visible response, inspect skin before and after, and use a shorter session): With impaired sensation the patient cannot report the stimulus reliably, so intensity is guided by visible response and the skin is inspected before and after to detect injury. A shorter session limits risk. Relying on the report or chasing tingling can lead to overdosing. A carrier change does not address reduced sensation.
  85. The correct answer is D (D2 flexion, which combines hip flexion, abduction, and internal rotation): Lower extremity D2 flexion begins in extension, adduction, and external rotation and moves into flexion, abduction, and internal rotation. D1 flexion uses adduction and external rotation. D2 extension is the reverse, starting from the finish position of D2 flexion. D1 extension combines extension, abduction, and internal rotation.
  86. The correct answer is C (Scaption with the thumb up, raising the arm in the scapular plane to shoulder height): Scaption with the thumb up in the scapular plane keeps the greater tuberosity clear of the acromion while activating the supraspinatus. Elevating in the frontal plane brings more compression, and upright rows load the shoulder in internal rotation at height. Side-lying external rotation is a valid cuff exercise, but it targets the infraspinatus and teres minor more than the supraspinatus.
  87. The correct answer is C (Parkinsonian gait, with a bent trunk and tiny shuffling steps): A stooped posture, tiny accelerating steps and reduced arm swing identify Parkinsonian gait. Cerebellar gait is wide based and irregular. Normal pressure hydrocephalus gives a shuffling magnetic gait but not a bent trunk or festination. Hemiplegic gait is one sided with a circumducting leg and a flexed arm.
  88. The correct answer is D (Active, because pressure reproduces her familiar pain complaint): An active trigger point causes spontaneous pain and, when compressed, reproduces the patient's recognized complaint. A latent point is tender locally but does not reproduce the patient's pain. Satellite and secondary points develop in other muscles because of an existing point and are not defined by this finding.
  89. The correct answer is C (Approximately 50 to 60 degrees F (about 10 to 15 degrees C)): Cold whirlpools are typically set at about 50 to 60 degrees F (10 to 15 degrees C), which cools effectively and stays tolerable. Water at 40 to 45 degrees F is closer to ice bath temperatures and is poorly tolerated. Water at 65 to 75 degrees F is only cool and gives little cooling. Water at 92 to 98 degrees F is neutral warm and is not used for cryotherapy.
  90. The correct answer is B (A beat of about 100 Hz at sensory intensity with comfortable tingling and no contraction): Gate control analgesia is usually delivered with a beat in the range of roughly 80 to 150 Hz at a sensory intensity, giving tingling without contraction. A 2 Hz beat with visible contraction is the strategy aimed at endorphin-mediated relief, typically for chronic pain. A 1000 Hz beat is far outside the usual beat range, and a subsensory level would not stimulate the afferent fibers. Tetanic contraction at 100 Hz adds fatigue and discomfort to an acute injury.
  91. The correct answer is C (Specificity, also called the SAID principle, meaning adaptations are tied to the imposed demands): Specificity, expressed as the SAID principle (specific adaptations to imposed demands), means training effects are largest for the movement pattern, velocity and energy system practiced. That is why a stair climbing job calls for task like stair training. Progressive overload, diminishing returns and variation are real training concepts but none of them selects the type of training to match a task.
  92. The correct answer is C (Increased fall risk, because the time exceeds the commonly cited cutoff near 13.5 seconds): For community-dwelling older adults a Timed Up and Go time above about 13.5 seconds is associated with increased fall risk, so 16 seconds is abnormal and fits her unsteadiness. Times under 20 seconds describe independent basic transfers in frail groups, a different use. Dependent mobility is suggested by times above 30 seconds. Fall risk can be present before any fall has occurred.
  93. The correct answer is C (Release or stretch the tight tonic muscles first, then retrain the weak phasic muscles): Janda's approach releases the overactive tonic muscles first, since their tightness inhibits their antagonists, and then retrains the weak phasic muscles. Strengthening a muscle that is reciprocally inhibited by a tight antagonist is less effective. Training everything at once or delaying correction does not follow the sequence.
  94. The correct answer is C (Head B has a higher beam nonuniformity ratio, producing hot spots of peak intensity): With the same ERA and the same displayed intensity, a difference in sensation points to the beam profile. A head with a high BNR has peaks several times the average, which feels like focal burning. A lower BNR means a more even beam, so it would reduce hot spots rather than cause them, and the displayed average intensity is the same for both heads. The stem states that the ERAs are equal, and frequency is not tied to one head here.
  95. The correct answer is D (Probe the skin and mark the site giving the strongest twitch at the lowest current.): The motor point is found functionally by moving a pen electrode over the skin and marking where the lowest current produces the strongest twitch. This identifies the site with the lowest nerve threshold. The tendon junction and belly center are not reliable substitutes, and bony prominences are chosen by neither resistance nor nerve location.
  96. The correct answer is A (6 to 20, so that a rating multiplied by ten approximates heart rate): The original Borg scale runs from 6 to 20 so that a rating times ten roughly matches the heart rate of a healthy young adult, with 6 near rest and 20 near maximum. The 0 to 10 scale is the revised category ratio version with different anchors. A scale starting at 1 or one measured as a percent of maximum is not the original instrument.
  97. The correct answer is C (Inferior glide of the humeral head on the glenoid fossa): When a convex surface moves on a concave surface, the glide occurs in the direction opposite the bone movement. In abduction the humeral head rolls superiorly, so it glides inferiorly, and an inferior glide mobilization restores the missing motion. A superior glide matches the roll and would jam the head upward. Anterior and posterior glides address rotation and flexion or extension limits rather than abduction.
  98. The correct answer is D (Intensity at the skin varies inversely with the square of the distance from the lamp): For a point source the radiant energy spreads over an area that grows with the square of distance, so intensity falls with the square of distance and doubling the distance cuts it to one quarter. A simple inverse relationship underestimates the drop. A cube law overstates it. A direct relationship reverses the physics.
  99. The correct answer is A (The C7 root, exiting at the C6-C7 intervertebral foramen): The C7 root supplies the triceps, wrist flexors, the middle finger and the triceps reflex, and exits at the C6-C7 foramen. C6 would affect the biceps reflex and thumb, C8 the finger flexors and little finger, and C5 the deltoid. The normal biceps reflex and thumb sensation exclude C6.
  100. The correct answer is C (An unloader brace applying a valgus force that shifts load toward the lateral compartment): For medial compartment osteoarthritis with varus alignment, an unloader brace applies a three-point valgus force that moves load toward the lateral compartment. A varus-directed force would increase medial loading. A patellar stabilizing brace treats patellofemoral tracking, and a functional brace limiting tibial translation is for ligament deficiency, so neither addresses compartment overload.

Practice Test 8 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (Proliferative phase, when fibroblasts build granulation tissue and early scar): New capillaries, fibroblasts, granulation tissue, and disorganized type III collagen mark the proliferative phase. The inflammatory phase is dominated by edema and phagocytosis, and coagulation happens in the first minutes. Remodeling comes later, when type III is replaced by stronger type I collagen aligned to stress.
  2. The correct answer is D (A dual task walking test, such as Timed Up and Go while counting backward): His scores are near the ceiling on single tasks, but his problems appear when attention is divided. A dual task test such as the cognitive Timed Up and Go can reveal that deficit. Repeating the Berg or adding a static test does not stress divided attention.
  3. The correct answer is A (Duty cycle of about 17 percent, an on to off ratio of 1 to 5): Duty cycle is on time divided by the total of on plus off time, so 10 divided by 60 equals about 17 percent. The on to off ratio is 10 to 50, or 1 to 5. Dividing on time by off time gives 20 percent, which ignores the on time in the denominator. The 83 percent figure is the rest fraction, and 50 percent would need equal on and off times.
  4. The correct answer is D (The heaviest load lifted through full range exactly ten times with correct technique): The 10RM is the greatest load a patient can move through the full range for ten repetitions with good form, and DeLorme percentages are applied to it. It is a repetition-based maximum, not a percentage of the one repetition maximum. It is found in a single set, not by averaging across sessions or by comparing three rested sets.
  5. The correct answer is B (Stop the walk, seat her, coach pursed-lip breathing, and apply prescribed supplemental oxygen): In COPD, exercise is generally stopped when saturation drops below about 88 percent or symptoms are marked. Seating the patient, coaching pursed-lip breathing, and giving prescribed oxygen restores saturation safely. Continuing at the same pace or only slowing slightly leaves her desaturated. Raising the incline increases the metabolic and ventilatory demand and worsens the problem.
  6. The correct answer is D (Shoulder extension, abduction, and internal rotation, finishing with the arm down and away from the body): D1 extension reverses D1 flexion, so the shoulder moves into extension, abduction, and internal rotation and the arm finishes down and away from the body. Finishing across the opposite hip is D2 extension, which uses adduction. Keeping external rotation fails to reverse the rotation component. A flexion finish overhead does not fit an extension diagonal.
  7. The correct answer is C (An audible foot slap at loading response with normal clearance in swing): A grade of 4 out of 5 means the dorsiflexors can lift the foot in swing, so clearance is normal, but they cannot fully control the forefoot's eccentric lowering after heel contact, producing foot slap. Toe drag and steppage appear when strength falls to roughly 3 out of 5 or less.
  8. The correct answer is C (Lift and squeeze the muscle in a pincer grasp away from the deeper vessels): The sternocleidomastoid lies over the carotid sheath, so it is lifted and held in a pincer grasp, which keeps pressure within the muscle and away from the vessels and nerves. Pressing the muscle toward the spine or applying deep thumb pressure risks compressing the carotid. Percussion on the neck is also unsafe.
  9. The correct answer is D (Overtraining syndrome, with a catabolic hormonal shift requiring an extended reduction in training load): Six weeks of heavy training with fatigue, poor sleep, a raised resting heart rate and a falling testosterone to cortisol ratio that persist after three weeks of reduced load point to overtraining syndrome. Simple overreaching recovers within days to a couple of weeks, so it does not fit. Anemia can cause fatigue but does not explain the hormonal shift or the training history. Glycogen depletion would respond to food within hours, so it cannot explain these findings.
  10. The correct answer is C (A rise of about 2 to 3 degrees Celsius, the moderate heating level): Moderate heating of 2 to 3 degrees C is associated with reduced pain and spasm and increased blood flow. Vigorous heating at 4 degrees C or more is reserved for collagen extensibility, and mild heating of 1 degree C affects metabolism and mild inflammation. A 6 degree rise would damage tissue.
  11. The correct answer is D (Hip abductor strengthening with reduced running volume and gait retraining): Lateral knee pain near 30 degrees of flexion with weak hip abductors and a pelvic drop on stance points to iliotibial band syndrome driven by poor proximal control. Strengthening the abductors, trimming running volume, and retraining gait address the cause. Stretching and foam rolling have already failed, an injection with full mileage ignores the overload, and patellofemoral taping treats a different condition.
  12. The correct answer is C (Sciatic nerve tension contributes, so stretch with the ankle plantarflexed to isolate hamstring length): Tingling that eases with ankle plantarflexion while the muscle sensation stays the same is a neural tension response, since dorsiflexion adds tension to the sciatic nerve. Stretching with the ankle plantarflexed removes neural tension so the hamstring length can be addressed. Calf shortness, scar tissue, or a capsular restriction would not produce a symptom that changes with ankle position alone.
  13. The correct answer is B (Toe extension winds the plantar fascia around the metatarsal heads and tightens it at the heel): Extending the great toe shortens the distance between the metatarsal heads and the heel through the windlass mechanism, raising tension in the plantar fascia at the calcaneal origin. That reproduces pain in plantar fasciitis and supports the diagnosis. The flexor hallucis longus, tibial nerve, and Achilles tendon are not loaded by this motion in the same way.
  14. The correct answer is C (Laser light is coherent, meaning its waves travel in phase, unlike the diode output): A laser produces coherent light, with waves in phase, along with narrow bandwidth and low divergence. Diodes emit incoherent light over a wider bandwidth with greater divergence. Photon energy depends only on wavelength, so two sources at 660 nm give equal energy per photon.
  15. The correct answer is B (The tensor fasciae latae at the lateral hip): Janda lists the tensor fasciae latae among the tonic muscles that tend toward hypertonicity and shortening, alongside the iliopsoas, rectus femoris, piriformis and erector spinae. The gluteus medius, vastus medialis and lower trapezius are phasic muscles that tend toward inhibition and weakness. The lateral hip position of the gluteus medius makes it a tempting neighbor, but it weakens rather than tightens.
  16. The correct answer is D (Neutral spine core stabilization along with hamstring flexibility work): The first phase of pars stress reaction rehabilitation uses neutral spine core stabilization with hamstring flexibility work, avoiding repeated extension. Repeated end-range extension and loaded Roman chair extension both stress the pars. Plyometric landings are impact loads that belong in a late phase just before return to diving.
  17. The correct answer is A (Manual traction applied briefly with adjustment of angle and force by feel): Manual traction lets the therapist change angle, force, and hand position moment by moment and stop immediately if symptoms change, so it is a good screening trial. A heavy mechanical dose of 50 percent of body weight would be too strong at the start of the cervical spine. Inversion cannot be graded or stopped quickly. Sending a home unit before the response is known removes supervision.
  18. The correct answer is A (Single-leg foam stance with a ball toss, then with eyes closed): She holds single-leg firm-floor stance for 30 seconds, so the next step adds an unstable surface and a secondary task, then removes vision. Bilateral board work is easier than her current level. Longer firm-floor holding adds little challenge. Hopping at eight weeks adds impact load before dynamic stability is shown.
  19. The correct answer is A (Lower the intensity and keep the head moving until the ache resolves): A deep, sharp ache over a bony surface suggests periosteal heating, because bone absorbs much of the beam and reflects the rest back at the interface. The fix is to reduce intensity and keep the head moving so that no spot accumulates energy. Stopping movement or leaving intensity unchanged would worsen the pain.
  20. The correct answer is D (Craniocervical flexion, a gentle nodding motion as if saying yes): Craniocervical flexion is a low-effort nod that activates the deep flexors, which attach to the cervical vertebrae and the base of the skull. Forceful resisted flexion and supine head lifts are dominated by the sternocleidomastoid. A shrug trains the upper trapezius, which is not a neck flexor.
  21. The correct answer is C (Large afferent input activates inhibitory interneurons in the dorsal horn, reducing onward nociceptive transmission.): Gate control theory holds that activity in large A-beta afferents, generated by the sensory-level tingling of conventional TENS, excites inhibitory interneurons in the dorsal horn. These interneurons close the gate on nociceptive input carried by small fibers. The opioid explanation belongs to the endorphin theory of low-rate stimulation. Conventional TENS produces little heat and does not block C fiber conduction.
  22. The correct answer is A (Lower minute ventilation at a given submaximal workload): Endurance training improves ventilatory efficiency, so minute ventilation is lower at the same submaximal workload even though maximal ventilation rises. Vital capacity and resting diffusing capacity change little in healthy adults because lung structure does not adapt to training. The ventilatory response to carbon dioxide tends to be blunted rather than increased, so a higher resting rate is not expected.
  23. The correct answer is D (Effleurage, which assists venous and lymphatic return toward the heart): A long, smooth, whole-palm stroke with light to moderate pressure directed toward the heart is effleurage, which assists venous and lymphatic return. Petrissage lifts and rolls the muscle belly rather than gliding. Vibration is a fine rhythmic shaking, and friction is a small deep circular stroke aimed at local adhesions, so neither fits the description or suits mild ankle swelling.
  24. The correct answer is A (Gently contract the abdominal wall in all directions as if bracing, without pulling the belly in): McGill favors abdominal bracing, a co-contraction of the whole abdominal wall that increases spinal stiffness without drawing the belly in, for loaded tasks like lifting. Hollowing activates the transversus abdominis in isolation and gives less stiffness. Flattening the lumbar curve moves the spine toward flexion under load. A soft abdomen with a full exhale leaves the spine less stiff.
  25. The correct answer is D (Keep the load heavy but cut volume to one or two short weekly sessions): Research on detraining and maintenance shows strength can be held with much less volume and frequency as long as training intensity stays high. A heavy load once or twice a week fits her limited time. Lowering the load to 50 percent loses the key stimulus, walking does not preserve strength, and high volume light work is not what maintains it.
  26. The correct answer is A (The reasoning is flawed because 810 nm is invisible, so no blink or aversion response occurs): An 810 nm beam lies outside the visible spectrum, so the eye gives no warning and the aversion response never occurs. A brief exposure can still injure the retina at class 3B power. Sunglasses do not filter this wavelength to a rated level, so matched goggles are needed.
  27. The correct answer is D (Poor anti-extension trunk endurance, so train planks with a maintained posterior pelvic tilt): Pelvic sag and lumbar hyperextension with strong isolated hips and shoulders point to poor anti-extension endurance of the trunk. Planks with a maintained posterior pelvic tilt train that control. Hip thrusts and scapular holds address regions that tested strong. Pallof presses train anti-rotation, which is not the observed fault.
  28. The correct answer is C (Grade III or IV oscillations, large amplitude into resistance or small amplitude at the limit): In the Maitland system, grades I and II are used to modulate pain because they stay in the free part of range, while grades III and IV move into resistance or sit at the limit and are used to stretch stiff tissue. This non-irritable stiff capsule needs stretch, so grade III or IV fits. Grade I, grade II, or traction-based pain relief would not reach the restricting tissue.
  29. The correct answer is B (1 MHz at 1.5 W/cm2 continuous with a slowly moving sound head): A 1 MHz beam penetrates to about 3 to 5 cm, which suits tissue 4 cm deep, and continuous output at around 1.5 W/cm2 produces the heating wanted. A 3 MHz beam is absorbed within 1 to 2 cm and does not reach the target. Low intensity pulsed settings provide a nonthermal effect and will not heat the tissue.
  30. The correct answer is D (Rate near 100 pps, short pulse duration, intensity giving comfortable tingling without visible muscle contraction): Conventional TENS combines a high pulse rate near 100 pps with short pulses of about 50 to 100 microseconds at sensory intensity, which selectively activates large A-beta fibers and produces tingling without contraction. Long pulses with strong intensity describe brief intense TENS. A low rate with twitching intensity describes acupuncture-like TENS. A low rate at sensory intensity is not a standard TENS mode.
  31. The correct answer is B (600 seconds, or 10 minutes of total hold time per leg each week): Each session gives 4 repetitions times 30 seconds, which is 120 seconds per leg. Over five days the total is 120 times 5, or 600 seconds, which equals 10 minutes. Checking the work, 600 divided by 60 is 10. Using 120 seconds counts a single day, and 1,200 seconds doubles the total by adding the second leg.
  32. The correct answer is C (3 MHz, with most energy absorbed in the superficial 1 to 2 centimeters): Higher frequencies are absorbed faster, so 3 MHz deposits most of its energy in the first 1 to 2 cm, which is where the extensor tendon sits. The 1 MHz beam travels farther and is chosen for tissue 3 to 5 cm deep. Reversing the frequency and depth pairing is the common error when a student remembers that the two frequencies differ but not which way.
  33. The correct answer is A (Submaximal isometric contraction held 3 to 6 seconds, then a 10 to 30 second assisted stretch): PNF stretching commonly uses a submaximal isometric contraction of about 3 to 6 seconds followed by a 10 to 30 second assisted stretch, and submaximal effort fits a recent strain. The other options load the strained muscle maximally, use ballistic efforts, or reverse the short contraction and longer stretch pattern. Dose choices such as these should match the tissue state.
  34. The correct answer is A (Tibialis anterior working eccentrically to lower the forefoot): After heel contact the ground reaction force pulls the foot into plantarflexion, and tibialis anterior lengthens under tension to lower the forefoot gently. Weakness of this eccentric control produces foot slap. Concentric work by the gastrocnemius or tibialis posterior would not slow the descent of the forefoot.
  35. The correct answer is C (Vertebral compression fracture related to steroid-induced bone loss): Older age, long-term corticosteroid use, and sudden pain after minimal force make osteoporotic compression fracture the leading concern. Focal bony tenderness supports it. These patients need imaging and medical management before loading exercise or manual force. Disc, facet, and strain explanations are common but do not account for the bone risk.
  36. The correct answer is A (She needs targeted multifidus exercise even though the pain has already resolved): Ultrasound research on first-episode low back pain showed the multifidus does not recover spontaneously when pain resolves, so specific exercise is needed to restore it. Walking and other general activity do not retrain the deep segmental muscle. The problem is inhibition and atrophy, not shortening, so stretching misses it. General aerobic conditioning and sport return lack the targeted recruitment the muscle needs.
  37. The correct answer is D (Reposition the patient or lamp so the beam meets the plaque perpendicularly): Dose falls with the cosine of the angle of incidence, so a surface 60 degrees off the beam receives far less energy. Positioning the lamp or patient so the beam meets the plaque perpendicularly corrects the edge. Longer exposure for the whole plaque overdoses the center. Raising the lamp lowers intensity everywhere, and moving it closer raises the center dose sharply.
  38. The correct answer is A (Amplitude is below 1,000 microamperes, typically under the sensory threshold of the patient.): Microcurrent delivers current below 1 milliampere, usually tens to hundreds of microamperes, which is below the sensory threshold of most patients. The patient therefore feels nothing, unlike with TENS. A 1 to 10 milliampere output exceeds the microcurrent definition and is often felt. Sensation depends on amplitude and fiber excitation, so neither burst timing nor pulse duration explains the lack of sensation.
  39. The correct answer is B (Reduced plasma volume and stroke volume, lowering cardiac output at maximal effort): Bed rest quickly reduces plasma volume and stroke volume, which lowers maximal cardiac output and therefore VO2 max. Muscle capillary density and mitochondrial enzymes change more slowly over weeks. Maximal heart rate stays roughly the same, and lung diffusion capacity is not the limiting factor in this early loss.
  40. The correct answer is B (Defer the massage until the fever and infection have resolved): A systemic infection with fever is a contraindication to massage, so deferral until recovery is correct. Stimulating massage will not boost immunity and may worsen malaise. Lymphatic drainage strokes still circulate fluid during acute infection. Gentle effleurage also raises circulation, and the muscular aches are part of the illness.
  41. The correct answer is D (Cold pack with gentle positioning, because warmth indicates ongoing acute inflammation): Local warmth at 36 hours shows inflammation is still active, so cold remains the better choice and may ease the spasm. Heat would raise permeability and swelling in an inflamed area. Alternating hot and cold packs, or heating first, does not respect the acute stage.
  42. The correct answer is B (Cryotherapy for ten to fifteen minutes with compression and relative rest): A warm, swollen tendon within a day of overuse is in the acute inflammatory stage, and cryotherapy with relative rest limits swelling and pain. Hot packs, thermal ultrasound, and thermal diathermy all increase blood flow and can worsen swelling. These heating options fit later subacute or chronic stages.
  43. The correct answer is C (Fatty acids, providing the majority of energy for the resting muscle and heart): At rest, oxidative metabolism relies mostly on free fatty acids, which supply roughly half to well over half of resting energy, with carbohydrate making up most of the rest. Protein contributes very little in a fed or fasted resting state. Phosphocreatine is held in reserve for brief high-power efforts rather than steady resting demand.
  44. The correct answer is C (A long amortization phase lets the stored elastic energy dissipate as heat): Elastic energy stored in the eccentric phase is lost as heat if the transition to the concentric phase takes too long. A very short amortization phase preserves that energy and the stretch reflex contribution. The pause does not change concentric strength, the landing does not by itself cause damage that reduces height, and spindle activity would not be inhibited.
  45. The correct answer is B (Tensor fasciae latae substitution, so cue hip extension and external rotation): Flexion and internal rotation during abduction show the tensor fasciae latae taking over for a weak gluteus medius. Cueing slight extension with external rotation shifts the work back to the gluteus medius. A trunk hike, adductor squeeze, or more flexion do not remove the tensor substitution.
  46. The correct answer is D (Shorter phase duration is less effective at depolarizing nerves, so more amplitude is required): At 10,000 Hz each phase is very short, and short phases are less efficient at depolarizing nerve membranes, so the patient needs more amplitude to feel the same effect. Skin impedance actually falls as frequency rises, so impedance does not explain the weak sensation. The beat frequency still depends on the difference between carriers, not on their absolute value.
  47. The correct answer is A (Stretch the iliopsoas and rectus femoris, and strengthen the abdominals and gluteus maximus): Janda's lower crossed pattern pairs tight hip flexors and lumbar extensors with weak abdominals and gluteals, which tilts the pelvis forward. Treatment lengthens the tight hip flexors and strengthens the inhibited abdominals and gluteus maximus. Stretching weak muscles or strengthening tight ones would worsen the imbalance.
  48. The correct answer is B (The arteriovenous oxygen difference, because arterial oxygen content is lowered): Hemoglobin carries most of the oxygen in blood, so low hemoglobin reduces arterial oxygen content and limits how much oxygen the muscles can extract, which narrows the arteriovenous difference. Her heart and lungs are normal, so stroke volume and maximal heart rate are not primarily affected. Capillary density is a structural training adaptation and does not respond to hemoglobin level in this way.
  49. The correct answer is B (A hot pack is acceptable, since metal implants do not heat selectively with it): A hot pack heats by conduction at the skin, so metal implants do not concentrate heat as they can with shortwave diathermy. The usual precautions of sensation, circulation and skin checks still apply. A time cut or a hotter pack is unnecessary and the second adds burn risk.
  50. The correct answer is D (Over the vastus medialis, with visual feedback encouraging higher signal amplitude on each set): Surface EMG over the vastus medialis, with feedback rewarding higher amplitude, helps a patient who cannot voluntarily recruit the quadriceps. A relaxation threshold suits overactive muscles, not weak ones. Lowering vastus lateralis signal does not address recruitment, and hamstring recording reflects the antagonist.
  51. The correct answer is B (Toward the medial side, using a medial glide correction): Lateral patellar displacement is corrected by anchoring tape on the lateral border and pulling the patella medially, giving a medial glide. An anteroposterior tilt correction treats fat pad irritation and does not change lateral position. A superior glide is used for a low riding patella. A lateral rotation correction treats a medially rotated patella, so none of them addresses lateral displacement.
  52. The correct answer is B (Isometric, with strength gains mostly confined to joint angles near the trained position): Quadriceps setting produces tension with no change in muscle length, which is an isometric contraction. Isometric strength gains are angle specific, so they appear mostly near the angle trained. For that reason multiple angles are used when the joint can move. Isotonic and isokinetic work both involve joint motion, which the cast prevents.
  53. The correct answer is A (Type IIx fibers, which depend mainly on anaerobic glycolysis and the phosphagen system): Type IIx fibers have the fastest myosin ATPase and the greatest peak force, and they rely on stored phosphagen and anaerobic glycolysis, so they fatigue in seconds to a minute. Type I fibers are slow and oxidative. Type IIa fibers are fast but have more mitochondria and resist fatigue better, and Type I fibers are small motor unit fibers with oxidative metabolism, not glycolytic ones.
  54. The correct answer is D (Full passive knee extension equal to the other knee plus early quadriceps activation): Early extension loss is hard to recover and is linked to arthrofibrosis and poor gait, so full passive extension is the first priority, along with quadriceps activation and effusion control. Flexion progresses gradually and should not come before extension. Weighted open chain extension strains the new graft. Single-leg wobble board work is premature while the knee rests in flexion.
  55. The correct answer is A (Limited lower abdominal control, so train slow leg lowering with core bracing): When the legs lower, the abdominals must hold the pelvis and lumbar spine against the pull of the hip flexors, and lumbar arching at 60 degrees shows control is lost early. Training leg lowering with a held posterior tilt addresses it. Stretching alone, back extensions, or straight leg sit-ups do not.
  56. The correct answer is A (Five minutes of exposure at 30 inches from the same lamp): E1 is produced by about 2.5 times the MED. With an MED of 2 minutes, 2 minutes times 2.5 equals 5 minutes. Ten minutes is the E2 dose at five times the MED, and twenty minutes is the E3 dose at ten times. Four minutes is a simple doubling and falls below the E1 multiple.
  57. The correct answer is C (Conventional TENS to the neck region, keeping electrodes away from the abdomen and lumbar area.): Electrical stimulation is avoided over the uterus and generally over the lumbar and pelvic regions, but neck pain can be treated with TENS placed on the cervical area. Lumbar placement does not suit neck pain. Electrodes spanning toward the abdomen risk current near the uterus. A blanket ban on electrical agents is not required.
  58. The correct answer is B (Manual ischemic compression combined with passive stretching, without vapocoolant): Cold urticaria makes any cold exposure risky, so manual ischemic compression with passive stretching avoids vapocoolant altogether. Warming first does not stop the cold spray from triggering hives. Ice massage is direct cold, and a briefer spray from farther away still applies cold to the skin.
  59. The correct answer is B (Score the pattern zero and refer for clinical evaluation of the painful shoulder): A positive clearing exam with pain scores zero on that pattern regardless of how the movement itself looks, and the pain source needs clinical evaluation. Scoring the reach alone would hide a painful structure. Drills and delayed retesting do not address the pain.
  60. The correct answer is D (One knee bent, the other leg straight, and both hands placed under the low back curve): The McGill curl-up starts with one knee bent and the other leg straight, with both hands under the low back curve so the neutral lumbar spine is preserved. Hands behind the head, crossed arms and both knees bent change the load or neck and lumbar posture. The hand placement under the lumbar curve is the specific feature.
  61. The correct answer is A (About 3.3 METs, from 11.5 mL/kg/min divided by 3.5): The walking equation gives 0.1 times 80, which is 8.0, plus 3.5 for rest, a total of 11.5 mL/kg/min. Dividing by 3.5 yields about 3.3 METs. Omitting the resting 3.5 gives 2.3, and reporting 11.5 treats units as METs. The running coefficient of 0.2 would give 19.5 and 5.6 METs, which does not apply to walking.
  62. The correct answer is B (Superficial frostbite: stop the cold, rewarm gently, and arrange medical evaluation): White, waxy, firm, insensate skin after prolonged ice indicates freezing injury, so cold is stopped, the area is rewarmed gently, and medical evaluation is arranged. Reactive hyperemia is red and warm with normal sensation, and urticaria forms welts. Vigorous massage can further damage injured tissue.
  63. The correct answer is B (Lie supine and pull the right knee toward the left shoulder, flexing and adducting the hip): Flexing and adducting the hip, with the knee pulled toward the opposite shoulder, lengthens the piriformis. Pulling the knee toward the same shoulder adds abduction, which slackens it. Raising the leg in abduction shortens it. Prone hip extension stretches hip flexors, not the posterior hip rotators.
  64. The correct answer is A (Elevation about thirty degrees forward of the frontal plane with the thumb up, below shoulder height): Scaption is performed roughly thirty degrees anterior to the frontal plane, which matches the scapula's position on the thorax, with the thumb up so the arm is externally rotated. This lowers subacromial compression. Internal rotation with the thumb down and elevation above ninety degrees narrow the space.
  65. The correct answer is A (Postural education, pillow-supported side-lying rest, and gentle stabilization exercises): Conservative care for pregnancy back pain focuses on posture, positioning, and gentle exercise. Therapeutic ultrasound near the uterus, prolonged heat to the trunk, and traction with an abdominal strap are avoided. Prolonged supine positioning can also reduce venous return in the second trimester.
  66. The correct answer is A (About 18 miles of running before carbohydrate stores are exhausted): Stored carbohydrate energy is 400 g times 4 kcal per gram, which is 1,600 kcal. Carbohydrate burned per mile is 120 kcal times 0.75, which is 90 kcal. Distance is 1,600 divided by 90, about 17.8, or roughly 18 miles.
