Ignoring the carbohydrate share gives 13 miles, and lower shares give longer distances.
  67. The correct answer is B (Absent warmth is expected, since the effect is photochemical and not thermal): At 100 mW and 4 J per point, tissue heating is minimal and the therapeutic effect comes from photochemical changes in the mitochondria. A patient feeling nothing is therefore normal. Using warmth as an endpoint would push the dose into an inhibitory or hazardous range, and reflection is not indicated by missing heat.
  68. The correct answer is B (Weakness of the right gluteus medius, the stance side abductor): During single-leg stance the stance side gluteus medius holds the pelvis level against gravity. When it is weak the opposite pelvis drops, so a left drop on right stance points to the right gluteus medius. The swing side muscles and lumbar muscles are not the primary stabilizers for this task.
  69. The correct answer is C (The BESS using tandem and single-leg stances on foam): A high functioning athlete needs a task that challenges balance beyond the Berg ceiling. The BESS on foam stresses vestibular and somatosensory control. The Timed Up and Go is designed for mobility screening and would likely be normal. The Tinetti was built for frail older adults and also has a ceiling, and repeating the Berg with eyes closed is not a validated, normed test.
  70. The correct answer is A (Mild superficial warmth over the forearm, avoiding cold applications to the hands): Cold triggers vasospasm in Raynaud phenomenon, so cryotherapy to the hands is avoided and gentle warmth is the safer choice. Shortening the cold dose does not remove the trigger. A contrast bath that begins in cold still exposes the hand to cold, so it carries the same problem.
  71. The correct answer is A (A hinged brace with medial and lateral supports and adjustable range of motion stops): A hinged brace with medial and lateral supports protects the medial collateral ligament from valgus stress, and range of motion stops allow controlled early movement. A locked immobilizer prevents the early motion the physician wants. A patellar sleeve treats patellar tracking, not valgus laxity. A varus-directed unloader brace is for medial compartment arthritis and would not protect the ligament.
  72. The correct answer is B (Move all electrodes to the posterior neck and shoulder, away from the carotid sinus): Electrodes are not placed over the carotid sinus because stimulation can alter heart rate and blood pressure. The fix is to relocate all pads to the posterior neck and shoulder. Changing carrier or lowering intensity leaves the electrodes over the same vulnerable area. Moving the anterior pair to the sternum places current across the chest near the heart, which is also inappropriate.
  73. The correct answer is A (Withhold the walking program and refer him urgently for vascular evaluation of limb ischemia): Rest pain relieved by dependency, a non-healing ulcer, and a cool pale foot are features of critical limb ischemia, which threatens the limb and needs urgent vascular assessment. Exercise therapy for claudication is not appropriate until perfusion is addressed. Elevating the leg lowers perfusion pressure, and compression can further compromise arterial flow.
  74. The correct answer is C (Active insufficiency, because the finger flexors are shortened over both joints at once): A multijoint muscle shortened across all the joints it crosses cannot generate effective tension because the filaments overlap too much, which is active insufficiency. With the wrist flexed, the finger flexors are slack, so grip weakens. Passive insufficiency refers to an overstretched antagonist limiting motion, and no external overload is present.
  75. The correct answer is D (A positive Hoffmann sign with brisk lower limb reflexes and clonus): Cervical myelopathy produces upper motor neuron findings such as a positive Hoffmann sign, hyperreflexia below the lesion, clonus, Babinski sign and gait disturbance. Spurling's test, a reduced biceps reflex and the shoulder abduction relief sign all describe a single nerve root problem. Myelopathy needs surgical consultation, not a rehabilitation trial.
  76. The correct answer is C (Transversus abdominis drawing-in with splinting, avoiding full curl-ups and loaded trunk flexion): A midline ridge and a gap of three finger widths indicate diastasis recti. The first step is low load transversus abdominis activation with splinting, while exercises that increase doming, such as curl-ups and loaded flexion, are avoided. Rotation with a ball and prolonged planks raise pressure on the midline and can worsen the separation.
  77. The correct answer is A (Spastic gait from an upper motor neuron lesion, needing urgent referral): Stiff scraping legs with brisk reflexes, a Hoffmann sign, bilateral extensor plantar responses, and clumsy hands indicate an upper motor neuron lesion, with cervical spondylotic myelopathy a leading concern needing urgent referral. A basal ganglia gait lacks the pyramidal signs. Peroneal neuropathy and proximal myopathy would reduce reflexes or leave the plantar response flexor, so they do not explain this picture.
  78. The correct answer is D (A true VO2 max was reached, as oxygen uptake plateaued despite rising workload): The classic criterion for VO2 max is a plateau in oxygen uptake, usually a rise of less than 150 mL/min or 2.1 mL/kg/min, despite rising workload. A peak value is reported when no plateau appears, and the stem gives no exchange ratio data. Real maximal tests do not need uptake to rise to the end. A plateau is the expected sign of maximal capacity, not evidence that the workload was too low.
  79. The correct answer is C (Class 4 beams can injure eyes and skin from diffuse reflection and can ignite materials): Class 4 begins above 500 mW, and at that output even a diffusely scattered beam can injure the eye or skin, and the beam can start fires. Invisibility depends on wavelength, and pulsing is a delivery mode. Neither defines the class, and a larger spot does not lower the hazard of a high-power beam.
  80. The correct answer is D (Nordic hamstring curls performed eccentrically with a partner anchoring the heels): Nordic hamstring exercise loads the hamstrings eccentrically at long muscle length, which matches the usual injury mechanism and has been shown to lower recurrence rates. Light concentric curls and ankle weight work do not create enough eccentric overload. Static stretching improves range but does not protect against reinjury on its own.
  81. The correct answer is B (Supine with knees supported, low comfortable force, and gradual increases over visits): A supine, supported position lets the cervical muscles relax and removes the weight of the head as a variable, and a low starting force raised over visits builds tolerance. A seated high force would provoke more guarding in an anxious patient. Extension narrows the foramina and a fast ramp increases stress. A prone position with the head unsupported at the table edge is awkward and unsafe.
  82. The correct answer is A (The small head, because its ERA matches the small area and it can keep flat contact): The effective radiating area should match the target. The small head fits the 2 cm area and keeps flat contact on a curved surface. A large head overlaps surrounding tissue and loses contact, ERA does not set penetration depth, and a lower intensity does not compensate for poor coupling.
  83. The correct answer is D (Train the uninjured left arm with progressive resistance exercise several times each week): Cross education is the finding that resistance training one limb raises strength in the opposite, untrained limb, mostly through central neural adaptation. Progressive resistance training of the uninjured arm can therefore blunt strength loss in the casted arm. Resting both arms gives no stimulus, light high-repetition work is a poor dose for transfer, and massage does not drive neural adaptation.
  84. The correct answer is D (It raises intensity gradually, producing a smoother and more comfortable contraction onset.): Ramp-up is the time over which the stimulator raises output from zero to full intensity at the start of each contraction. A gradual rise avoids a sudden, jerky contraction and improves comfort and tolerance. Recovery between contractions is controlled by the off time. Ramp does not change pulse duration or carrier frequency.
  85. The correct answer is C (Stretch the pectorals and upper trapezius, and strengthen the lower trapezius and deep neck flexors): Correcting the upper crossed pattern means lengthening the overactive pectorals and upper trapezius and strengthening the inhibited lower trapezius, rhomboids, serratus anterior, and deep neck flexors. Strengthening the tight muscles deepens the posture. The other plans stretch inhibited muscles or load an already overactive one, which reinforces the imbalance.
  86. The correct answer is D (Press into the therapist's hand in an isometric hamstring contraction for several seconds, then relax): Hold-relax uses a brief isometric contraction of the tight muscle at end range, followed by relaxation and a new passive stretch. The contraction is held without joint motion, usually for about 6 to 10 seconds. Contracting the quadriceps describes the agonist contraction method, and moving through range against resistance describes contract-relax. Staying passive for a full minute is simple static stretching with no PNF contraction.
  87. The correct answer is D (From the muscle origin toward the insertion, with light tension and untensioned ends): The standard teaching is that facilitation tape runs from origin to insertion, applied with light tension and with the end anchors laid with no stretch. The reverse direction is used for tone reduction. A strip across the fibers has no directional intent, and heavy tension on the ends can lift the adhesive and irritate skin.
  88. The correct answer is A (The right limb, because its stance time is shorter than the left): Right stance is 0.58 of 1.10 seconds, about 53 percent, while left stance is 0.72 seconds, about 65 percent, and the asymmetry shows the right limb is protected by unloading it sooner. Antalgic gait shortens stance on the painful side. A normal total cycle time does not remove an asymmetry between the limbs.
  89. The correct answer is A (Lower intensity below the motor threshold, since contraction is not the aim here): In acute edema control the intensity stays below the motor threshold, because contraction can increase bleeding and swelling in a fresh injury. Reducing intensity is the direct fix. A low pulse rate pump belongs to later, chronic swelling. Electrode position and polarity do not control contraction.
  90. The correct answer is A (Greater extensibility of collagen, which allows more length gain with sustained stretch): Warm collagen becomes more extensible, so the same stretching force produces more length change with less tissue stress. Heat increases rather than decreases capillary permeability and metabolic rate. Heat also dilates cutaneous vessels instead of constricting them.
  91. The correct answer is D (The crossed extensor reflex, which extends the opposite limb so it can bear body weight): A painful stimulus triggers flexor withdrawal on the same side, and the crossed extensor reflex simultaneously excites extensors of the opposite limb so the body is supported on the other leg. Flexor withdrawal explains the right leg only. The tonic neck reflex is a postural reflex, and the inverse myotatic reflex relaxes a muscle rather than stiffening it.
  92. The correct answer is D (The iliopsoas and rectus femoris together with the thoracolumbar erector spinae): Lower crossed syndrome pairs tight hip flexors and lumbar extensors on one diagonal with weak abdominals and gluteals on the other. Tight iliopsoas and rectus femoris tip the pelvis forward while the erector spinae deepen the lordosis. The abdominals and gluteal muscles are the inhibited arm, so listing them as overactive reverses the pattern.
  93. The correct answer is D (Contact his physician about orthostatic hypotension and medications, and teach staged rising): A systolic drop of 32 mmHg with symptoms on standing meets the criterion for orthostatic hypotension, and his alpha blocker and diuretic are likely contributors. The falls occurred on rising, so the physician needs to review the medications while the patient learns slow staged transitions. Balance, vestibular, or device measures leave the cause of the falls in place.
  94. The correct answer is B (Automatic beat frequency sweep, such as varying between 1 and 100 Hz): A beat sweep automatically varies the beat frequency across a range, so the nerves do not adapt to a single constant stimulus. Interferential current is alternating, so polarity reversal is not an available strategy. Vector scan shifts where interference is greatest but keeps the beat the same. Changing the carrier alters skin impedance and does not prevent accommodation.
  95. The correct answer is B (About 138 beats per minute, from measured maximum and resting rate in the reserve formula): A verified maximal test result is more accurate than a prediction, so reserve is 172 minus 60, or 112. Seventy percent of 112 is about 78, and adding the resting rate of 60 gives about 138 beats per minute. Using the predicted maximum gives 151. Taking a simple percent of either maximum gives 120 or 133 and is not the Karvonen method.
  96. The correct answer is A (Delay plyometrics and continue strengthening until the effusion resolves and strength is adequate): Persistent effusion and post-activity aching show the joint is still irritable, so plyometric loading should wait until the effusion resolves and strength and control are adequate. Strength of four out of five alone does not clear him. Depth jumps and single-leg trampoline hopping impose even greater impact and instability demands.
  97. The correct answer is C (Moist heat warms mainly superficial tissue, so deep muscle heating needs a different modality): Hydrocollator packs heat by conduction and raise temperature only in the superficial centimeter or so of tissue. A deep structure such as the piriformis needs a modality that reaches deeper tissue. Extra toweling reduces heat delivery and never makes deep tissue warmer than skin.
  98. The correct answer is A (10 J, found by multiplying 0.25 W by 40 s): Energy in joules equals power in watts multiplied by time in seconds. A 250 mW output is 0.25 W, and 0.25 W times 40 s is 10 J. The other values come from misplacing the decimal point or skipping the milliwatt to watt conversion.
  99. The correct answer is B (Postural kyphosis, treated with postural strengthening and thoracic mobility exercise): A curve that corrects with extension and normal vertebral shapes indicate postural kyphosis, which responds to strengthening of the scapular and extensor muscles and thoracic mobility work. Scheuermann disease is stiff and shows wedging, and congenital kyphosis shows a malformed vertebra. Scoliosis is a lateral curve and is not described here.
  100. The correct answer is D (Pinch the muscle and angle the needle tangentially away from the chest): Pinching the muscle and angling the needle tangentially away from the chest keeps the tip in the muscle and away from the pleura. A perpendicular approach over the apex risks puncture. A deep breath lifts the apex closer to the needle. Passing through the full muscle thickness brings the tip toward the pleura even with a fine needle.

Practice Test 9 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is D (Cuff and scapular stabilizer strengthening with neuromuscular control training for several months): Atraumatic multidirectional instability is treated first with dynamic stabilizer strengthening and neuromuscular control, because the capsule is already lax. Stretching and mobilization add laxity. Surgery is reserved for failure of a long rehabilitation trial, and a sling does not improve dynamic stability.
  2. The correct answer is B (Lead down with the right leg first, then bring the left leg down): When descending, the leading weaker leg lowers the body while the stronger leg controls the descent, summarized as up with the good, down with the bad. Leading with the strong leg makes the weak knee do the eccentric work. A reciprocal pattern without a rail is unsafe for this patient.
  3. The correct answer is A (Use 2 to 4 pps with visible twitching intensity for 20 to 30 minutes.): Low-rate acupuncture-like TENS at 2 to 4 pps with motor-level intensity is thought to release endogenous opioids, giving a slower onset of 20 to 30 minutes but relief that outlasts the session. Conventional gate control relief tends to fade soon after the unit is stopped. Raising conventional rate or using brief intense or sensory-level burst settings does not create the prolonged opioid-mediated effect.
  4. The correct answer is A (The weight lifted is limited by the weakest point in the range of motion): A fixed weight must be light enough to move at the weakest point of the arc, so the muscle is under-loaded where leverage and length-tension are more favourable. Strongest-angle loading would stall the lift at weaker angles. Accommodating resistance belongs to isokinetic dynamometers. A fixed muscle length describes an isometric action, not a full-range curl.
  5. The correct answer is B (Tight hip flexors and lumbar extensors; weak abdominals and gluteals): The lower crossed pattern pairs tight hip flexors and lumbar extensors with weak abdominals and gluteals. The imbalance tips the pelvis anteriorly and increases lordosis. Reversing the groups or calling the abdominals tight would predict a flat back posture rather than the lordosis seen here.
  6. The correct answer is A (Advise against inversion because high blood pressure raises risk when inverted): Inversion raises blood pressure and venous pressure in the head, so untreated hypertension makes it unsafe. A shallow angle still raises pressure, and the risk is not limited to supine traction. A single reading under a high cut-off does not make an unmanaged resting pressure of 168 over 102 acceptable. Lacking glaucoma removes one contraindication, but hypertension remains.
  7. The correct answer is C (Postpone the session and refer him for medical review of his blood pressure): A resting systolic pressure above 200 mmHg or diastolic above 110 mmHg is a relative contraindication to exercise testing and training. The absence of symptoms does not remove the risk. Lighter cycling still raises systolic pressure. Resistance work raises pressure sharply, and a warm-up does not reliably lower such a high reading, so referral comes first.
  8. The correct answer is A (Ultrasound is contraindicated over the chest and device area because it may disturb pacemaker function): Therapeutic ultrasound is avoided over the chest and the pacemaker region because it may interfere with device function. Restricting the caution to the generator alone leaves the anterior shoulder too close. Demand type does not change that. Shortwave diathermy is indeed contraindicated, but that fact does not make ultrasound safe near the device.
  9. The correct answer is B (Energy is supplied mostly by fat oxidation, with a small carbohydrate contribution): The respiratory exchange ratio is about 0.70 when fat is the only fuel burned and 1.0 when carbohydrate is. A steady value of 0.72 therefore points to mostly fat, with a small carbohydrate share. A midpoint near 0.85 would indicate an even mix. Anaerobic metabolism pushes the ratio above 1.0 through buffered carbon dioxide, which is not seen here.
  10. The correct answer is A (Centralization, a favorable response supporting continued use of that movement direction): Centralization is the movement of the most distal referred symptoms toward the spine during repeated movements, and it predicts a good outcome. It identifies the direction of preference to prescribe. Peripheralization is the opposite pattern, with symptoms spreading distally. A lateral shift is a visible postural finding, and dysfunction syndrome produces end-range pain that stays in one place.
  11. The correct answer is C (Reduced lumbar flexion mobility, so add lumbar flexion mobility drills): A raise of 85 degrees is normal and the hips flex well, so neither the hamstrings nor the hip limits the reach. The flat lumbar curve shows the missing motion is lumbar flexion, which is the target of the mobility work. Strengthening hip flexors does not address a motion limit.
  12. The correct answer is C (Grade II, a neck complaint with objective musculoskeletal findings but no neurological deficit): Grade II requires neck pain plus musculoskeletal signs such as decreased range of motion and point tenderness. Grade I has symptoms only, Grade III adds neurological findings such as reduced reflexes or weakness, and Grade IV is reserved for fracture or dislocation. This patient has objective musculoskeletal signs and a normal neurological exam, so Grade II fits.
  13. The correct answer is D (Exhale during the lifting effort and avoid sustained maximal isometric holds): Exhaling during the lifting effort avoids the Valsalva maneuver, and avoiding maximal sustained isometric holds limits blood pressure spikes in a man whose pressure is already elevated. Holding the breath before and during the lift raises intrathoracic and arterial pressure. Long isometric holds, even at light load, raise pressure through continuous muscle compression of vessels. Near maximal loads strain the cardiovascular system and are poorly suited to him.
  14. The correct answer is D (The Arndt-Schulz principle, which describes a biphasic response with inhibition at excessive dose): The Arndt-Schulz principle holds that weak stimuli do little, moderate stimuli promote activity, and strong stimuli inhibit it. That biphasic curve is why laser dose has an optimal window. The inverse square law concerns distance, the Grotthuss-Draper law concerns absorption, and the Bunsen-Roscoe reciprocity law concerns intensity multiplied by time.
  15. The correct answer is A (Light, gentle effleurage only, avoiding deep pressure and percussive strokes): An INR of 3.8 with unexplained bruising means fragile vessels and a real risk of hematoma, so light gliding strokes are the safe choice and the prescriber should be told. Moderate kneading still compresses tissue enough to bruise. Firm stripping after a light start adds the same risk. Sustained moderate myofascial traction also stresses vulnerable tissue, and avoiding percussion alone does not make it safe.
  16. The correct answer is C (Roughly 20 to 40 degrees of posterior convexity in the sagittal plane): Normal thoracic kyphosis measures about 20 to 40 degrees by the Cobb method. Values above roughly 40 to 45 degrees are called hyperkyphosis, and values below 20 degrees are described as hypokyphosis or flat back. Mild increases with age are common, but the normal range itself does not shift to 50 to 70 degrees.
  17. The correct answer is D (Low-load chin nod holds that train the deep cervical flexors): Low-load chin nods target the deep cervical flexors, which are inhibited in upper crossed syndrome, without recruiting the superficial flexors. Shrugs and extension lifts train muscles that are already tight. Resisted protrusion loads the sternocleidomastoid, which takes over when the deep flexors are weak.
  18. The correct answer is D (Withhold ultrasound over the site until bleeding has stopped, then reassess): An expanding swelling shortly after trauma suggests ongoing hemorrhage, and ultrasound can increase blood flow and bleeding. It is contraindicated over active hemorrhage regardless of the pulse setting or technique. The area is treated with rest and monitoring first, with ultrasound reconsidered later.
  19. The correct answer is C (Skin impedance falls as frequency rises, so more current reaches motor nerves with less skin discomfort.): Skin behaves partly as a capacitor, so its impedance falls as frequency rises. At 2,500 Hz less of the current is lost crossing the skin, so deeper motor nerves can be stimulated with less sensation at the electrodes. Cycle duration is shorter, not longer, at higher frequency. Alternating current has no net charge, so there is no polar chemical effect.
  20. The correct answer is D (Nine points: one for each thumb, little finger, elbow, and knee, plus trunk flexion): The Beighton scale awards one point for each side for thumb, little finger, elbow, and knee maneuvers, which gives eight points, plus one point for forward trunk flexion with palms to the floor, for a total of nine. It does not include ankles, hips, or shoulders. Scoring only the dominant side would give five points and would miss bilateral hypermobility.
  21. The correct answer is A (Tai Chi classes with slow controlled weight shifting): Tai Chi, with slow controlled weight shifting, has randomized trial evidence for lowering falls in community-dwelling older adults. Walking clubs and aerobic dance improve fitness but do not target balance or reduce falls consistently. Seated or floor-based flexibility yoga lacks the dynamic standing balance demand.
  22. The correct answer is B (Slow the pace until her rating falls to about 12 to 13): Moderate intensity matches roughly 12 to 13 on the 6 to 20 Borg scale, and a rating of 17 with heavy breathing and no ability to speak shows she is well above it. Slowing to the moderate range corrects the prescription. A rating of 9 to 10 is very light and would under dose her. Added rest breaks leave the pace too high, and stopping for a stress test is unnecessary in a woman with no cardiac disease and no symptoms.
  23. The correct answer is B (An active gluteus minimus trigger point is referring pain to the lateral thigh and leg): A negative straight leg raise, normal reflexes and reproduction of the familiar pain with palpation point to gluteus minimus trigger point referral, which typically spreads down the lateral thigh and leg. Radiculopathy would usually show neurologic findings, and iliotibial band syndrome and piriformis syndrome have their own distinct examination patterns. Palpation that reproduces the familiar pain is the key finding that links the muscle to the complaint.
  24. The correct answer is B (Overload, applying a training stress that is greater than the tissue's current capacity to adapt): The overload principle says a tissue adapts only when loaded beyond what it is used to, whether by resistance, volume, frequency or duration. Specificity concerns the match between training and task. Reversibility describes loss of gains when training stops, and individuality concerns tailoring the program to the person, none of which states the requirement to exceed habitual load.
  25. The correct answer is B (Weak deep neck flexors, trained with chin tucks): A forward ear position relative to the line is a forward head posture, usually with weak deep cervical flexors and tight upper trapezius and suboccipitals. Craniocervical flexion training with a chin tuck strengthens the weak group. Stretching the deep flexors worsens their weakness, and shrugs load the already overactive upper trapezius. Thoracic extensor work helps kyphosis but does not address the head position here.
  26. The correct answer is A (The smallest exposure that produces faint, just visible redness on the tested patch): The MED is the shortest exposure at a set distance that produces minimal, barely perceptible erythema on the tested skin. Doses below it are sub-erythemal. Redness with edema and peeling is a second degree reaction at about five times the MED. Blistering is a much higher reaction and is not used to define the MED.
  27. The correct answer is A (Excess hip and knee flexion in swing to lift the toes clear): Foot drop from common peroneal nerve injury leaves the dorsiflexors unable to lift the foot, so the patient raises the knee and hip higher to prevent toe drag, a steppage gait. Circumduction, vaulting, and lateral trunk lean are compensations for stiff knees, long swing limbs, and abductor problems respectively.
  28. The correct answer is C (Grade I or II oscillations in the pain-free range to modulate pain, avoiding end-range loading): Constant night pain, pain before resistance, and lingering symptoms mark an irritable joint, where treatment should modulate pain rather than stretch tissue. Grade I or II oscillations in the pain-free range do this. Grade IV mobilization, prolonged stretching, or maximal contractions at a painful end range provoke guarding and increase irritability. Stretching progresses later, after pain and irritability settle.
  29. The correct answer is A (To expand the collapsed concave rib area and help derotate the trunk): Rotational angular breathing sends inhaled air into the collapsed concave regions, expanding the ribs there and encouraging derotation of the trunk. Directing air toward the convex hump would reinforce the prominence. The technique is a corrective breathing pattern, not a bracing or latissimus strengthening drill.
  30. The correct answer is D (A beat frequency of 80 Hz, found by subtracting one carrier from the other): The beat frequency equals the difference between the two carriers: 4000 Hz minus 3920 Hz is 80 Hz. Adding the carriers gives 7920 Hz, which the tissue does not feel as modulation. Either carrier alone is an input to the crossing, not the beat. The 80 Hz modulation is the frequency the patient experiences.
  31. The correct answer is D (Heat the tissue, then apply a low-load prolonged stretch held while it cools): Plastic lengthening of collagen is best achieved by heating the tissue, then applying a low-load prolonged stretch maintained while it cools. A brief high-load stretch produces mostly elastic change and risks tearing. Ballistic bounces cannot hold tissue at length. Heating afterward only relieves soreness and misses the window when tissue is most extensible.
  32. The correct answer is C (Muscle glycogen phosphorylase deficiency, blocking glycogen breakdown to glucose-1-phosphate): Absence of myophosphorylase prevents glycogen breakdown, so lactate does not rise on ischemic exercise, and cramps with myoglobinuria and a second wind phenomenon occur as blood glucose and fatty acids are delivered. Normal ammonia shows effort was adequate and rules out myoadenylate deaminase deficiency. Complex I defects give an excessive lactate rise, and carnitine palmitoyltransferase deficiency causes symptoms after prolonged exercise.
  33. The correct answer is D (The Biering-Sorensen test, held with the trunk horizontal off the table edge): The Biering-Sorensen test times how long the person holds the unsupported upper body horizontal, which loads the trunk extensors. The side bridge tests lateral trunk muscles. The curl-up and plank load the flexor side of the trunk.
  34. The correct answer is A (Neck flexed about 25 degrees with a starting force of 10 to 15 lb): Moderate flexion of roughly 20 to 30 degrees opens the posterior intervertebral foramina, and a first-session force near 10 to 15 lb is conservative for a 155 lb patient. Extension closes the foramen and may reproduce arm symptoms. Forces such as 50 lb are excessive for an initial trial, and flexion beyond about 30 degrees shifts stress away from the target.
  35. The correct answer is B (A rise of about 4 degrees Celsius above baseline, sustained during the treatment): Mild heating (about 1 degree C) speeds metabolism and mild inflammation, while moderate heating (2 to 3 degrees C) reduces pain and spasm and raises blood flow. Vigorous heating (4 degrees C or more) is needed to increase the extensibility of dense collagen, which is this patient's goal. A rise of 8 to 10 degrees would burn tissue.
  36. The correct answer is D (Posterior pelvic tilts with drawing-in and gluteal activation, plus hip flexor stretching): An anterior tilt with lordosis goes with tight hip flexors and weak abdominals and gluteals, so the plan lengthens the hip flexors and activates the lower abdominals and gluteals with posterior tilting. Anterior tilting and extension stretches reinforce the posture. Press-ups add extension. Anchored sit-ups and hip flexor strengthening shorten the tight muscles further.
  37. The correct answer is A (Withhold calf stimulation and arrange urgent medical evaluation for possible deep vein thrombosis.): Unilateral calf pain, swelling, warmth, and tenderness after knee replacement suggest deep vein thrombosis. Muscle contraction could dislodge a clot, so calf stimulation is withheld and the patient is sent for urgent medical evaluation. Lower intensity or compression stockings do not remove the risk. Electrical stimulation of any kind over a suspected thrombus is avoided.
  38. The correct answer is D (Unstable angina that has not yet been stabilized with medical therapy): Unstable angina that has not been stabilized signals active plaque instability and a high risk of infarction, so it is an absolute contraindication to exercise. Resting systolic pressure above 200 mmHg, left main stenosis, and asymptomatic moderate valve stenosis are relative contraindications, where benefits may outweigh risks under supervision. A therapist can still proceed with careful monitoring in those cases.
  39. The correct answer is C (Just proximal to the metatarsal heads, so it spreads and relieves the heads): A metatarsal pad sits just behind the metatarsal heads so it lifts and spreads them and takes pressure off the interdigital nerve. Placed directly under the heads it increases pressure on the painful area. Placed distal to the heads it loads the toes and does nothing for the heads. An arch support under the navicular addresses the midfoot, not the forefoot.
  40. The correct answer is D (Avoid the mole, refer for dermatologic evaluation, and proceed with the joint line points): An irregular, enlarging dark lesion needs medical evaluation for melanoma and should not receive laser energy. The joint line is a separate site and can be treated while the referral proceeds, taking care to keep the beam off the mole. A bandage is not a reliable barrier to scatter, and irradiating the lesion is inappropriate even at low dose.
  41. The correct answer is A (Brief cooling of the legs, while avoiding hot packs that could worsen symptoms): People with multiple sclerosis often worsen with heat, so brief cooling of the legs can ease tone and fatigue before gait training. Moist heat, a long warm immersion at the start of a contrast bath, and thermal ultrasound all raise tissue temperature. Any of them can aggravate her symptoms despite their general use for spasm.
  42. The correct answer is C (Reduce running volume, cross train with low impact exercise, and increase load gradually once pain free): Diffuse posteromedial tibial pain without focal tenderness or night pain after a sudden mileage jump fits medial tibial stress syndrome, a load problem managed by reducing volume, cross training, and building load gradually once pain free. Crutches and urgent MRI are for suspected stress fracture. A brief rest followed by the same mileage repeats the overload, and shoes or taping alone leave the load unchanged.
  43. The correct answer is A (Three net ATP per glycogen glucose unit processed anaerobically): Glycolysis from free glucose produces four gross ATP and spends two, netting two. Starting from glycogen, phosphorylase uses inorganic phosphate rather than ATP for the first step, so only one ATP is invested: 4 gross minus 1 invested equals three net ATP. The figure of four ignores the investment, and the large figure applies to full aerobic oxidation.
  44. The correct answer is C (Stop the stretch and examine the cervical spine and brachial plexus before repeating it): Arm tingling that appears with a neck stretch suggests neural tension or cervical involvement, so the stretch should stop and the cervical spine and brachial plexus be examined. Lowering overpressure but holding longer still provokes symptoms. Adding a nerve glide treats the symptom before the cause is found. A collar delays assessment and weakens neck muscles.
  45. The correct answer is B (0.75 meters, from 75 meters per minute over 100 steps): Speed is 10 meters over 8 seconds, which is 1.25 meters per second, or 75 meters per minute. Dividing 75 by a cadence of 100 steps per minute gives 0.75 meters per step, checked by 0.75 times 100 equals 75. The stride length would be twice that, 1.50 meters.
  46. The correct answer is A (Run the current for 20 minutes at 4 mA.): Dose equals current times time, so time equals dose divided by current. Dividing 80 milliampere-minutes by 4 milliamperes gives 20 minutes. Dividing the dose by two ignores the 4 milliampere setting, subtracting 4 from 80 gives 76 with no basis in the formula, and multiplying 80 by 4 gives 320, which inverts the relationship.
  47. The correct answer is B (Refer for neurological evaluation of suspected spinal accessory nerve injury): Shoulder droop, weak shrug, lateral scapular displacement, and winging worse in abduction fit trapezius weakness from spinal accessory injury, especially after neck surgery. The normal wall push-up argues against serratus palsy. A neurological evaluation is needed, and posture and capsular approaches do not address the deficit.
  48. The correct answer is D (Moderate risk, supported by ECG monitoring for a limited number of early sessions): An ejection fraction of 40 to 49 percent, a capacity between 5 and 7 METs, and exercise ST depression under 2 mm are moderate-risk features. Moderate risk usually warrants ECG monitoring for a limited early period. Low risk requires an ejection fraction of 50 percent or more, capacity above 7 METs, and no ischemia. Her findings do not meet high-risk criteria such as an ejection fraction below 40 percent or ST depression of 2 mm or more.
  49. The correct answer is C (Withhold heat and refer promptly for evaluation of possible deep vein thrombosis): Unilateral swelling, warmth, tenderness and a larger leg after surgery point to possible deep vein thrombosis, so heat is withheld and the patient is referred promptly. Heat treats it as muscle tightness and delays diagnosis. Stretching and walking could disturb a clot. Compression and elevation address swelling but wait a day while thrombosis remains unexamined.
  50. The correct answer is B (The modified curl-up, the side bridge, and the quadruped bird dog exercise): McGill's big three are the modified curl-up for the anterior wall, the side bridge for the lateral stabilizers, and the bird dog for the extensors, chosen for endurance with low spinal compression. Full sit-ups load the spine through the hip flexors and are avoided. The dead bug and the hamstring curl are useful exercises but are not part of the big three. The plank and the straight leg raise are also outside the set.
  51. The correct answer is B (A cushioned full-contact insole with arch fill and a metatarsal pad): A rigid cavus foot has little natural shock absorption and concentrates load under the heel and metatarsal heads, so a cushioned insole that fills the arch spreads pressure and a metatarsal pad unloads the heads. A strong medial post targets a pronated foot. A medial wedge that raises the navicular adds stress to an already rigid arch. A thin flat insert gives no cushioning.
  52. The correct answer is B (Positioning splints holding the ankles near neutral, plus regular passive dorsiflexion): In a sedated, bedbound patient the calf shortens in plantarflexion, so prolonged positioning near neutral with splints plus regular passive dorsiflexion prevents contracture. Brief stretching leaves the feet in shortening for most of the day. Splinting in plantarflexion preserves the shortened range, and a footboard cannot drive active dorsiflexion in a sedated patient.
  53. The correct answer is C (Random practice that mixes the three transfers in unpredictable order within each session): Once a patient can perform each task, random practice that interleaves tasks forces the learner to retrieve a plan each trial. Performance in the clinic looks worse, but retention and transfer to home are better. Blocked practice helps early acquisition, continuous guidance prevents error detection, and mental rehearsal with one transfer weekly gives too little physical practice.
  54. The correct answer is B (Alternate walking and jogging intervals on alternate days, building running time gradually): Graded exposure uses short running intervals broken by walking, with rest days between sessions, and advances volume only when symptoms stay quiet. This rebuilds tolerance while monitoring the response. Further complete rest causes deconditioning in a patient who has already recovered. Race pace on consecutive days is an excessive early test, and half mileage in continuous runs still skips the interval progression.
  55. The correct answer is B (Leaning the trunk forward so the force line passes in front of the knee): A forward trunk lean puts the ground reaction force line anterior to the knee axis, creating an extension moment that keeps the knee locked without quadriceps work. A posterior lean is the compensation for weak gluteus maximus, and a lateral lean is for abductor problems. Flexing the knee would raise the quadriceps demand and increase buckling.
  56. The correct answer is D (Ultraviolet C radiation with a wavelength near 254 nanometers): Because frequency rises as wavelength falls, the shortest wavelength carries the most energy per photon. Of the listed radiations the 254 nanometer UVC is the shortest. UVA at 365 nanometers carries less, red light at 660 less again, and near infrared at 905 the least. Longer wavelengths penetrate more deeply, but depth is not the same as photon energy.
  57. The correct answer is D (About 80 times lower at 4000 Hz, since the frequency is 80 times higher): Reactance equals 1 divided by 2 pi times frequency times capacitance. Since 4000 divided by 50 equals 80, and capacitance is unchanged, reactance at 4000 Hz is one eightieth of that at 50 Hz. That 80-fold drop explains why medium-frequency current passes through skin so readily. A direct relationship or equal impedance would contradict the formula.
  58. The correct answer is C (Shortened right hamstrings, limiting hip flexion by about 20 degrees): The test is passive, and the pelvis starting to rotate at 60 degrees shows the hamstrings are limiting the motion roughly 20 degrees short of the 80 degree norm. The iliopsoas lengthens during hip flexion and does not limit it. Weak hip flexors would not matter because the examiner supplies the motion.
  59. The correct answer is A (Use a hypoallergenic tape with minimal tension and remove it with great care): Steroid-thinned skin tears easily with strong adhesive, so a gentle hypoallergenic product with minimal tension and careful removal is the safer choice. Zinc oxide tape and high-tension strips both increase skin trauma, and a recent tear should not be covered with adhesive. Skin inspection should be repeated at each visit while tape is in use.
  60. The correct answer is D (Withhold hot and cold packs on the insensate skin and use exercise instead): With absent protective sensation, the patient cannot report burning or excessive cold, so any thermal agent over that skin is unsafe. Extra toweling and frequent checks reduce but do not remove the risk. Cold carries its own injury risk on insensate skin, and a mild contrast bath still exposes it to temperature changes it cannot feel.
  61. The correct answer is C (Standing on a narrow beam or foam while receiving a quick moderate nudge at the shoulders): The hip strategy takes over when the ankle cannot control sway, as on a narrow or compliant surface, or when the perturbation is faster and larger than the ankle can handle. A slow small sway on firm floor favors the ankle strategy. A large pull that carries the center past the feet requires a step.
  62. The correct answer is D (Falling stroke volume from fluid loss and skin blood flow, offset by a faster rate): Sweating without fluid replacement lowers plasma volume, and skin blood flow takes a share of cardiac output for cooling, so stroke volume falls over time. The heart compensates with a higher rate to maintain output, which is called cardiovascular drift. The pace is constant, so crossing a threshold is not the cause, and a larger extraction would reduce rather than raise the output needed. Blood pressure does not drive the rise in heart rate.
  63. The correct answer is D (Go to the emergency department now for urgent evaluation): Perineal numbness, loss of bladder sensation and bilateral foot weakness form the cauda equina pattern, which needs emergency imaging and often decompression within hours. Delay risks permanent bladder, bowel and sexual dysfunction. Heat with continued exercise and anti-inflammatory care treat it as routine pain. A scheduled outpatient scan within the week is far too slow for a rapidly progressing deficit.
  64. The correct answer is B (Defer passive stretching across the fracture site until healing is adequate and the surgeon clears it): Early callus is soft and not strong enough for stretch loads, so passive stretching across the fracture site waits until healing is adequate and the surgeon clears motion. Stretching too soon can displace the fracture or delay union. Isometric contractions still create forces across the site, and joint mobilization applies direct stress to a wrist with unhealed bone.
  65. The correct answer is C (Ice with compression and elevation, then gentle pain free motion): Warmth, redness, and swelling at day three show the inflammatory phase is still active, so cooling with compression and elevation controls edema while motion is kept gentle. Heat and thermal ultrasound increase blood flow and could add swelling. A contrast bath ending in warm water also favors edema, and heat is better saved for the later stiff, non swollen stage.
  66. The correct answer is D (Autogenic inhibition from Golgi tendon organ firing in the hamstrings after the contraction): In hold-relax the therapist has the patient contract the tight muscle isometrically, and the resulting tension excites Golgi tendon organs. Their Ib input inhibits the same muscle, so it relaxes briefly and can be stretched further. Reciprocal inhibition belongs to the agonist contraction method, higher spindle sensitivity would oppose stretch, and a few seconds of effort do not heat connective tissue meaningfully.
  67. The correct answer is A (Tolerance has developed, so raise the dose in small steps): Loss of erythema at an unchanged dose signals tolerance, so the dose is raised in small steps to restore an E1 reaction. Less redness does not mean more sensitivity, and halving the dose would lose effect. PUVA is a later option, but tolerance should be overcome first. A plateau with persistent plaques is not clearance.
  68. The correct answer is B (Poor anti-rotation control, so practice slow leg reaches with a dowel): Pelvic rotation and lumbar twist with a rising leg point to poor anti-rotation control, trained with slow leg reaches while holding a dowel. Hip flexor length tested normal, so stretching iliopsoas does not fit. Weighted crunches train flexion, not rotational control, and thoracic extension does not explain a twist at the lumbar spine.
  69. The correct answer is C (Remove the tape, treat as contact dermatitis and avoid reapplying that tape): Itching and vesicles in the exact outline of the strips are a classic contact dermatitis reaction to the adhesive, so the tape comes off and is not reapplied. Leaving it on prolongs exposure. A steroid cream under fresh tape continues the same adhesive contact, and a different strip over inflamed skin risks further irritation, so the reaction is treated first and a different support considered later.
  70. The correct answer is D (Hip flexion, adduction, and external rotation, finishing with the leg across the midline): Lower extremity D1 flexion moves the hip into flexion, adduction, and external rotation, which carries the leg across the midline. Flexion with abduction and internal rotation is D2 flexion. Abduction with external rotation does not form a standard diagonal. Extension with adduction and external rotation is the D2 extension pattern.
  71. The correct answer is B (Avoid both, since reduced blood flow cannot meet changing tissue metabolic demand): A foot with weak pulses and pallor cannot raise blood flow to meet the greater demand heat creates, and it tolerates cold poorly. Both thermal agents are therefore avoided. Heat does not improve supply in diseased arteries, cold carries risk, and contrast baths add the same stresses.
  72. The correct answer is D (2.0 mA per square centimeter beneath the active electrode.): The area of the active electrode is 3 times 5, or 15 square centimeters. Current density equals current divided by area, so 30 mA divided by 15 square centimeters equals 2.0 mA per square centimeter. Dividing area by current gives 0.5, and using the sum of the sides, 8 cm, gives 3.75. Multiplying current by area gives 450, which is not a density.
  73. The correct answer is D (It stayed the same at about 5.0 liters per minute because the changes offset): Before training, cardiac output was 72 times 70, or 5,040 mL/min. After training it is 60 times 84, which is also 5,040 mL/min, so resting output is unchanged near 5.0 L/min. The lower heart rate and higher stroke volume offset each other. Using only one changed term, or mixing old and new values, produces the 5.8, 3.6, and 4.2 liter answers.
  74. The correct answer is B (Avoid forceful stretching, manage the effusion, and restore motion with gentle pain-free active range): A warm swollen knee with boggy end feel and pain on overpressure is irritable with effusion, so forceful stretching is avoided and effusion managed while motion returns through gentle active range. Grade IV mobilization, maximal contract-relax and overpressure stretching all force an irritable joint and can increase swelling and pain.
  75. The correct answer is B (Stationary cycling in a flexed, forward-leaning position at low resistance): Cycling with a flexed trunk keeps the canal and foramina open, so the patient can build aerobic fitness without triggering claudication. Upright incline walking, downhill walking, and stair climbing all increase lumbar extension and typically reproduce symptoms. Cycling also allows duration to build gradually.
  76. The correct answer is D (Slow reversal, with resistance moving smoothly from one muscle group to its antagonist): Slow reversal alternates isotonic contractions of antagonists with no relaxation between them, which trains smooth reversals of direction. Rhythmic stabilization uses isometric holds, and hold relax is used to increase range. Rhythmic initiation addresses starting movement. The described alternating resisted motion matches slow reversal.
  77. The correct answer is A (Cerebellar ataxia, a wide base with uneven step length): A wide base, lurching, uneven step length, and dysmetria on finger-to-nose testing point to cerebellar ataxia, a typical result of alcohol related cerebellar degeneration. Vestibular loss produces veering rather than dysmetria. Parkinsonian gait has short shuffling steps, and waddling reflects proximal weakness with pelvic drop, so neither explains the limb incoordination.
  78. The correct answer is B (To maintain venous return so blood does not pool in the legs and drop pressure): A gradual cool-down keeps the leg muscle pump working so venous return is maintained and blood does not pool, preventing a drop in pressure. Lactate clearance does not prevent next day soreness. Glucose burning is not the purpose and hypoglycemia is not a cool-down concern. Excess oxygen consumption takes much longer than a few minutes to normalize.
  79. The correct answer is C (A lace-up or semi-rigid ankle brace worn inside the shoe during practice and games): Lace-up and semi-rigid braces restrict inversion while allowing sagittal motion, and they lower recurrent sprain rates during sport. Kinesiology tape offers mild sensory input but little mechanical restraint. Padded high-top shoes do not control inversion, and an elastic sleeve gives warmth and compression but no lateral support.
  80. The correct answer is C (Limitation of internal rotation, flexion, and abduction of the hip joint, with extension less affected): The hip capsular pattern shows greatest limitation of internal rotation, flexion, and abduction, with less limitation of extension and relatively preserved external rotation. This is a typical finding in hip osteoarthritis. The other combinations include motions that remain comparatively free in a capsular restriction.
  81. The correct answer is C (1 MHz, because its longer wavelength penetrates to about 3 to 5 centimeters): Therapeutic ultrasound at 1 MHz penetrates roughly 3 to 5 cm, so it reaches a muscle belly 4 cm deep. A 3 MHz beam is absorbed in the first 1 to 2 cm and would mostly heat skin and superficial fascia. The wavelength and attenuation both favor the lower frequency for deep targets.
  82. The correct answer is B (Perform a new MED test with the new lamp before continuing the course): Lamp output falls as a lamp ages, so a new lamp usually delivers more intensity than the old one at the same distance. Doses based on the old lamp may then overexpose the patient. A new MED test or radiometer check restores a reliable dose. Raising the dose or moving the lamp closer makes the overexposure worse.
  83. The correct answer is A (Agonist contraction technique, driving the quadriceps to relax the hamstrings by reciprocal inhibition): Because contracting the hamstrings hurts, the best option contracts the opposing quadriceps instead. Quadriceps activation reduces hamstring excitability through reciprocal inhibition and lets the therapist add range without loading the injured tissue. Hold-relax and contract-relax both require the injured muscle to contract, and ballistic bouncing stresses healing tissue and provokes the stretch reflex.
  84. The correct answer is C (The delivered dose equals 40 milliampere-minutes of direct current.): Iontophoresis dose is current in milliamperes multiplied by time in minutes, so 4 mA times 10 minutes equals 40 mA-min. Adding gives 14, which has no meaning as a dose. Dividing 10 by 4 gives 2.5, and dividing 4 by 10 gives 0.4, both inverting the product relationship.
  85. The correct answer is D (Firm, thin, non-slip soles with a low heel and ankle support): Thin firm soles with a low heel keep the foot closer to the ground and preserve sensory feedback, while non-slip tread and ankle support add stability. Thick soft soles reduce feedback and bare feet on smooth floors slip. High heels raise the center of mass and narrow the base.
  86. The correct answer is A (Deep neck flexors, trained with low load craniocervical flexion before any head lift): Chin jutting with prominent sternocleidomastoids shows that the superficial flexors are dominating because the deep flexors cannot stabilize the neck. The deep flexors are trained first with low-load craniocervical flexion before head lifts. Strengthening the already overactive sternocleidomastoid worsens the imbalance. The upper trapezius and cervical extensors are not the failing muscles.
  87. The correct answer is B (Limited community ambulator, since the speed is 0.6 meters per second): Speed is 6 meters divided by 10 seconds, or 0.6 meters per second, double checked as 0.6 times 10 equals 6. Perry's categories treat speeds under 0.4 meters per second as household ambulation, 0.4 to 0.8 as limited community, and above 0.8 as full community. Healthy adults average about 1.3 meters per second.
  88. The correct answer is A (Peripheralization of symptoms, so the force or position should be changed or traction stopped): Pain moving from the calf into the foot is peripheralization, an adverse response, so the force or position should be changed or traction stopped. Centralization would favor continuing. Peripheralization is not an expected early response, and adding nerve glides while continuing would risk further aggravation.
  89. The correct answer is B (A single layer of damp toweling between the pack and skin): Moisture improves conduction, so one layer of damp toweling lets cold pass efficiently and also protects the skin from direct contact with the frozen gel. Dry toweling and wool insulate and slow cooling, which is the purpose of layers in heat treatments. Plastic wrap sealed on dry skin adds little conduction.
  90. The correct answer is A (Four electrodes on two separate circuits whose current paths cross inside the treated tissue): The four-pole technique uses two independent circuits, each with its own pair of electrodes, positioned so the paths cross in the target tissue. The beat forms in the tissue where the two carriers interfere. Two electrodes on one circuit describes the premodulated technique. A single circuit with four pads or a shared dispersive pad cannot create tissue interference.
  91. The correct answer is A (Phosphocreatine breakdown, which rapidly rebuilds ATP through the creatine kinase reaction): Phosphocreatine donates its phosphate to ADP through creatine kinase, regenerating ATP within a fraction of a second and without oxygen. This system dominates maximal efforts lasting up to roughly 10 seconds. Glycolysis is slower to ramp up and takes over as phosphocreatine falls, while fat and glycogen oxidation depend on oxygen delivery and supply ATP at a much lower rate.
  92. The correct answer is A (Conventional settings near 100 pps with short pulses at sensory intensity, worn during activity.): Conventional TENS has a rapid onset and can be worn through the day because it uses sensory intensity without contraction. Acupuncture-like settings need 20 to 30 minutes to work and cause twitching that would aggravate painful rib movement. Brief intense mode is meant for short procedures, not all-day wear. Burst settings at twitch intensity would also move the injured ribs.
  93. The correct answer is C (Shorten the movement to a heel slide with the knee bent, keeping the arms still): Shortening the movement to a heel slide with the knee bent reduces the lever so she can keep a neutral spine while practicing the same motor control. A slower lowering over the same range raises difficulty, a towel roll substitutes for control, and overhead arms with both legs raise the demand.
  94. The correct answer is B (Restoring lead hip internal rotation to relieve compensatory lumbar rotation): Lost hip rotation forces the lumbar spine to supply the missing rotation, so restoring lead hip range reduces lumbar stress. Stretching the lumbar rotators would add motion where it is already excessive. Sit-ups and thoracic flexion do not address the stiff hip.
  95. The correct answer is B (Moist heat before mobility exercise and ice afterward if soreness or swelling follows): At four weeks without warmth or swelling, heat before exercise increases tissue extensibility and comfort, and ice afterward addresses soreness or swelling if it appears. Cooling first would stiffen tissue before mobility work. Avoiding heat is an acute stage rule that no longer applies, and heat afterward with cold before reverses the useful order.
  96. The correct answer is D (3.5 milliliters of oxygen per kilogram of body weight per minute): One MET is defined as the resting oxygen uptake of about 3.5 mL per kg per minute, so any activity can be expressed as a multiple of rest. The value of 35 is ten times too large and resembles a typical VO2 max of a fit adult rather than rest. The value of 0.35 is ten times too small. An absolute liter per minute figure cannot be applied across people of different size, which is why the definition is normalized to body weight.
  97. The correct answer is D (An intensity of 2 milliwatts per square centimeter at the skin): Doubling the distance cuts the intensity to one quarter because intensity follows the inverse square of distance. Eight divided by 4 equals 2 mW/cm2. A simple inverse relationship would give 4, and an inverse cube would give 1. Intensity cannot rise when the source moves away, so 16 is wrong.
  98. The correct answer is D (Quadriceps sets, straight leg raises, and seated knee motion in the allowed range without axial loading): Touchdown weight bearing prohibits meaningful axial load through the healing plateau, so exercises that do not load the joint, such as quad sets, straight leg raises, and range of motion, are used to preserve strength. Leg press, single leg squats, and treadmill walking all load the fracture beyond the order. A brace does not change the restriction.
  99. The correct answer is B (Hand behind the back, with the shoulder in extension and internal rotation): Placing the hand behind the back extends and internally rotates the shoulder, moving the supraspinatus tendon forward from under the acromion where the therapist can reach it. At the side, the tendon is covered, and overhead abduction jams it under the arch. Horizontal adduction exposes the posterior cuff region instead.
  100. The correct answer is B (Submaximal, pain-free isometric contractions of the ankle muscles at several joint angles): Two days after a grade II sprain the tissue is in the inflammatory phase, so loading must be gentle. Submaximal pain-free isometrics activate the muscles without stressing the ligament. Balance board work with eyes closed is a later-phase task. Heavy eccentric calf raises load the wrong structure too early, and resisted band work to fatigue provokes the inflamed ligaments.

Practice Test 10 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is A (Forty millimeters of mercury, with the patient resting in lumbar neutral): The standard baseline for the lumbar pressure biofeedback unit is 40 mmHg with the spine resting in neutral, so later changes in the dial show movement of the lumbar spine against the cuff. Other starting pressures or starting from a flattened spine or a brace would change the reference. The patient should rest quietly before the test begins.
  2. The correct answer is C (Cervicogenic headache, treated with upper cervical mobilization and deep flexor training): A flexion-rotation range under about 32 degrees points to C1-C2 dysfunction, and the posture-triggered unilateral pattern with neck restriction fits cervicogenic headache. Trials support upper cervical mobilization combined with craniocervical flexion training. Migraine, tension-type and cluster headaches lack the reproducible neck findings seen here.
  3. The correct answer is B (Isokinetic exercise, which accommodates resistance to effort at a set angular velocity): Isokinetic devices fix the speed and match the resistance to the patient's effort at every point, so the resistance falls when effort drops at the painful arc. A heavier fixed isotonic weight raises the load right where pain occurs. A maximal isometric hold at the painful angle and plyometric jumping both increase stress there.
  4. The correct answer is D (Denervated muscle needs very long pulse durations, so use a long-duration stimulator): Denervated muscle fibers respond poorly to short pulses and need very long pulse durations, often in the range of tens or hundreds of milliseconds. High voltage pulsed current is far too brief to excite them. Raising the rate, changing polarity, or enlarging the pad does not lengthen the pulse.
  5. The correct answer is C (The step-down becomes pain-free or markedly less painful immediately after the taping): The standard clinical test of a taping technique is an immediate reduction in pain during the provocative task, such as a step-down or squat. A snug feeling, resting patellar position or later swelling changes do not show that the correction has reduced symptoms during function. Static appearance and the athlete's description of tape tightness do not predict whether symptoms will improve during activity.
  6. The correct answer is A (Stable cavitation and acoustic streaming that alter cell membrane permeability): Nonthermal effects are attributed to stable cavitation, in which gas bubbles oscillate, and to acoustic streaming, which moves fluid near cell membranes and changes their permeability. Heating is the thermal mechanism, and nerve depolarization belongs to electrical stimulation. Unstable cavitation is a hazard that is avoided.
  7. The correct answer is B (Mobility first, then stability, then controlled mobility, and finally skill): The stages run from mobility, which is available range and initiation, to stability, which is co-contraction holding in a posture, to controlled mobility, which is moving within a weight-bearing posture, and finally skill, which is coordinated distal movement. Each stage builds on the one before it. Controlled mobility cannot come before stability because moving in a posture requires holding it first. Skill is last.
  8. The correct answer is A (Moist heat for fifteen minutes, then gentle stretching and active motion exercises): A chronic stiff neck without inflammation responds to heat that raises tissue extensibility, followed right away by gentle stretching and active motion. Cold before stretching lowers extensibility and suits acute irritation instead. Heat followed by rest wastes the window of increased extensibility. Low intensity pulsed ultrasound is largely nonthermal, so it does not prepare the tissue for movement as moist heat does.
  9. The correct answer is C (Phosphofructokinase, the enzyme catalyzing the committed step of glycolysis): Phosphofructokinase converts fructose-6-phosphate to fructose-1,6-bisphosphate, the committed and rate-limiting step, and is stimulated by ADP, AMP and phosphate. Hexokinase acts earlier and is inhibited by its own product but does not commit the molecule to glycolysis. Pyruvate kinase acts at the last step and is not the main controller. Glycogen phosphorylase supplies glucose from glycogen and belongs to a separate pathway.
  10. The correct answer is B (Age, sex, body weight, and heart rate immediately at the end of the walk): The Rockport equation uses age, sex, body weight, walking time, and the heart rate measured right at the finish of the mile. The end of walk heart rate reflects how hard the heart worked at that pace. Resting blood pressure, height, and RPE are not variables in the equation. A pre-test heart rate would not capture the response to the walk.
  11. The correct answer is A (Circumduction, an outward arc of the swing limb with no pelvic elevation): The three compensations all give floor clearance to a limb that does not shorten well. Here the pelvis is level and the sound heel stays on the floor, which excludes hip hiking and vaulting, and the limb arcs outward, which defines circumduction. Steppage uses extra hip and knee flexion.
  12. The correct answer is C (Modified curl-up, side bridge, and bird dog with brief isometric holds): The McGill big three train the abdominal wall, lateral stabilizers, and extensors using low spinal load and short isometric holds with a neutral spine. This builds endurance without repeated spinal flexion under compression. Full sit-ups and weighted twists load the discs heavily. Flexibility drills and fatigue-level loaded extensions do not match the stabilization goal.
  13. The correct answer is A (A bony block, so stretching will not increase range and should not be forced): An abrupt, hard stop with no give is a bony end feel, and here it reflects old fracture changes that physically block motion. Stretching or forceful overpressure cannot lengthen bone and can injure the joint. A capsular end feel is firm and leathery with some give. A muscular end feel is springy and elastic, and guarding feels like a muscle spasm stopping motion, so neither fits.
  14. The correct answer is C (UVC delivered at a wavelength near 254 nanometers): UVC near 254 nanometers is strongly bactericidal and is the band selected for infected, colonized wounds. Narrowband and broadband UVB are used for psoriasis and for stimulating healing, not for killing surface bacteria. UVA has weak bactericidal action. UVC penetrates only the surface, which suits a superficial bacterial load.
  15. The correct answer is D (Walking, neutral-spine abdominal bracing, and hip hinge practice with no external load): Until the surgeon clears heavier loading, rehabilitation stays within the lifting limit and avoids repeated flexion under load. Walking, bracing, and unloaded hip hinge patterning prepare him for later strengthening. Loaded deadlifts exceed the 10 pound limit despite strict form. Weighted sit-ups and loaded flexion stretches stress the healing disc.
  16. The correct answer is A (Avoid lumbar traction because distraction forces carry a fracture risk with severe osteoporosis): A T-score of minus 3.1 indicates severe osteoporosis, and traction forces applied through a harness and rib belt raise the risk of vertebral or rib fracture. Lower force does not remove the risk because the compromised bone is the problem. Inversion also loads the spine. A pain free interval does not change bone density.
  17. The correct answer is C (Use a pulsed, low-intensity setting or another modality, because the patient cannot report excessive heat): Patient feedback is the main safeguard against overheating in thermal ultrasound, so numb skin is a precaution. Lower, pulsed doses or another approach are safer. High-intensity continuous settings or a stationary head would risk a burn that the patient cannot detect.
  18. The correct answer is D (About twenty percent, made of two periods of about ten percent each): Double limb support occurs twice per cycle, once after initial contact and once in preswing, each lasting roughly 10 percent. Together they make about 20 percent of the cycle. Counting only one period gives 10 percent, and as walking speed rises the double support share shrinks until running removes it.
  19. The correct answer is B (Lateral weight shifting toward the left leg with mirror feedback): Asymmetric weight bearing reinforces itself, so training should drive loading of the paretic left leg through controlled weight shifts with visual feedback. Seated work builds trunk control but not standing symmetry. Right single-leg stance trains the limb that already works. Static wide standing with weight on the right repeats the faulty pattern.
  20. The correct answer is B (A medium frequency near 4000 Hz delivered on each of the two channels): Interferential current uses medium-frequency carriers, traditionally about 4000 Hz, because skin impedance falls as frequency rises and the current crosses skin comfortably. A 50 Hz current is a typical low-frequency value and meets much more skin resistance. A 100 Hz rate is also low and resembles a beat or pulse rate, not a carrier. A 27 MHz current belongs to shortwave diathermy, a heating agent rather than an electrical stimulation current.
  21. The correct answer is B (119 beats per minute, calculated as 0.70 times 170): Age-predicted maximum is 220 minus 50, or 170 beats per minute. Seventy percent of 170 is 119 beats per minute. Multiplying 220 by 0.70 gives 154 and skips the age subtraction. A maximum of 180 gives 126, and 80 percent of 170 gives 136, which does not match the prescribed intensity.
  22. The correct answer is B (Seated open chain knee extension against a lever arm at the ankle): Seated open chain knee extension fixes the hip and moves only the knee against the lever arm, so the quadriceps work with little help from other muscles. Front squats, leg presses and split squats are closed chain lifts in which the gluteals, hamstrings and calf share the load. They build the quadriceps but cannot isolate it.
  23. The correct answer is C (Mechanical traction is likely to reduce the radicular symptoms, so a trial is reasonable): When manual distraction lessens arm pain, widening the foramina is probably relieving root irritation, so traction is likely to help. Root irritation that responds to distraction is an indication for traction, not a contraindication. A reproduced Spurling pain points to a radicular source, not a muscular one. Relief with a pull does not suggest ligament laxity, which is tested with stability tests.
  24. The correct answer is C (Resynthesis of phosphocreatine and ATP plus the cost of a raised body temperature): Excess post-exercise oxygen consumption reflects resynthesis of phosphocreatine and ATP plus the cost of a raised body temperature, among other recovery processes. Lactate oxidation contributes but cannot explain the whole excess. Enzyme down regulation does not occur, since recovery raises metabolic demand. The old oxygen loan from the lungs idea has been replaced by this multifactor view.
  25. The correct answer is C (The pelvis tilted posteriorly and the lumbar spine flexed, pressing harder into the cuff): A rise above baseline means the lumbar spine pressed down on the cuff, which occurs with posterior pelvic tilt and lumbar flexion as the leg lowers. A fall below baseline would indicate anterior tilt and extension. Rotation is not reported by the cuff reading. Good control keeps the reading near baseline.
  26. The correct answer is C (Left pelvis drops slightly, held by the right hip abductors): In right single support the unsupported left side of the pelvis normally drops a few degrees, about 5 degrees total obliquity, and the right gluteus medius restrains it. A pelvis that rises on the swing side indicates hip hiking. A completely level pelvis is not normal, and a drop on the stance side is not the normal pattern.
  27. The correct answer is C (Weakness of the right gluteus medius on the stance side): In single-leg stance the stance-side gluteus medius must hold the pelvis level. When it is weak, the pelvis drops on the opposite, unsupported side, so a left-side drop while standing on the right means right gluteus medius weakness. The muscle on the swing side is not holding the pelvis. Gluteus maximus controls hip extension.
  28. The correct answer is D (Narrowband UVB at 311 nanometers without any oral photosensitizing drug): Narrowband UVB at 311 nanometers is effective for psoriasis and needs no photosensitizing drug, so it does not extend the sun sensitivity beyond the session. Oral psoralen leaves skin and eyes sensitive to sunlight for hours, which is risky for an outdoor worker who will not protect himself. Topical psoralen also sensitizes the skin. UVA alone without psoralen is not an effective psoriasis treatment.
  29. The correct answer is A (Avoid heavy lifting and repeated bending while walking farther each day): Standard early guidance after a microdiscectomy limits heavy lifting, repeated bending and prolonged sitting while encouraging progressive walking. This protects the repair and keeps tissue active. Lifting 25 pounds with a rounded back loads the disc too soon, and end-range flexion stretching does the same. Prolonged rest to week twelve is outdated and promotes deconditioning.
  30. The correct answer is C (Stretch pectoralis and upper trapezius, and strengthen deep neck flexors and lower trapezius): The upper crossed pattern combines tight pectorals, upper trapezius, and levator scapulae with weak deep neck flexors, lower trapezius, and serratus anterior. The plan stretches the tight group and strengthens the weak group. Reversing the groups, strengthening tight muscles, or choosing the lower crossed hip and lumbar pattern would worsen the posture.
  31. The correct answer is B (Do not place electrodes over the tumor site, and obtain oncologist approval before any stimulation.): Electrical stimulation is contraindicated over a known malignancy, because it could affect tumor tissue and hide important pain changes. Spinal metastasis also needs medical oversight. The clinician should avoid placement over the tumor and involve the oncologist before using any stimulation. Intact skin, endorphin rationale, or sub-threshold intensity does not remove the contraindication.
  32. The correct answer is A (Add a small increment of 1 to 2 lb and keep the repetition range the same): When a patient completes the target repetitions with good form and low effort on consecutive sessions, the load can be raised by a small increment, generally about 2 to 10 percent. Dumbbells come in coarse steps, so on an 8 lb weight the smallest practical increase of 1 to 2 lb is used and the repetition range stays the same. Moving to 30 repetitions changes the goal toward endurance, doubling the load is too large a jump for an irritable shoulder, and waiting longer withholds the stimulus she is ready for.
  33. The correct answer is B (Splint the ankle in plantarflexion, protect from weight bearing, and refer): A palpable gap, a positive Thompson test, and inability to rise onto the toes indicate a complete Achilles rupture. The immediate step is to splint in plantarflexion to approximate the ends, protect weight bearing, and refer for operative or functional management. Stretching and eccentric loading stress a torn tendon, a compression wrap with delayed review leaves the ends separated, and waiting for imaging leaves the tendon unprotected.
  34. The correct answer is B (Dense collagenous tissue such as tendon, ligament and joint capsule): Absorption rises with protein and collagen content, so tendon, ligament, fascia, scar and capsule absorb the most. Fat and fluid absorb little, and muscle is cooled by its blood supply. This is why ultrasound is chosen to heat the dense connective tissue of joints and tendons selectively.
  35. The correct answer is D (Low-load craniocervical flexion with biofeedback, progressed in small steps): A chin jut with strong sternocleidomastoid activity shows superficial substitution for the deep cervical flexors, so training uses low load with biofeedback and builds pressure in small steps. Resisted flexion strengthens the substituting muscle. High-effort chin tucks also recruit superficial flexors. Traction and passive stretching do not retrain deep flexors.
  36. The correct answer is A (Stationary cycling or seated arm and leg ergometry with well-fitted shoes): Loss of protective sensation means repetitive impact or friction can cause unnoticed ulcers. Non-weight-bearing modes such as cycling or seated ergometry deliver aerobic work without that stress, with properly fitted shoes. Jogging and hopping repeat impact loads on the foot. Barefoot walking exposes insensate skin to injury with no warning pain.
  37. The correct answer is A (Approximation, a compressive force through the joint to stimulate the stabilizing muscles): Approximation compresses the joint surfaces and stimulates joint receptors and stabilizers, which improves weight-bearing responses in quadruped. Traction separates the surfaces and facilitates mobility and pulling. A quick stretch initiates movement. Resisted rolling is a different task and does not compress the joint.
  38. The correct answer is D (Polarity makes no meaningful difference with a balanced biphasic waveform; amplitude and placement matter more.): A balanced biphasic waveform alternates direction each phase and has no net charge, so there is no true anode or cathode and no polar chemical effect. Swapping leads changes nothing meaningful. The effect depends on amplitude, pulse parameters, and electrode placement.
