Ion attraction and acid or alkaline reactions belong to direct current, and burn prevention is not an issue here.
  39. The correct answer is B (Drape the surrounding intact skin so only the ulcer is exposed): UVC is absorbed in the outer layers and burns healthy skin, so intact surrounding tissue should be draped to expose only the ulcer. A dressing or wet gauze over the wound would absorb the radiation and defeat the treatment. Widening the margin exposes fragile skin that has no need of irradiation. Draping keeps the dose on the target.
  40. The correct answer is D (A palpable taut band containing a hypersensitive spot that refers pain on compression): The classic criteria are a taut band in a skeletal muscle with a hypersensitive nodule that, when pressed, reproduces or refers pain. The 11 of 18 pattern is the older fibromyalgia tender point criterion and has no referred pain. Insertional or joint line tenderness points to tendon or joint pathology.
  41. The correct answer is C (Spray while the muscle is passively lengthened, then follow with moist heat): In spray and stretch, brief skin cooling is paired with passive lengthening of the muscle so the stretch is tolerated, and moist heat afterward restores comfort. Prolonged ice is a different treatment. Spraying without stretching omits the key step, and heating first reverses the usual sequence.
  42. The correct answer is D (Dizziness, diplopia, dysarthria, dysphagia and drop attacks with nausea, numbness and nystagmus): The five Ds are dizziness, diplopia, dysarthria, dysphagia and drop attacks, and the three Ns are nausea, numbness and nystagmus. They reflect posterior circulation ischemia and call for stopping manual therapy and arranging urgent evaluation. The other clusters describe radiculopathy, systemic disease and myelopathy, each with a different next step.
  43. The correct answer is C (Extended relative rest with sharply reduced volume and intensity until performance and mood recover): Persistent performance decline, raised resting heart rate, sleep and mood disturbance that continue after 2 weeks of lighter training point to overtraining syndrome rather than ordinary fatigue. Recovery needs prolonged relative rest with markedly lower volume and intensity, often weeks. More intervals add stress, a carbohydrate supplement does not fix the syndrome, and a 3 day break is too short.
  44. The correct answer is C (Grade 2, which is full motion with gravity eliminated): Completing the full range in a gravity-eliminated position but not against gravity is grade 2 (poor). Grade 3 requires the full range against gravity, which she cannot do. Grade 1 means a contraction without motion, and she moves the entire range. Grade 0 means no contraction at all.
  45. The correct answer is B (The smaller electrode carries the higher current density and produces the stronger stimulation.): Current density is total current divided by electrode area, so the same current spread over 2 square centimeters is ten times denser than over 20. The smaller electrode therefore feels stronger and is used as the active electrode. Equal total current does not mean equal density. Proximity to the muscle belly changes which tissue is reached, not the density at the skin.
  46. The correct answer is A (Early protected weight bearing with a brace and pain free motion): Functional treatment with a brace or semirigid support, early protected weight bearing, and pain free motion gives faster return to activity than prolonged immobilization. Long cast immobilization and delayed motion lead to stiffness and weakness. Stretching an injured lateral ligament into inversion works against healing and adds instability.
  47. The correct answer is D (Shortened latissimus dorsi and pectoral muscles with weak lower and middle trapezius): Arms drifting forward during an overhead squat with normal ankles, torso and full passive flexion suggest short latissimus dorsi and pectoral muscles with weak lower and middle trapezius. The shortened muscles pull the arms forward and the weak scapular muscles cannot correct it. The lower-leg and pelvic patterns are real faults but are normal here. Full passive range rules out capsular restriction, and deltoid weakness does not explain the drift.
  48. The correct answer is A (Expanded plasma volume that increases venous return and stroke volume): Plasma volume expands within days of starting endurance training, which raises venous return and stroke volume and lowers submaximal heart rate. Mitochondrial growth and capillary proliferation need several weeks. Structural changes in the ventricle also take weeks to months, so they cannot explain a response at ten days.
  49. The correct answer is B (Sharpened Romberg in tandem stance with the eyes closed): A sharpened Romberg in tandem stance with the eyes closed narrows the base and removes vision, making it more sensitive for mild dorsal column loss. Single leg stance with the eyes open lets vision mask the deficit. Timed Up and Go measures dynamic mobility. Repeating the standard Romberg with the eyes open and head turns adds visual input and does not challenge proprioception.
  50. The correct answer is D (Treat the muscle with the beam directed away from the testes, or choose another modality): The testes are sensitive to ultrasound, so the beam should be directed away from them or another modality chosen for an origin this close. Pulsing lowers average heating but does not remove the risk. A 3 MHz beam is shallow yet still affects nearby superficial tissue, and angling toward the pubic bone at lower intensity still aims energy at the groin.
  51. The correct answer is B (Withhold lumbar traction and offer a pelvic support belt with positioning advice): Pregnancy is a contraindication to lumbar traction because the belts and pull stress the abdomen and pelvis, so a pelvic support belt and positioning advice are better options. Lower force, leg elevation, pillow support, or a small inversion angle do not remove the risk and some of these positions are unsafe at 26 weeks. The belt gives support without adding distraction forces to a pregnant abdomen.
  52. The correct answer is C (Talocrural joint mobilization with a posterior glide of the talus on the fixed tibia): Dorsiflexion limited equally with the knee flexed or extended, with a firm capsular end feel and a reduced posterior talar glide, points to a joint restriction rather than gastrocnemius shortening. Dorsiflexion needs the talus to glide posteriorly on the tibia, so posterior glide mobilization is the match. Calf stretching targets muscle length, which is not the main problem, and an anterior glide is the wrong direction.
  53. The correct answer is C (Small slow oxidative motor units fire first, and larger units are added as force rises.): Henneman's size principle says small slow oxidative units with low thresholds fire first, and larger units join as force demand rises. Tying recruitment to movement speed confuses the size principle with ballistic strategies. Putting large fast units first reverses the order. Cell body distance plays no role in recruitment order.
  54. The correct answer is D (Withhold exercise and refer for urgent cardiac evaluation): Exertional mid-back and jaw pain with sweating that eases with rest is angina until proven otherwise, and full thoracic motion with no pain on palpation argues against a musculoskeletal source. Exercise is withheld and urgent cardiac evaluation arranged. Mobilizing first, imaging the spine, or sending him for a dental opinion while he keeps exerting all delay recognition of a possible cardiac event.
  55. The correct answer is B (About 80 degrees of hip flexion before the pelvis begins to rotate): Kendall's norm for hamstring length is about 80 degrees of hip flexion with the knee extended and the pelvis kept still. Less than that suggests shortness. The 120 degree figure belongs to passive hip flexion with the knee bent, which is not limited by the hamstrings.
  56. The correct answer is D (Remove or cover reflective surfaces and keep the beam from striking them): Shiny surfaces can redirect a class 4 beam into eyes elsewhere in the room, so they are removed or covered and the beam is kept off them. Dimming the lights dilates pupils and increases retinal exposure. Eyewear is required for everyone present, and a class 4 beam stays hazardous at a meter.
  57. The correct answer is A (Poor foot sensation shifts reliance to vision, which dim light degrades): Neuropathy degrades the feet and ankle signals, so the nervous system weights vision more heavily. Low light reduces the quality of visual input, which leaves too little reliable information for balance. Dim light does not act on the vestibular system, and cataracts affect vision rather than the labyrinth.
  58. The correct answer is A (Short rectus femoris, so stretch it with the pelvis held posteriorly tilted): Knee flexion in prone stretches the rectus femoris over the hip, and a short muscle tips the pelvis forward and extends the lumbar spine. The corrective stretch must hold the pelvis posteriorly tilted. Hamstrings are slackened by knee flexion, and the test is passive, so quadriceps weakness is not involved.
  59. The correct answer is B (Interrupted direct current with pulse duration above 100 ms at 1 to 10 pulses per second.): Denervated muscle fibers respond directly to current, but they have a very long chronaxie, so they need long pulses, over 100 ms, at low rates such as 1 to 10 pulses per second. Interrupted direct current provides these long monophasic pulses. Short biphasic pulses and Russian stimulation rely on an intact motor nerve and fail to produce a contraction here. Conventional TENS is designed for sensory pain control.
  60. The correct answer is B (After about 5 minutes, then at intervals throughout the treatment): The pack is hottest early in treatment and heat builds in the skin, so the skin is inspected after the first 5 minutes and the patient is asked about comfort. A first check at 10 or 15 minutes misses the period when a burn is most likely to start. Checking only at removal finds damage after it has occurred.
  61. The correct answer is D (Massage can reduce perceived soreness and tension, but it does not speed lactate clearance): Research has not shown that massage increases lactate removal, and lactate also returns to baseline within about an hour on its own. Its benefits are subjective, reduced perceived soreness and tension, along with relaxation. It does not heat muscle meaningfully and does not accelerate glycogen resynthesis.
  62. The correct answer is C (Wrist in neutral, near zero to five degrees of extension, with fingers left free): Carpal tunnel pressure is lowest with the wrist near neutral, so night splints hold it at about zero to five degrees of extension. Flexion and extension both raise pressure inside the tunnel. A thumb abduction and ulnar deviation posture is not a carpal tunnel splint position.
  63. The correct answer is A (Stop the treadmill, seat or recline him, monitor pressure and rhythm, and notify the physician): A systolic drop with increasing workload, together with pallor and dizziness, signals inadequate cardiac output and is a criterion to stop exercise. He should be seated or reclined, and pressure and rhythm monitored while the physician is notified. Continuing at a lower speed or walking around risks syncope. Water and further staging do not address the cause.
  64. The correct answer is C (Light jogging followed by leg swings and walking lunges through progressively larger ranges): Before explosive work, a general warm-up followed by dynamic movements raises tissue temperature and rehearses the sprint pattern through progressively larger ranges. Long static holds, often longer than 60 seconds per muscle, can transiently reduce force and power. Partner PNF at end range is a therapeutic method and is not specific to warm-up. Forceful bouncing adds injury risk without a movement-specific benefit.
  65. The correct answer is C (Cross-body posterior capsule stretching combined with scapular stabilizer strengthening): Limited cross-body adduction and lost internal rotation point to a tight posterior capsule, which pushes the humeral head upward and forward and contributes to impingement. Stretching the posterior capsule and strengthening the scapular stabilizers address this. Stretching the anterior capsule, end-range subscapularis work, and heavy shrugs miss the deficit.
  66. The correct answer is C (The goggles do not cover 660 nm, so matched 660 nm eyewear is needed first): Optical density ratings apply only across the wavelength range printed on the goggles, here 780 to 1100 nm. A 660 nm beam falls outside that band, so the goggles may transmit it nearly unattenuated. Red class 3B light is still an eye hazard, and a power threshold does not waive wavelength matching.
  67. The correct answer is C (Greater mitochondrial density and oxidative enzyme activity, reducing reliance on glycolysis): Aerobic training increases mitochondrial number and oxidative enzymes such as succinate dehydrogenase and citrate synthase, so more ATP comes from oxidation and less pyruvate is converted to lactate at the same absolute pace. This can occur with little change in VO2max. Maximal heart rate does not rise, and greater glycolytic capacity would raise rather than lower lactate.
  68. The correct answer is B (The greater trochanter, slightly anterior to the knee axis and the lateral malleolus): Kendall's ideal side view places the plumb line through the ear lobe, shoulder joint, greater trochanter, slightly anterior to the knee axis and slightly anterior to the lateral malleolus. The other options describe landmarks that lie behind or off this line. Deviations from it point to forward head, kyphosis, or pelvic tilt.
  69. The correct answer is C (Transverse strokes across the tendon with the tendon placed on a slight stretch): Deep friction massage is applied transversely across the tendon fibers with the tendon on a slight stretch, so the tendon is pressed against the underlying bone. Slack tendon reduces effect, parallel strokes do not break adhesions across fibers, and broad kneading of the bulk is general massage.
  70. The correct answer is D (A total score below 45 points out of the possible 56): The Berg Balance Scale has 14 items scored 0 to 4, for a maximum of 56, and a total below 45 is the commonly used threshold for increased fall risk. The cutoff near 19 belongs to the Tinetti tool and to the Dynamic Gait Index, which use different totals. A score under 21 on the Berg marks only the highest risk band, so it would miss many patients who are already at risk.
  71. The correct answer is A (Warm for 3 to 4 minutes, then cold for 1 minute, repeated): Contrast baths use longer warm immersions and short cold immersions, typically 3 to 4 minutes warm to 1 minute cold, repeated for several cycles. Reversing the ratio leaves the hand mostly in cold. Equal long immersions are not standard, and 30 second dips are too brief to change vessel behavior.
  72. The correct answer is B (Negative electrode in the wound bed, as cathodal current is bacteriostatic): The standard textbook choice for an infected or inflamed wound is the negative electrode in the wound bed, since cathodal stimulation is described as bacteriostatic. Positive polarity is associated with later epithelial growth, not infection control. A monophasic current does not alternate, and polarity matters in this current.
  73. The correct answer is D (Continue walking until the pain reaches moderate, about 3 of 4, then rest until relieved): The standard claudication protocol has the patient walk until pain reaches a moderate level, about 3 on the 4-point scale, then rest until the pain is relieved and repeat. This repeated ischemic stimulus drives improved walking capacity. Stopping at the first twinge is too conservative and pushing to severe pain is not recommended. Claudication pain reflects reversible ischemia, not tissue damage.
  74. The correct answer is D (Supine strap hamstring stretch held 30 to 60 seconds, repeated two to four times): Loaded spinal flexion raises vertebral compression fracture risk in osteoporosis, so a supine strap stretch, which keeps the spine unloaded, is safer. Older adults may benefit from holds toward 30 to 60 seconds, repeated two to four times. Standing and long-sitting forward bends load the flexed spine, and ballistic bouncing adds shear and compression risk.
  75. The correct answer is C (Passive motion is limited about as much as active motion, especially external rotation): In adhesive capsulitis the capsule limits motion, so passive range is restricted as much as active range, with external rotation lost first and a firm end feel. In a cuff tear, active motion is lost while passive motion is nearly full. A drop arm sign and painful resisted abduction point toward tendon injury.
  76. The correct answer is B (Continue the gentle abdominal contraction while breathing normally with relaxed ribs): The contraction should be sustained at low effort while the patient breathes normally, so the deep muscle works independently of respiration and the superficial muscles stay quiet. Breath holding in or out cannot be sustained for ten seconds and raises tension. Drawing the ribs down firmly recruits the upper abdominals.
  77. The correct answer is B (A longer step length, from about 0.54 meters to about 0.71 meters): Speed equals step length times step rate. Cadence barely changed, from 100 to 102 steps per minute, so the gain comes from step length rising from about 0.54 to 0.71 meters. A cadence of 120 or step time near 0.45 seconds contradicts the numbers, and a length near 0.60 meters would not yield 1.20 meters per second.
  78. The correct answer is D (Heart rate reserve times the desired intensity fraction, then add the resting rate): The Karvonen method first finds heart rate reserve as maximal minus resting heart rate, multiplies it by the chosen intensity fraction, and then adds the resting rate back. Adding resting rate restores the baseline that was removed when the reserve was calculated. A straight percentage of maximum is a different method that ignores resting rate. Subtracting resting rate or multiplying resting rate by the fraction would give targets that fall below real working heart rates.
  79. The correct answer is B (Thoracic manipulation or mobilization added to neck and scapular strengthening): For mechanical neck pain with stiff mid thoracic segments, trials support adding thoracic mobilization or manipulation to neck and scapular strengthening. Therapeutic ultrasound is a passive modality with little support for lasting benefit. Kinesiology tape shows little effect on pain or disability in this condition. Intermittent traction added to stretching has limited evidence, so none of them matches the support for thoracic manual therapy.
  80. The correct answer is D (Gluteus minimus, with a taut band in the anterior gluteal region): Gluteus minimus trigger points produce a lateral thigh and leg pain pattern that mimics sciatica but spares the foot and lacks neurologic signs. Quadratus lumborum refers to the iliac crest and greater trochanter, piriformis to the sacroiliac area and buttock, and tensor fasciae latae mostly to the lateral thigh and not to the calf. A palpable band in the anterior gluteal region and a pain path to the ankle that spares the foot complete the picture.
  81. The correct answer is B (Split the region into fields of about two to three times the ERA and treat each): Heating requires the energy to be concentrated, so the field should be roughly two to three times the ERA, here about 8 to 12 cm2. A 25 cm2 area is over six times the ERA, so it is split into sections. Raising intensity risks hot spots and a frequency change does not alter the ERA.
  82. The correct answer is D (Use amplitude or frequency modulation, or periodically raise the intensity during the session.): Accommodation occurs when the nervous system adapts to a constant stimulus, so modulating amplitude or frequency, or raising intensity as the sensation fades, keeps the input changing and restores effect. Dropping below the sensory threshold removes the stimulus. Larger electrodes lower current density and weaken the input. TENS uses balanced biphasic or pulsed waveforms, so no charge accumulates beneath one pad.
  83. The correct answer is A (Stop emission, then resume at lower power with the probe kept moving over the area): Heavily pigmented skin absorbs more of the beam in melanin, so a stationary 400 mW probe can produce heating that is not therapeutic. The safe step is to stop, lower the power, and keep the probe moving to spread the energy. Burning heat is a warning, pressure does not cool the skin, and melanin absorbs more strongly at shorter wavelengths.
  84. The correct answer is C (A short amortization phase between the eccentric loading and the concentric push off): In the stretch shortening cycle a rapid eccentric stretch loads the muscle and spindles, and a very short amortization phase lets stored elastic energy and the stretch reflex add to the concentric push off. A held squat or a long amortization phase lets that energy dissipate. A full stop before each jump removes the stretch reflex contribution. Short ground contact is the hallmark of good plyometric technique.
  85. The correct answer is D (Pectoralis major and minor, upper trapezius, and levator scapulae): Janda's upper crossed syndrome pairs tight pectorals, upper trapezius, and levator scapulae with weak deep neck flexors, rhomboids, middle and lower trapezius, and serratus anterior. The tight muscles pull the head forward and round the shoulders. The remaining options describe the weak group or muscles outside the pattern.
  86. The correct answer is C (Slow eccentric lowering from full extension, assisted up by the therapist): He cannot lift against a 2 lb cuff but controls lowering, so eccentric loading from full extension with help on the way up uses the strength he has. Hamstring curls train the antagonist, not the weak quadriceps. Isometric sets alone skip the usable eccentric strength, and fast isokinetic testing is a measure, not a strengthening approach.
  87. The correct answer is C (Right gluteus maximus, which normally controls hip flexion at initial contact): Gluteus maximus keeps the trunk upright by controlling hip flexion at initial contact and loading. When it is weak, the patient throws the trunk back to hold the center of mass behind the hip, producing a posterior trunk thrust. The stem rules out quadriceps weakness, and abductor weakness would show lateral pelvic drop or lean.
  88. The correct answer is C (To provide fixed attachment points for later strips, applied without tension): Anchors are applied on the proximal leg and the midfoot to give the supporting strips a stable base to adhere to. They are laid without much tension, because a tight circumferential anchor can impair circulation. Stirrups provide the main medial and lateral stability, so the anchors themselves do not restrict inversion or eversion.
  89. The correct answer is D (Until the skin is numb, usually within about 5 to 10 minutes): Ice massage continues until the skin is numb, which usually takes about 5 to 10 minutes over a small area. Redness appears early, before analgesia, and aching and burning are intermediate sensations that come before numbness. Twenty minutes is the dose for a cold pack, and ice massage over that long risks excessive cooling.
  90. The correct answer is D (Pause treatment, re-moisten the gauze with saline, and reset intensity before resuming): Dry gauze raises impedance and concentrates current, causing burning. The treatment is paused, the gauze re-moistened with saline, and intensity reset to a comfortable level before resuming. Raising intensity worsens the problem, gel is not the standard interface in a wound, and reversing polarity changes the biology of the treatment while ignoring the dry interface.
  91. The correct answer is D (Impairments in a remote body region can contribute to the patient's presenting symptoms): Regional interdependence holds that impairments in a remote region, such as a stiff thoracic spine or weak hips, can contribute to symptoms elsewhere. Examination therefore includes regions beyond the painful one. Treating only the local site ignores this idea. Dermatomal referral describes how pain is felt, and screening for visceral causes is red flag practice, so both are real concepts that are not this definition.
  92. The correct answer is A (Substantial fall risk, since a reach of 6 inches or less is abnormal): In Functional Reach a distance of 6 inches or less is associated with markedly increased fall likelihood, and 6 to 10 inches indicates moderate risk. A 5 inch reach is therefore abnormal and substantial. Placing him in the moderate band understates the finding. Published norms for older men are well above 7 inches. Repeating the test does not change the clear result.
  93. The correct answer is D (A beat of 1 to 10 Hz with intensity giving visible rhythmic contractions): A muscle pump needs rhythmic, intermittent contractions, which a low beat of about 1 to 10 Hz at motor intensity provides. Elevation helps gravity assist the fluid return. A sensory-level 100 Hz beat gives analgesia without contraction. Tetany holds the muscle contracted and does not alternate squeeze and relaxation, and a 5000 Hz beat is not a realistic setting.
  94. The correct answer is A (Avoid heat over the irradiated skin and choose a gentler, nonthermal approach): Irradiated skin and underlying tissue tolerate heat poorly, and heat over a field treated for malignancy is avoided. A nonthermal approach treats the tightness without that risk. Insulation, short time and completion of treatment do not restore normal heat tolerance.
  95. The correct answer is A (Set up a controlled area with signs and restricted entry): Class 4 devices call for a controlled area with signs and limits on who may enter while the beam is on, in addition to eyewear. A label alone does not stop people from walking in. Consent forms do not protect staff, and pulsing changes the delivery pattern without changing the class of the device.
  96. The correct answer is D (Sustained pressure at tolerable discomfort, held until the tenderness eases): Ischemic compression applies sustained pressure at tolerable discomfort and holds it until the tenderness eases. Rapid oscillation and deep stripping are different techniques that do not hold a point. Maximal pressure to sharp pain is not tolerable and is likely to increase guarding in an anxious patient.
  97. The correct answer is B (Spasticity, a velocity dependent increase in muscle spindle mediated stretch reflex activity): Resistance that rises with the speed of passive movement is the defining feature of spasticity, an exaggerated spindle driven stretch reflex after an upper motor neuron lesion. Rigidity is velocity independent, and a fixed contracture would limit range at slow speeds as well. Tendon organ hypersensitivity would produce relaxation, not rising resistance.
  98. The correct answer is D (Pectoralis minor, stretched by retracting and posteriorly tilting the scapula while the arm is supported): A shortened pectoralis minor pulls the coracoid process forward and down, tilting the scapula anteriorly and downwardly rotating it, so the shoulder sits forward off the table. It is stretched by retraction and posterior tilt of the scapula. The serratus anterior and lower trapezius do not produce this pattern, and retraction shortens the rhomboids.
  99. The correct answer is C (At least 150 minutes per week of moderate-intensity aerobic activity): ACSM and federal guidelines set the minimum at 150 minutes per week of moderate-intensity activity. The 75 minute figure is the minimum for vigorous activity, which counts double. The 300 minute figure is the higher target associated with additional health benefit, not the minimum. A 90 minute total is not a guideline value.
  100. The correct answer is D (Stop standing extension and test another direction, such as flexion or a side-glide): Distal spread of symptoms that remains afterward is a sign the direction is unfavorable. The clinician should stop it and test flexion or a lateral component. Continuing, adding overpressure, or advancing the same direction risks aggravating nerve root irritation.
